Across ten indoor and outdoor simulation scenes, AGE-Capture achieves a mean peak signal-to-noise ratio (PSNR) $3.30$--$3.53\,\mathrm{dB}$ higher than that of the online baselines while using $58.2\%$ of the common mission-time budget on average. Its mean peak GPU memory usage is $91.0\%$--$93.3\%$ lower than that of the same baselines. Experiments with different sensor configurations validate the adaptability of the shared acquisition pipeline, while flights in nine real-world scenes further demonstrate onboard closed-loop operation under practical computational and motion constraints.

### Paper [50]
Title: Reference-Free Humanoid Skateboarding via Termination-Shaped Reinforcement Learning
URL: https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.26054
Authors: Ganeshvar Soenessardien, Michael Przystupa, Kevin Sebastian Luck
Abstract: Humanoid locomotion research has advanced rapidly on walking and running, but \emph {object-mediated} locomotion, in which a robot must coordinate its body with a passive external object, remains difficult. One challenging task is skateboarding, which requires simultaneous interaction with the ground and a passive but movable platform to achieve forward motion. Current methods for tackling skateboarding with humanoid robots either require human demonstrations or utilise analytic steering models that require system identification. In this paper, we introduce a deep reinforcement learning framework that does not rely on prior data such as demonstrations and enables robots such as the K1 humanoid to learn to skateboard using model-free learning. The method combines task rewards with exploit-blocking terminations. We introduce a number of constraints restricting behaviours such as losing the planted board foot, standing with both feet on the board, excessive twisting, separation from the board, and unstable board motion. Their changing prevalence during training produces an implicit progression from stance control towards sustained robot--board locomotion.

### Paper [51]
Title: Reference-Twist-Based Velocity-Domain Spatiotemporal Calibration for mmWave Radar, Event Camera, and IMU
URL: https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.26083
Authors: Zhenghao Zou, Yang Lyu, Xiaohu Guo, Chunhui Zhao, Quan Pan
Abstract: Multi-sensor navigation requires a common spatial and temporal basis, but geometric calibration relies on targets, features, point clouds, or pose associations that are difficult to establish across mmWave radars, event cameras, and inertial measurement units (IMUs). These sensors nevertheless share rigid-body motion. This paper formulates reference-conditioned velocity-domain spatiotemporal calibration for a rigid suite of two mmWave radars, one event camera, and two IMUs. Given a trusted reference body twist, radar Doppler, event normal flow, and inertial measurements are regressed against the same motion to estimate relative rotations, lever arms, time offsets, and sensor-specific bias and scale without artificial targets, structure from motion, or cross-modal point-cloud registration. The reference is treated as a conditioning input rather than a sensor to be calibrated. We compare joint and reference- conditioned estimation, derive conditional identifiability and CRLB-level covariance and bias bounds, and state the operating conditions under which conditioning is informative. Indoor and forest recordings with axis-separated motion evaluate initial- ization, temporal alignment, motion degeneracy, reference-input sensitivity, and residual consistency. Because the reference does not add physical observability, the estimates remain conditional on reference accuracy, synchronization, motion excitation, and projection diversity.

### Paper [52]
Title: Dynamic Human-Robot Role Allocation through Entropy-Based Monitoring and LLM Reasoning
URL: https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.26090
Authors: Elena Merlo, Marta Lagomarsino, Hamidreza Raei, Arash Ajoudani
Abstract: Role allocation strategies in human-robot collaboration typically assign tasks to both the human and the robot, limiting the user's ability to take initiative. Furthermore, allocation decisions may conflict with the user's intentions, and the rationale behind such allocations may remain unclear, potentially reducing both acceptance and collaboration fluency. The novelty of the proposed framework is that the human is left free to contribute to the task, while the robot is dynamically allocated activities through a cost-driven Behavior Tree architecture according to user-expressed preferences and behavior. Explicit preferences are extracted from natural language instructions using a large language model, whereas human activity is monitored online through a Shannon entropy-based analysis of hand-object interactions. Furthermore, the framework can recognize anomalies in human execution and autonomously trigger corrective robot actions. The approach was validated through a pilot study and a multi-subject experiment involving 14 participants. Results demonstrate the potential of such an allocation strategy for enabling intuitive and engaging collaboration without increasing the perceived workload.

### Paper [53]
Title: UniVEP: Unifying Velocity and Endpoint Prediction for Robot Manipulation
URL: https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.26103
Authors: Zhengtu Liang, Qiwei Liang, Tao Lin, Shihang Weng, Boyang Cai, Xiangyu Chen, Yikeng Chen, Jiaming Liang, Bohan Tan, Yu Yan, Renjing Xu
Abstract: Flow-matching robot policies usually use one prediction target throughout action generation, even though recovering actions from noise and polishing nearly clean actions bring different challenges. We systematically compare velocity prediction (V), endpoint prediction (X), and velocity-weighted endpoint prediction (X--V) across multiple robot policies, benchmarks, and inference budgets. On StarVLA and LiLa-WAM, X--V works best for few-step generation, while V gains more from iterative refinement. We further uncover their complementary strengths across noise levels: clean actions have concentrated covariance spectra, endpoint predictions stay more stable under high noise, and velocity predictions offer more accurate corrections near clean actions. Inspired by these findings, we introduce UniVEP, which learns both prediction targets together with consistency supervision and switches from endpoint recovery to velocity refinement as noise drops. Each step only needs one network run, and joint training also boosts single-step endpoint generation. Tests on simulated and real-world manipulation show better success rates and efficiency. On RoboTwin, UniVEP hits 61.36% success with one StarVLA run, beating the best single-target baseline at sixteen runs (59.96%). With LiLa-WAM, two runs outperform the best sixteen-step single-target baseline and cut inference latency by 77.1%.

### Paper [54]
Title: PhysVAM: Physics-oriented Future Value Action Model for Dynamic Object Manipulation
URL: https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.26105
Authors: Yuxiang Yang, Jingbo Zhang, Jiaru Zhong, Junliang Chen, Yirui Zhou, Guangming Wang, Haitao Lin, Huaiyuan Xu, Yi Wang, Lap-Pui Chau
Abstract: Predicting future scene or object states does not necessarily reveal how robot-object interactions will advance or hinder a task. This challenge is particularly pronounced in dynamic robotic manipulation, where objects continue moving during perception and execution. Effective action generation therefore requires not only modeling object dynamics but also anticipating how future interactions will shape task progress. To this end, we propose \textbf{PhysVAM}, a Physics-oriented Future Value Action Model that represents anticipated interactions through their effects on future task progress. This value-based formulation provides task-directed foresight without reconstructing future visual states. Conditioned on current observations and historical object and robot motion, PhysVAM jointly learns action generation and multi-horizon future-value prediction. The value branch learns horizon-specific latent representations through future task-value prediction from successful and failed trajectories. These future-value representations are integrated into the action policy through gated cross-attention, guiding action generation toward interactions that facilitate task success. Extensive simulation and real-world experiments demonstrate superior task success, with absolute success-rate improvements of 27.0 and 27.5 percentage points over the strongest baselines in simulation and real-world tasks, respectively. Project page: https://metarobo.github.io/physvam.github.io/.

### Paper [55]
Title: Spatially Conditioned Trajectory Diffusion
URL: https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.26127
Authors: Yefim Nudelman, Dor Atzmon, Tom Tirer
Abstract: Multi-Robot Motion Planning (MRMP) requires trajectories that avoid environmental obstacles and collisions between robots in a shared workspace. Diffusion-based planners often address obstacle avoidance through sampling-time guidance, while some conditioned approaches aggregate map geometry into a global representation. We present Spatially Conditioned Trajectory Diffusion (SCTD), a map-conditioned trajectory generator for multi-robot planning. SCTD retains multiple position-aware occupancy tokens accessed through cross-attention throughout a temporal U-Net, with hard endpoint inpainting enforcing start and goal states. One model trained on single-robot demonstrations across procedural environments generates obstacle-aware trajectories without sampling-time obstacle-cost guidance. High-level Conflict-Based Search (CBS) coordinates these trajectories through constrained replanning, retaining inter-agent guidance during sampling and reusing the model across workspace layouts and team sizes. Experiments assess single-robot success and trajectory quality, generalization to unfamiliar layouts with added obstacles, and coordinated multi-robot planning. On 15,000 held-out tasks, SCTD achieves higher success than the evaluated guided diffusion baseline, with the largest gains on hard-detour tasks, while trajectory-quality comparisons show smoother trajectories. Conditioning ablations show higher success with spatial tokens than with a global map embedding, and map-replacement tests demonstrate dependence on the supplied geometry. The resulting multi-robot system achieves higher team success than the compared diffusion baselines, with experiments covering teams of up to 15 robots.

### Paper [56]
Title: A Practical Proprioceptive Data Collection Utility for the Unitree GO1
URL: https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.26146
Authors: Jack Anders Smitterberg, Tan Chen
Abstract: The Unitree Go1 is a widely used platform for legged robotics research, yet collecting proprioceptive data from it is unnecessarily difficult: the Unitree SDK is sparsely documented, and ROS1 (Robot Operating System), the Go1's native middleware, is fully deprecated. We present an open-source graphical utility that enables researchers to reliably record proprioceptive data from the Go1, including joint states, IMU (Inertial Measurement Unit) readings, foot force sensor data, and gyroscope measurements, without requiring ROS expertise. The tool provides a simple GUI (Graphical User Interface) for starting, stopping, and labeling recordings in real time. The contribution of this paper is the collection utility. We characterize the acquisition quality of 51 recordings collected with it, including the achieved sampling rate and repeated samples. As a secondary, deliberately modest check that the data is useful, we train a terrain classifier on it. The results are weak: a Temporal Convolutional Network reaches about 38% accuracy on held-out recordings across ten terrain classes (10% would be chance), and a window-level split that ignores recording boundaries inflates the same measurement to about 99%. We report this as a demonstration that the logged signals carry terrain information, not as a competitive classifier. The utility is designed to lower the barrier to collecting Go1 proprioceptive data without ROS.

### Paper [57]
Title: One View, One Action: Spatially Generalizable VLAs through Observation-Aligned Experts and Cross Graph Consistency
URL: https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.26161
Authors: Jinxi Liu, Ruifeng Zhai, Hao Liu, Guangrun Wang
Abstract: Multi-view vision-language-action (VLA) models still face challenges in spatial generalization, particularly under changes in camera viewpoints and robot initial states. These models typically receive action supervision only in the robot base frame, requiring them to implicitly learn the spatial correspondence between observations from each view and base-frame actions. We propose One View, One Action (OVOA-VLA), which learns to predict actions in each view's camera frame, allowing the same physical motion to provide diverse, observation-aligned supervision across views. OVOA-VLA jointly trains a base-frame expert and view-specific action experts, whose robot-state inputs and action targets are expressed in their respective camera frames. Two complementary directed attention graphs model both directions of interaction between the base and view-specific experts, jointly leveraging view-specific action supervision to improve base-frame prediction. Cross Graph Consistency further encourages agreement between the two graphs' base-frame predictions for the same sample. With the same multi-view inputs, the model supports both full and base-only inference. Experiments across multiple simulation benchmarks demonstrate improved manipulation performance and robustness to camera-viewpoint and robot-initialization perturbations. Full and base-only inference achieve average success rates of 88.1% and 85.7% on LIBERO-Plus, respectively. In real-world evaluations, base-only inference raises the average success rates from $\pi_{0.5}$'s 28.3% to 58.3% across three tasks under spatial distribution shifts.

### Paper [58]
Title: Planning to Stop: Factored Certification and Terminal Repair for Noncircular Robots
URL: https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.26167
Authors: Runtian Zheng, Congpeng Zhang, Ying Liu
Abstract: Tight-space navigation requires a robot to clear obstacles and stop inside its goal despite uncertainty. We present GeoDiff, a motion-program planner coupling original-goal stopped-set inclusion, witness-guided terminal repair, and factored proof compilation. An exact feasible-translation characterization guides repair; request-local reuse reduces repeated geometric work. We establish conditions for compiler equivalence and reach-and-stop soundness. Across 11,440 floorplan-derived jobs, GeoDiff certifies 61/139 tasks at 5-cm tolerance within 15 s, versus 44/139 for the stop-set baseline. The strongest observed external certificate adapter certifies 13/139 tasks from 119/139 native routes, separating route availability from certification. On tasks derived from public BARN maps, GeoDiff certifies 202/270 at 5-cm tolerance for a Jackal-shaped body, versus 3/270 for the portal baseline. Development analyses distinguish emitter savings from solver coverage, and 1,420 route-tracking episodes characterize the execution interface. These results show how terminal reasoning and proof reuse increase finite-budget certification coverage while preserving the original physical task and goal.

### Paper [59]
Title: EmbodyAgent: Post-Training Embodied VLM Agents via Distillation, SFT, and RL
URL: https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.26196
Authors: Wei Dai, Jinkun Liu, Jiayi Chen, Han Liu, Wenbo Ding, Shaolun Huang
Abstract: Vision-language models (VLMs) can act as closed-loop robot-use agents (RUAs) by writing and executing control code through tool interfaces. However, turning an open-weight VLM into a high-performing embodied agent requires a complete post-training stack: out-of-the-box open VLMs fail completely at interactive tool contracts, strong frontier teachers are accessible only as black-box endpoints without exposed weights or token probabilities, and supervised imitation alone plateaus under rollout distribution shift. We present EmbodyAgent, an end-to-end post-training framework with three tightly coupled pipelines: (1) a black-box data synthesis pipeline that queries a strong teacher under a forced think-then-code contract and filters trajectories by task success; (2) an SFT pipeline that distills the explanation-and-program targets into an open-weight 9B VLM with both vision and language encoders trainable; and (3) an online RL pipeline that optimizes student-generated episodes via group-relative advantage with a runtime-error-penalized outcome reward. All three pipelines share one primitive-free Python execution tool exposing only raw visual observation and seven-dimensional end-effector control, with no action decoder and no interface shift across stages. Under a strictly separated evaluation protocol on ten LIBERO Spatial tasks (disjoint state splits, greedy test-time decoding, training-only safeguards), held-out success on 100 test states rises from 0/100 (base 9B VLM) to 14/100 (after SFT) and 84/100 (after RL), while training rollout success climbs from 8.4% to 87.7% and median model calls per episode drop from 36 to 11. We further identify a self-reinforcing runtime-error mode induced by outcome-based RL on executable code, and establish a failure-category monitoring and sampling-side curation protocol that stabilizes training.

### Paper [60]
Title: Teaching Vision-Language-Action Models to Stop Safely
URL: https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.26239
Authors: Zheyuan Liu, Chuhan Wang, Jinduo Guo, Yue Huang, Xiangchi Yuan, Ningshan Ma, Meng Jiang
Abstract: Vision-language-action (VLA) models enable robots to perform diverse tasks from visual observations and language instructions. While prior work has explored task suppression and refusal, stopping prohibited behavior once execution is underway and controlling the resulting physical response remain challenging. Our evaluation reveals that policies which suppress a task from its initial state can still complete it when taking over partway through execution. Even when the task remains unfinished, the robot may drop a held object, making non-completion an incomplete measure of safety. To address these challenges, we introduce State-Aware Fallback Imitation (SAFI) for tasks where holding provides an appropriate alternative response. SAFI comprises two stages, (1) fallback target construction, which assigns stationary arm commands and consistent gripper targets throughout prohibited-task demonstrations, and (2) joint policy adaptation, which learns these targets alongside the original actions of retained tasks. This supervision teaches an explicit alternative response at intermediate execution states while maintaining the robot's other skills. Extensive experiments with OpenVLA-OFT across LIBERO and BadLIBERO tasks evaluate prohibited completion, physical incidents, and retained-task performance. On the evaluated LIBERO tasks, SAFI reduces aggregate target success after takeover to 1.3\%, with no recorded physical incidents or action saturation, while preserving high retained-task success.

### Paper [61]
Title: FastRoverVLA: Fast Vision-Language-Action Execution for Off-Road Autonomous Driving
URL: https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.26242
Authors: Changtao Zhao, Yingxue Guan, Li Wang, Shijie Zhao, Yuhang Zhang, Shengkai Zhang, Qing Wang, Ziying Song
Abstract: Off-road vision-language-action (VLA) models generate scene-conditioned plans, but frequent trajectory updates can repeatedly invoke expensive multimodal inference. Reusing a prediction reduces this demand while leaving its commands exposed to changes in vehicle motion. FastRoverVLA addresses this mismatch by decoupling visual plan generation from motion-conditioned execution. Its training-free interface maintains decoded plans between visual predictions. A speed-curvature action buffer is aligned with measured motion at each update, integrated into a local trajectory, and advanced by consuming the current action. The corrected remainder is written back to the buffer, preserving motion corrections across successive updates. A bounded refresh rule normalizes speed and curvature variations within a newly decoded plan and assigns a short or long reuse budget according to the resulting plan-roughness score. The interface requires neither backbone features nor an additional learned driving policy. Over a 3 s horizon, independent real-request evaluation achieves average displacement error (ADE) and final displacement error (FDE) of 0.328/0.596 m on ORAD-3D and 0.266/0.526 m on RELLIS-3D, reducing FDE by 18.0% and 36.7% at request rates of 6.0% and 4.8%, respectively. These runs retain lower errors across four backbones and both datasets and reduce Qwen3-VL request-processing cost by factors of 16.6 and 20.8 under serial replay, while mechanism and motion-only controls identify persistent state correction as the main source of the observed accuracy benefit. The source code and evaluation materials are publicly available at https://github.com/ADEPT-ORG/FastRoverVLA.

### Paper [62]
Title: I See You: Initial Multi-Robot LiDAR Registration with Mutual-Observation Checks
URL: https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.26276
Authors: Jonathan Lichtenfeld, Oskar von Stryk
Abstract: Compared to single-robot deployments, multi-robot teams can significantly improve search-and-rescue (SAR) mission success by covering large areas in parallel. To collaborate effectively, these robots must be registered in a common reference frame. Standard registration methods typically rely on external infrastructure or significant map overlap, but real deployments require infrastructure-free, stationary initial alignment for immediate operation. While global LiDAR point cloud registration addresses this, existing single-scan methods often fail silently in aliased or degenerate environments. Approaches scaling beyond two agents also remain rarely evaluated in real-world scenarios. We propose a centralized, deployment-ready framework for pre-mission multi-robot registration. Our system performs pairwise coarse-to-fine alignment and actively rejects impossible pose hypotheses via deterministic plausibility checks. We validate the framework on a real-world custom dataset of four synchronized robots in various environments with ground truth. The checks reject more than a third of the incorrect pairwise registrations while falsely rejecting less than 1% of the correct ones, and reduce the number of team registrations with a wrong robot pose by 38%. Dataset and code are publicly available.

### Paper [63]
Title: LocoMuJoCo: A Comprehensive Imitation Learning Benchmark for Whole-Body Control
URL: https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.26293
Authors: Firas Al-Hafez, Davide Tateo, Oleg Kaidanov, Aditya Bhatt, Artur Zelik, Zhengyi Luo, Jan Peters
Abstract: LocoMuJoCo is an open-source benchmark for imitation learning (IL) and reinforcement learning (RL) on whole-body control. The library couples MuJoCo (CPU) with MJX and the MjWarp backend (GPU) under a single API, exposes 9 humanoid, 3 (musculo-)skeletal, and 4 quadruped embodiments, and ships a curated native motion-capture dataset for every humanoid, to which it can also retarget AMASS and LAFAN1. A modular plug-in architecture makes observations, rewards, control modes, terrains, and domain randomization fully interchangeable, while compact JAX implementations of many RL and IL algorithms provide JIT-compiled end-to-end training, and the companion loco-mujoco-real repository provides sim-to-real deployment. We describe the design, the SMPL-to-robot and robot-to-robot retargeting pipeline, and a usage example. LocoMuJoCo is released under MIT at https://github.com/robfiras/loco-mujoco.

### Paper [64]
Title: Exposure-Aware Informative Path Planning Using Deep Reinforcement Learning
URL: https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.26302
Authors: Muzaffar Qureshi, Yihui Mao, James W. Cross, Kyle Volle, Rushikesh Kamalapurkar
Abstract: This paper addresses the finite-budget exposure-aware informative path planning (IPP) problem for a fleet of mobile sensing agents. The agents map an unknown scalar field while limiting exposure to high field intensity. Existing IPP methods optimize informativeness under motion or resource constraints, but do not address field exposure constraints. We formulate exposure-aware IPP (EA-IPP) as a scalarized bi-objective belief-state Markov decision process that trades the normalized Gaussian-process (GP) information gain against a normalized measurement-derived fleet exposure index. A parameter-shared proximal policy optimization (PPO) framework with decentralized actors and a centralized fleet critic is trained offline and evaluated on unseen fields. Across the radar, plume, and wildfire field families, the EA-IPP agent achieves the lowest GP reconstruction RMSE among the evaluated baselines while retaining D-optimal information within $10\%$ of the non-learning information baseline. The minimum field exposure is approximately $34.6\%$, $20.1\%$, and $27\%$ below the non-exposure-aware baseline for radar, plume, and wildfire, respectively.

### Paper [65]
Title: Reasoning on Call: Efficient Agentic Robot Control for Long-Horizon Robot Manipulation
URL: https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.26341
Authors: Zilang Cen, Chao Tang, Fangcheng Liu, Weishi Mi, Wei Hao, JiaYu Xu, Dehao Huang, Yehui Tang, Xiaohui Cui, Tingguang Li, Xiaoyuan Zhang
Abstract: Large multimodal models can support long-horizon robot manipulation, but frequent model calls incur substantial latency and token costs. The central challenge is determining when agent assistance or intervention is needed and whether errors require correcting robot execution, subtask planning, or both. To address this problem, we propose RoC (Reasoning on Call), an efficient framework that coordinates a low-level policy and a high-level reasoning agent through an online task-state monitor. The monitor combines temporal visual observations with ordered interaction memory to jointly estimate progress within the active subtask and predict its successor. These predictions guide event-triggered agent review for coordinated execution repair and plan revision. Verified outcomes govern memory updates and subtask transitions, allowing routine execution to remain with the policy while reasoning focuses on decisions requiring additional assessment. On RMBench, RoC improves task success rate by 24.0 percentage points and reduces token consumption by 65.3% compared with the strongest agent-based baseline. It also outperforms the strongest policy-based baseline by 15.1 percentage points in task success rate. Real-world experiments further validate its effectiveness across three long-horizon manipulation tasks. Overall, RoC supports efficient agent intervention and coordinated recovery across execution and planning for long-horizon robot manipulation.

### Paper [66]
Title: PUPPET: Physical-Unified Platform for Presence, Embodiment, and Teleoperation
URL: https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.26350
Authors: Mahika Vats, Milan Groshev, Carlos Gomez Cubero, Eduardo Castell\'o Ferrer
Abstract: Remote access to robotics hardware does not by itself guarantee usable experimental data. A teleoperated trial can complete the task and still produce an incomplete or inconsistent record. PUPPET aims to solve this problem by presenting a cyber-physical framework that opens a physical robotics laboratory through a continuously synchronized VR replica of the robot, stranding environment, and task objects. In addition, PUPPET integrates a trial validation pipeline built into the experimental workflow. We evaluated PUPPET in a within-subject study with 30 participants, who performed the same table-to-shelf object-transfer task in person, through 2D teleoperation, and through PUPPET. VR reduced post-learning completion time by 27.7% over 2D (68.7,s vs. 95.0,s), statistically indistinguishable from in-person execution (72.1,s). It also produced more validation-ready (85.6% vs. 66.0%) and intervention-free (89.7% vs. 67.2%) trials. With PUPPET we show that modality shapes the remote task performance and the reusability of the resulting experimental record.

### Paper [67]
Title: Alignment Drives Reuse: Learning Generalizable Robot-Use Agents from Heterogeneous Experience
URL: https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.26376
Authors: Ziyue Wang, Yaopeng Yang, Liyi Zhou
Abstract: Vision-language models (VLMs) demonstrate broad generalization across tasks and domains. However, comparable generalization in robot manipulation remains challenging. Heterogeneity in robot embodiments, task formulations, geometric conventions, and action interfaces fragments interaction experience across benchmarks and hardware. We rethink robot skill learning through alignment: Can heterogeneous interactions across benchmarks and embodiments jointly improve one shared skillset for robot manipulation? We propose UniCraft, a framework for learning robot manipulation skills by aligning heterogeneous settings. Specifically, UniCraft represents observed scene geometry using consistent coordinate conventions and aligns sensing and control interfaces across robots. It organizes tasks from different benchmarks in a canonical format, allowing interaction feedback to jointly refine shared skills and the orchestration policy that guides their selection and composition, without any demonstrations or model weight updates. We interleave tasks from 3 benchmarks and 6 embodiments, collecting 2,700 learning rollouts per agent model. During training, UniCraft improves pooled task success on unseen scenarios and tasks by 9-28 percentage points on LIBERO-PRO, 10.8-13.3 on RoboTwin 2.0, and 3.1-14.4 on RoboCasa365 (Epochs 1-3). After training, with the learned skills frozen, UniCraft demonstrates zero-shot transfer across 4 new robot embodiments and 5 additional agent models on robosuite and RoboDojo.

### Paper [68]
Title: Beyond the Sink Interpretation: Radial Attention in Flow-Based VLA Policies
URL: https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.26385
Authors: Yuxi Zheng, Yueqian Lin, Yudong Liu, Zijia Tang, Junyu Pan, Yiheng Wang, Hai Helen Li, Yiran Chen
Abstract: Prior work calls the high attention on the first and last action tokens of a flow-based VLA policy a radial action sink, and uses these tokens to set the execution horizon. In language models, a sink is a token with high incoming attention, a small value, and little effect on the output. We test whether that profile holds, not the horizon rule, in one $\pi_{0.5}$ checkpoint on five RoboCasa tasks. The two endpoints receive 5.4% of action-key mass, 1.4 percentage points above their uniform share, and rotary positional encoding produces this excess: directly in shallow layers and through upstream action-token RoPE deeper in the network. The endpoints do not fit the sink profile. Their value norms stay near the action-token mean in all 18 layers. On 250 paired episodes, masking them or zeroing their values changes success by less than 4 points, and replacing their value directions with those of another observation lowers it by 3.2 to 7.2 points across three donors, whereas the same replacement on two non-endpoint tokens in the executed prefix lowers it by 28.4 points. The token that does fit the sink profile on mass and value norm is the prefix BOS; on this checkpoint, zeroing its value raises success in two paired runs, by +13.2 points (95% CI [+7.0, +19.4]) and +6.0 points ([+0.2, +11.8]). Inside the action chunk, a relative-offset routing profile estimated on other tasks keeps 75% of action-token spread and keeps closed-loop success on three held-out tasks (42.7% against a 38.7% baseline), while uniform or shuffled routing drops success to 3-4%. High attention mass alone does not tell what a token does.

### Paper [69]
Title: SpecFlow: Flow Matching in the Acceleration Spectrum for Multi-Agent Trajectory Forecasting
URL: https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.26390
Authors: Ziyang Xu, Peng Han, Hai Zhou
Abstract: Multimodal trajectory forecasting must cover diverse future behaviors without sacrificing dynamical coherence. Most generative forecasters evolve frame-wise positions directly, leaving the relationships among position, velocity, and acceleration implicit. We present SpecFlow, a conditional flow-matching framework that treats the Discrete Cosine Transform (DCT) coefficients of future accelerations as its generative state. SpecFlow learns a transport from Gaussian noise to acceleration spectra and reconstructs velocity and position through fixed analytical integration initialized by the last observed state. A joint phase-space objective supervises all three kinematic levels. To represent destination uncertainty without future information, an endpoint predictor produces candidate destinations and confidence scores solely from observed context, and the highest-confidence candidate conditions the spectral denoiser. Alternating attention over trajectory hypotheses and agents captures multimodality and interaction. Under best-of-20 evaluation, SpecFlow achieves average minADE/minFDE of 0.15/0.22 on ETH/UCY, 0.69/0.86 at four seconds on NBA, and 7.38/11.57 on SDD. It establishes state-of-the-art results across all five ETH/UCY scenes and achieves the lowest four-second ADE on NBA with a tied-best FDE, while remaining competitive on SDD. Together, these results demonstrate that acceleration-spectrum flow matching provides an effective structured generative state across smooth pedestrian motion, coordinated multi-agent sports dynamics, and heterogeneous outdoor trajectories. Code will be made publicly available.

### Paper [70]
Title: TacInno: Teaching Vision-Language-Action Models the Contact Dynamics Beyond Vision and Action
URL: https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.26405
Authors: Weile Li, Yunfan Lou, Muqing Shi, Zihao Zhang, Zhenbiao Huang, Boxian Yao, Xiyu Zhu, Zhibo Pang
Abstract: Vision-Language-Action (VLA) models have recently been equipped with tactile sensing to perceive visually subtle contact events in contact-rich manipulation. However, adding tactile input yields inconsistent gains, and some visuo-tactile VLA models rely little on tactile input, because much of the tactile change, such as the gel deformation during gripper closure, is predictable from visual observations and executed actions. To address this problem, we introduce TacInno, which isolates the contact dynamics that vision and executed actions cannot explain, termed tactile innovation, and uses tactile innovation only as a training target. Specifically, the TacInno tokenizer, conditioned on visual observations and executed actions, compresses the future tactile change into a discrete code, and the policy, TacInno-VLA, learns to predict the code, adding no module for the code at deployment. TacInno-VLA achieves average success rates of 87.5% across six simulation tasks and 70.5% across four real-world tasks, outperforming the strongest visuo-tactile baseline by 10.7 and 9.0 percentage points, respectively. Ablation studies validate the contributions of visual and action conditioning, multi-frame encoding, and auxiliary code prediction. Together, these results demonstrate the effectiveness of tactile innovation as a supervision signal for contact-rich manipulation.

### Paper [71]
Title: Awaken-HI: Scaling Humanoid Intelligence with Whole-Body Human Experience
URL: https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.26420
Authors: EngineAI
Abstract: A central goal of humanoid robotics is for robots to perform the everyday work people do in homes and workplaces. Existing egocentric video datasets cover many of these activities but provide little synchronized whole-body motion data for learning loco-manipulation at scale. We build Awaken-Human, which pairs approximately 50,000 hours of egocentric stereo video with synchronized whole-body human motion and covers more than 100 skills. Building on this, we also introduce Awaken-HI (Humanoid Intelligence), a vision-language-action model for humanoid loco-manipulation that jointly predicts original human motion and retargeted robot actions through a shared action expert. We show that loco-manipulation skills transfer more effectively from people to humanoids when human trajectories directly supervise the shared action expert. Increasing human pretraining data reduces held-out robot action prediction error according to an empirical scaling law and raises the mean success rate on downstream real-robot tasks. After full-corpus pretraining and T800 adaptation, Awaken-HI achieves a mean success rate of 93.75% across eight individual tasks and outperforms GR00T N1.7 and $\pi_{0.5}$ in both individual and long-horizon evaluations. The same policy generalizes to objects unseen during robot post-training and to new pairings of familiar objects and destinations.

### Paper [72]
Title: Mind2Act World: A Diagnostic Benchmark for Embodied Mind, Physical Action, and Mind-Action Composition in Robotic Manipulation
URL: https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.26444
Authors: Yuzhi Huang, Jie Wu, Fanding Huang, Junchao Lin, Weichen Zhang, Ziyi Xiong, Yang Li, Zhengwei Xie, Zhimin Wang, Zhen Liu, Jiazhen Huang, Yuzheng Zhang, Yuche Liu, Hongyi Yang, Chen Tang, Shixiang Tang, Hakan Aktas, Adam Wu, Duo Wu, Kangye Ji, Shuzhao Xie, Huanan Liu, Weijue Bu, Chenxin Li, Xinyuan Wang, Tianyi Bai, Yinan Mao, Jiawei Wang, Minglei Li, Heng Yu, Ehsan Adeli, Minchen Li, Yong-Lu Li, Cengiz \"Oztireli, Huazhe Xu, Chao Yu, Jing Zhang, Shanghang Zhang, Weitao Zhou, Wenwu Zhu, Cheng Chi, Jingyan Jiang, Zhi Wang
Abstract: Embodied intelligence aims to let robots perform complex physical tasks with human-like adaptability and reliability, which requires three complementary capabilities: history-based cognitive reasoning, precise and timely physical execution, and the composition of reasoning and execution through environmental feedback. Yet existing benchmarks either evaluate reasoning and execution within the same tasks or assess them separately without their composition, so they reveal little about whether a failure comes from reasoning, execution, or their composition. We introduce Mind2Act World, a diagnostic benchmark of 30 simulated manipulation tasks from five real-world application domains. Each task has three difficulty levels and two scene settings (Clean and Random), yielding 180 configurations. The tasks form three views: Mind tests decisions based on past observations, Act tests precise and timely execution, and Mind2Act combines both in closed-loop tasks where action outcomes guide later decisions. Comparing the three views shows whether a system lacks a capability or fails to compose capabilities it already has. A diagnosis harness further attributes the failed episodes of three representative systems to Mind, Act, or their composition and summarizes each system in a profile card. Our evaluation of multimodal agents, vision-language-action models (VLAs), and world-action models (WAMs) shows that competence in a single view does not carry over to Mind2Act tasks. Even the strongest agent succeeds in 52.7% of Mind and 39.0% of Act episodes but in only 12.0% of Mind2Act episodes. The strongest agent retains task state but struggles with sustained contact, whereas end-to-end policies execute practiced motions but retain little task state. These findings point to composing reasoning and execution through environmental feedback as the main open problem.

### Paper [73]
Title: An Open-Source ROS 2 Framework for Shared Control in Surgical Teleoperation: Integrating the Haply Inverse3 with the da Vinci Research Kit
URL: https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.26485
Authors: Balint Varga, Sebastian Fritz, Gergely Kiss, Kristof Takacs, Peter Galambos
Abstract: Surgical teleoperation deprives surgeons of touch, forcing visual inference of tissue forces; the commercial platforms now restoring force feedback remain closed, preventing open research on haptic shared control. This paper provides a system contribution: it presents an open-source, ROS 2-native framework that integrates the compact, three-degree-of-freedom (3-DOF) Haply Inverse3 haptic device with the da~Vinci Research Kit (dVRK). The framework includes a novel mapping strategy that combines 3-DOF translational device input with separately tracked VerseGrip orientation into 6-DOF patient-side manipulator commands. Two virtual-fixture-inspired guidance modes serve as reference implementations to evaluate the framework in a within-subjects user study on a teleoperated hot-wire benchmark task in simulation, and the architecture is further demonstrated on physical dVRK hardware. Relative to unassisted manual control, linear guidance significantly reduced both the number of wire contacts and the task completion time, whereas the progressive-stiffness guidance produced consistent but statistically non-significant trends in the same direction. Subjective ratings of workload, agency, and satisfaction showed favorable descriptive trends for the guidance modes. The results demonstrate the feasibility of haptic shared control with a compact device coupled to the dVRK and provide an openly available, reproducible platform for future research. They further suggest that simpler linear feedback may be more robust for inexperienced operators than more complex profiles.

### Paper [74]
Title: JARVIS: An Agent That Designs, Builds, and Tests Circuit Boards on Its Own
URL: https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.26503
Authors: Shibo Zhou, Tianqi Hong, Zheng Yan, He Yang
Abstract: Power electronics is central to the energy transition, but developing new hardware still relies on human-led design, fabrication, testing, and iteration. We present JARVIS, an embodied AI agent that autonomously closes this loop for circuit boards. JARVIS maintains machine-specific capabilities and constraints, scans its workspace using a robot-mounted 3D scanner, and constructs an executable digital twin in which it rehearses robot motions, fabrication steps, and circuit behavior before consuming materials. A single text instruction initiates the physical experiment. Using unmodified desktop equipment, the agent coordinates a robot arm, a PCB printer that deposits conductive silver ink and solder paste, and a pick-and-place machine. It verifies progress using cameras and sensors, then tests the fabricated circuit with an OPAL-RT real-time simulator and an oscilloscope. Based on the measurements, JARVIS diagnoses deviations from the design and generates a revised design in KiCad for subsequent rehearsal. In a demonstration involving a 60 Hz notch filter for grid-connected converter sensing, JARVIS completed a first-run experiment in 2 h 39 min without human intervention, achieving 50.6 dB attenuation at 60 Hz. Its digital-twin rehearsal identified five issues before fabrication, including a test criterion that would have incorrectly rejected a functioning board. During execution, the agent also resolved encountered faults autonomously. These results demonstrate an integrated agent-driven workflow for physical design, fabrication, measurement, and iterative improvement of power-electronic circuitry. To our knowledge, JARVIS is the first system in power electronics in which an agent closes the physical design-build-test-learn loop for circuit boards.

### Paper [75]
Title: ACE-Brain-0.7: Learning Execution-Grounded Reasoning for Embodied Agents
URL: https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.26519
Authors: Yinhe Wang, Shijun Shi, Feng Pan, Tao Tao, Wei Mao, Jiangning Zhang, Zhiyao Ren, Yihang Jiao, Haoming Gu, Yunpeng Qing, Jiyu Guo, Zhi Hou, Dacheng Tao, Xiaogang Wang
Abstract: Reliable reasoning for long-horizon embodied tasks requires predictions grounded in the observed execution state. We present ACE-Brain-0.7, a 9B vision--language model that combines spatial grounding, task understanding, and progress estimation. To train the model, we curate 3.7 million question--answer pairs, drawing execution supervision from egocentric human videos, robot episodes, and simulation. These questions span different stages of an execution and connect task-level judgments with observed progress and outcomes. We then fine-tune Qwen3.5-9B on this collection together with additional task, spatial-grounding, and progress-estimation data. Across six public task-reasoning benchmarks, ACE-Brain-0.7 achieves an average score of 76.5, ahead of GPT-6 Astra at 72.7 and Claude Opus 5.5 at 73.5. Our model also leads the evaluated models in spatial grounding, averaging 75.90 across seven benchmarks and reaching 91.25 Acc@0.5 on RefCOCOg. Controlled evaluations further indicate that our task-only model makes more effective use of visual history and more accurately distinguishes execution states. Together, these results show that ACE-Brain-0.7 effectively combines spatial perception with temporal understanding, delivering strong execution-grounded reasoning and comparing favorably with closed-source models.

### Paper [76]
Title: PhyDEX: A Benchmark for Physical Reasoning in Vision-Language-Action Models via Dynamic Extrinsic Dexterity
URL: https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.26565
Authors: Hao Vo, Taisei Hanyu, Yuki Ikebe, Pham Tri Quang, Sreevenkata Anjani Tishita Godavarthi, Nghi D. Q. Bui, Daniel Sonntag, Chase Rainwater, Duy Minh Ho Nguyen, Anh Nguyen, Ngan Le
Abstract: Vision-Language-Action (VLA) models have advanced rapidly in language-conditioned robotic manipulation, yet existing benchmarks mainly evaluate gripper--object contact and largely ignore how objects interact with the environment through gravity, friction, inertia, and mass distribution. As a result, it remains unclear whether current policies truly understand physics or merely interpolate from their training data. We address this gap through the lens of dynamic extrinsic dexterity and introduce PhyDEX, a throwing benchmark in which a robot must infer an object's mass, center of mass, and friction to send it to a destination beyond its kinematic workspace. PhyDEX contains 35K trajectories (143 hours) across nine tasks and 100 scenes, covering uniform, irregular, and dynamic-mass objects with static, multiple, and moving destinations. All data are generated by a scalable, fully automatic pipeline that combines grasp generation, motion planning, and closed-loop throw optimization under randomized physical properties. We evaluate three representative VLAs, GR00T N1.7, DynamicVLA, and ForceVLA, and find that all achieve success rates below 3\%. Our analysis reveals two key bottlenecks: limited robustness of pretrained models, which causes most failures before the throw, and physical blindness, as providing ground-truth physical properties roughly doubles success. Experiments on a humanoid robot and in the real world show the same failure patterns. We hope PhyDEX will drive the development of physically grounded robot policies.

### Paper [77]
Title: PGX: Physically Grounded Execution for Long-Horizon Humanoid Loco-Manipulation
URL: https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.26596
Authors: Kai Lu, Chenyang Ma, Ioannis Havoutis
Abstract: Robotic vision-language-action (VLA) models can execute diverse individual manipulation skills, yet they still struggle with long-horizon loco-manipulation tasks, where temporal structure, skills, and precision are tightly coupled within a monolithic model. We propose Physically Grounded Execution (PGX), a framework that offloads temporal and precision control to two classical training-free components, allowing the VLA to focus on stage-specific skill dynamics. PGX decomposes long-horizon loco-manipulation into three layers: (i) a predicate-based state machine governs task progression, using physical predicates to gate phase transitions, trigger recovery, and coordinate navigation and waiting. Shared predicate semantics connect offline demonstration segmentation, online execution, and stage-wise evaluation; (ii) VLA skills operate within active manipulation phases, with flat or stage-trained models concentrating on task-relevant actions; and (iii) world-coordinate locking (WCL) maintains spatial consistency through base station keeping, object-relative positioning, and world-anchored contact targets. We demonstrate the effectiveness of PGX with extensive experiments conducted on three simulated tasks and one real-world task using a Unitree G1 humanoid robot for long-horizon loco-manipulation, showing improvements in stage progression and task success rates. Project page is available at: https://kl-research.github.io/pgx

### Paper [78]
Title: A Tutorial on Complementarity-Based Contact Models for Robotics
URL: https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.26600
Authors: Onur Beker, A. Ren\'e Geist, Michael Posa, Marc Toussaint, Justin Carpentier, Georg Martius
Abstract: Simulating rigid-body dynamics with contact is fundamental to robotics, from motion synthesis and control to mechanism design. Many widely used simulators rely on cone complementarity formulations, whose physical assumptions and numerical solution require substantial background. While excellent surveys, monographs, and course notes exist, they mostly address computational mechanics or computer graphics or document a single simulator. To the best of our knowledge, a self-contained pedagogical treatment for robotics, bringing these formulations and their solvers together with complete derivations and a unified notation, does not yet exist. This tutorial addresses this gap by connecting rigid-body dynamics, conic optimization, and frictional contact. In particular, we explain how complementarity conditions encode non-penetration and frictional dissipation, determining separation, sticking, and sliding without prescribed contact modes. We distinguish equivalent transcriptions from convex relaxations, showing how the latter can introduce artifacts such as lift-off and creep. We examine the existence and uniqueness of motion and contact forces, including the solver's role in selecting among multiple solutions. We then compare first-order and Newton-based methods, explain regularization and proximal strategies, and discuss trade-offs between computational cost, robustness, and physical fidelity. Connections to widely used simulators, optimal control, and differentiable simulation help readers interpret simulation results, choose suitable formulations and solvers, and develop their own contact dynamics routines.

### Paper [79]
Title: AgentRoboCritic: Understanding the Capability of Frontier Agents on Direct Robot Control
URL: https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.26613
Authors: Yiyang Du, Yuxuan Kuang, Qitao Zhao, Yixuan Wang, Zhanqiu Guo, Yi Xu, Xin Li, Wenbin He, Liu Ren, Chenyan Xiong
Abstract: Frontier multimodal language agents such as GPT-6 Astra have demonstrated strong capabilities in controlling robots directly via low-level numerical actions without high-level primitives. However, existing benchmarks report only episode-level success rates, which show neither how these agents succeed nor where they fail. In this paper, we present a systematic action-level analysis of frontier agents on direct robot control across 12 manipulation benchmarks. We introduce AgentRoboCritic, an intent-grounded critique framework in which the policy agent states a local intent alongside each action, and a critic evaluates the action's outcome and failure mode against this intent. Our action-level analysis demonstrates the promising capabilities of frontier agents on direct robot control, including closed-loop recovery from local execution failures, reliable semantic grounding across diverse scenes, and strong zero-shot manipulation without task-specific adaptation. It also identifies their current limitations, including task-relevant physical execution bottlenecks such as grasping and alignment, and uneven recovery across failure modes. Finally, we validate our critic on a 400-action human-annotated benchmark and release a dataset of 12,844 annotated actions alongside a fine-tuned open-source critic, Muse-Glimmer-30B, whose failure-mode accuracy rises from 32.58% to 74.24%, enabling low-cost action-level analysis.

### Paper [80]
Title: Infer3D: Inverse Generative Modeling Enables Robust 3D Reconstruction
URL: https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.25061
Authors: Amani Kiruga, Mark Hamilton, Joshua B. Tenenbaum, William T. Freeman, Vincent Sitzmann, Yilun Du, Ayush Tewari
Abstract: Humans can understand the 3D structure of scenes even from highly unfamiliar or extreme viewpoints. Cognitive science suggests this generalization stems from a top-down generative inference strategy. In contrast, state-of-the-art 3D reconstruction neural networks typically rely on amortized, feedforward inference. While fast, this pattern-matching approach degrades when faced with out-of-distribution (OOD) scene conditions unseen during training. Inspired by the generative nature of human vision, we propose Infer3D, an inverse generative modeling framework that recovers the latent variables of a generative model to best match the monocular input. To prevent degenerate flat outputs, we couple the generative image prior with a frozen 3D lifting network that constrains the optimization space to a valid, multi-view-consistent 3D manifold. Across a variety of controlled synthetic and real-world tasks, Infer3D demonstrates superior OOD reconstruction accuracy compared to feedforward networks trained on equal or larger datasets. Furthermore, mirroring how human perception requires more time to process confusing stimuli, we introduce an adaptive inference strategy that expends more test-time compute on ambiguous OOD examples while efficiently routing in-distribution inputs. Project website: https://amanikiruga.github.io/infer3d-site/

### Paper [81]
Title: Phantom Success, Silent Failure: Auditing the Success Checkers of Simulated Manipulation Benchmarks
URL: https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.25179
Authors: Zirui Song, Jiadong Zhang, Zheng Lu, Xiaosong Ma, Xiaojun Chang, Xiuying Chen
Abstract: Simulated manipulation benchmarks measure policy performance using hand-written success checkers that label each episode as a success or failure. Passing an expert demonstration shows that a checker accepts one way of completing the task. Learned policies, however, may stop after partial progress or complete the task in a different way. A checker may then accept an incomplete execution (phantom success) or reject an execution that satisfies the instruction (silent failure). In this paper, we introduce SimAudit, an LLM-based pipeline that audits success checkers against task instructions. SimAudit analyzes the instruction and checker code to identify scenarios where the two may disagree, recreates those scenarios in simulation, and runs the original checker. Multi-view renderings are then used to verify whether the observed behavior satisfies the instruction. Across RoboTwin 2.0, RoboCasa365, and RoboDojo, 183 simulated test cases produced the predicted checker verdict. Visual verification and deduplication yielded 61 distinct defects across 49 tasks, including 40 phantom successes and 21 silent failures. Most defects in RoboCasa365 accepted incomplete executions, most in RoboDojo rejected valid executions, and both error types were common in RoboTwin 2.0. These findings suggest that validating success checkers requires testing both incomplete executions and valid alternatives to expert demonstrations, and motivate more rigorous evaluation protocols for future benchmarks.

### Paper [82]
Title: Crisis Governance at the Joints. Mapping the Ethical and Societal Territory of Crisis Management Technologies: The Case of Human-Swarm Teaming
URL: https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.25202
Authors: Anthony Longo
Abstract: Crisis and disaster governance increasingly relies on technologies that sense, classify and act faster than humans can. Drone swarms coordinated with human operators, known as human-swarm teaming, provide a productive case to review the field of crisis governance. They promise better situational awareness in disaster response, but they also raise questions about human control, responsibility, surveillance, triage, dual use and public legitimacy. This report maps the ethical and societal territory that HST opens up. It unpacks this territory with a 3i-framework, the Interface, where operators and swarms work together, the Infrastructure, where organisations distribute tasks, decisions and blame, and the Imaginary, where law, policy and shared societal assumptions define what a crisis is and which interventions it justifies. The central claim is that these concerns cannot be addressed one level at a time. Many of the most pressing problems, such as responsibility gaps and the erosion of meaningful human control, arise at the joints between levels, through four translations, habits harden, routines become stories, principles become procedures, and procedures become prompts. Drawing on a critical review of literature across philosophy of technology, political theory, moral psychology, philosophy of mind, AI ethics, and critical disaster and security studies, the report develops this framework, illustrates it with two worked examples, and derives design and governance implications and a set of questions for developers, emergency organisations and policymakers.

### Paper [83]
Title: Improving Imitation Policies for Multi-Phase Tasks through Residual Learning Allocation
URL: https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.25320
Authors: Clinton Enwerem, Saleh Kemal, John S. Baras, Calin Belta
Abstract: Residual reinforcement learning corrects an imitation-trained base policy using a limited budget of environment interaction. In a task with several phases, this budget must be divided among the phases, and the phase in which the base policy fails is not necessarily the phase in which a correction most improves task completion. We formalize this distinction through the completion gain: the change in full-task completion probability when, during a single phase, a state-feedback controller replaces selected components of the base policy's action. We decompose the completion gain into the probability of reaching the phase and the conditional improvement in completion from the start of the phase, and we estimate it from paired episodes. The positive estimates determine which action components the residual policy corrects and how training episodes are distributed across phases. In stochastic point-mass docking experiments, most failures occurred in the final docking phase, whereas the largest completion gain occurred in the preceding channel phase; gain-based allocation improved completion over allocation proportional to failure counts and, in a variant with a longer approach, over uniform allocation at half of the budget. In simulated bimanual robotic assembly, the completion-gain estimates were positive only for insertion and release, although few failures occurred during release; in a second assembly configuration, a residual policy trained in the release phase improved completion from held-out states at the start of release by 16.9%.

### Paper [84]
Title: MIR-Depth: Multi-Range Refinement for Single-View RGB-D Depth Completion of Transparent Objects
URL: https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.25417
Authors: Xinzhe Fu, Qi Shao, Yuanfei Xie, Taoyu Wu, Zhihao Cao, Long Hu, Hong Liang, Xianzhi Li, Anh Nguyen, Baoru Huang, Rongsheng Li
Abstract: Recovering accurate visible-surface depth from a single RGB-D image and an object mask is essential for robotic grasping and manipulation of transparent objects. Specular reflections cause missing depth, while refraction corrupts nonzero returns with background depth, making raw measurements unreliable as surface anchors. Under a fixed sampling budget, refining an initial single-view prediction within a single search interval either misses large refraction offsets or sacrifices fine local resolution. To resolve this single-view trade-off, MIR-Depth constructs Coordinate-Aware Multi-Range Hypotheses: a 3D-aggregated global inverse-depth volume and depth-axis-filtered pixel-wise local volumes around a prior-guided initial depth. Selective Recurrent Refinement then introduces out-of-bounds lookup masking and validity-masked softmax fusion to iteratively update inverse depth from these volumes using a multi-scale ConvGRU. Finally, Spatially Gated Correction applies a learned per-pixel spatial gate to the full-resolution residual, preserving accurate initial geometry without imposing raw sensor readings as hard constraints. Experiments on TransCG, TODD, TRansPose, ClearPose, and HouseCat6D show that MIR-Depth achieves lower error than ReMake, DFNet, BP-Net, and PSD. Real-robot evaluations confirm accurate surface geometry for 6-DoF grasp pose prediction. Code and pretrained models will be publicly available.

### Paper [85]
Title: TuringPAI: A Foundation for On-Device Physical AI
URL: https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.25474
Authors: Qiman Wu, Hanlin Chen, Yuhua Wei, Mingyuan Wang, Shaman Tang, Minghao Li, Rui Xin, Xingye Liu, Zhichao Yang, Yiqi Yu, Hongyan Pei, Xiaowen Tian, YingYing Liu, Shiwen Liu, Jiahui Hu, Yizhao Wang, Yuheng Zhang, Anhua Liu, Yue He, Shuang Su, Da Zhu, Hua Zhou, Pengfei Diao, Haojie Yang, Weichao Huang, Hang Zhang, Honggou Yang, Xianming Liu
Abstract: Physical AI requires models that understand spatial relationships and dynamic environments and translate observations into actions under limited onboard compute. We present TuringPAI, a family of models for on-device Physical AI comprising TuringPAI-VL for shared vision-language understanding, TuringPAI-Drive for autonomous driving, and TuringPAI-Embodied for robotic manipulation. TuringPAI-VL is a 0.65B model built on HybridViT-MLQ, which combines convolutional local aggregation with Transformer-based global modeling. Multi-scale Latent Queries integrate spatial features and observation history within the same visual Transformer, providing a compact, structured interface to the language model. Training combines general vision-language data with geometric, cross-view, and temporal supervision. TuringPAI-VL supports general multimodal understanding and improves quantitative geometric perception over Qwen2-VL-2B, while providing a shared initialization for the two downstream VLA models. For autonomous driving, TuringPAI-Drive maintains all evaluated ADE/FDE metrics within 1.2\% of a full-token HybridViT baseline using the MLQ interface and achieves comparable aggregate scores in an in-house simulation evaluation. Using XPeng Native Runtime (XNRT) on a single NVIDIA Orin-X SoC, TuringPAI-Drive generates trajectories at 12\,Hz in on-vehicle operation. For robotic manipulation, TuringPAI-Embodied achieves a 96.8\% average success rate across the four LIBERO suites. These results demonstrate a shared vision-language foundation for driving and robotic manipulation under practical on-device constraints.

### Paper [86]
Title: Mesh-Free Keypoint Learning for Category-Level 6D Object Pose Estimation
URL: https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.25513
Authors: Junghoon Seo, Hakjin Lee, Jaehoon Sim
Abstract: Category-level 6D pose estimation relates partial RGB-D observations of unseen instances to a canonical frame shared across a category. Object-aware reconstruction helps adaptive keypoints retain the geometry of the visible surface. Common reconstruction targets select observed points by their distance to a registered instance surface. We present MORSEPose, which derives this support directly from standard pose and metric-size annotations. We map observed 3D points to normalized object coordinates and retain those inside a tolerancedilated pose-and-size envelope. This construction is consistent across camera frames and metric scales. When the annotated extent bounds the object surface, the envelope conservatively contains the support of a conventional surface-distance selector. We integrate this target with multi-level RGB-D features, adaptive keypoints, and learnable category context. Experiments on REAL275 and HouseCat6D show that the envelope closely matches surface-based supervision at both point and task levels, remains stable under boundary perturbations and viewpoint changes, and supports competitive category-level pose accuracy across benchmarks.

### Paper [87]
Title: FlashX: Scaling Robotic Experience Reuse for Embodied Agents with CBA-Based NAND Flash
URL: https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.25580
Authors: Yuseon Choi, Sanghyeon Park, Eunjong Lee, Hanseo Lee, Sangjin Kim
Abstract: Embodied agents are emerging as a promising paradigm for complex robotic tasks, leveraging Vision-Language-Action (VLA) and World Action Models (WAMs) to generate actions through Diffusion Transformer (DiT)-based iterative denoising. However, existing models often struggle to generalize to out-of-distribution (OOD) environments. We observe that retrieving successful experiences and using them to warm-start denoising enables agents to better adapt to unfamiliar situations while reducing computational overhead. This creates a new opportunity to improve task accuracy and inference efficiency simultaneously, in contrast to conventional acceleration techniques that trade accuracy for computation. However, realizing this opportunity requires scaling experience banks, introducing substantial storage and search overhead that challenges the capacity, bandwidth, and power constraints of edge systems.
  We propose FlashX, a CMOS Directly Bonded to Array (CBA)-based NAND flash architecture for scalable experience reuse in embodied agents. FlashX exploits CBA's high internal bandwidth for parallel pSLC key search, employing interleaved Compute-in-Flash (CIF) engines to sustain sensing throughput while retaining dense TLC latent storage. Proprioception-aware clustering further reduces search overhead and enables incremental experience acquisition without global reclustering. Evaluations across VLA and WAM workloads demonstrate up to 12.24\%p and 7.43\%p improvements in task success under dense and compute-efficient configurations, respectively. FlashX achieves 2.57$\times$ higher search throughput than the strongest prior NAND search architecture under pSLC sensing, with a further 9.7$\times$ search reduction through clustering. This delivers 1.29--3.95$\times$ end-to-end speedup and 1.12--2.34$\times$ energy efficiency improvement over SSD-based retrieval.

### Paper [88]
Title: LoopedWAM: Looped World Action Models for Elastic Inference
URL: https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.25595
Authors: Anh Dao, Xinxin Liu, Hang Yu, Jeffrey A Chan Santiago, Yuzhang Shang
Abstract: World action models (WAMs) couple generative video modeling with robotic action prediction but typically require large video and action transformers, limiting deployment efficiency. We present LoopedWAM, a compact WAM that recurrently applies a shared six-block core in each expert, executing 30 transformer layers while storing only 12 blocks per expert. To account for their asymmetric inference costs, the video and action experts can operate at different depths: visual features are computed once per query, whereas action computation repeats at every sampling step. We further propose elastic self-distillation, which trains a single elastic checkpoint, LoopedWAM-E, across action-depth and sampling-step budgets. With only 0.58B policy parameters and no robot pretraining, LoopedWAM achieves 96.1% success on LIBERO, 91.9% on RoboTwin 2.0, and 52.2% on RoboCasaGR1, while using $7.4\times$ less peak GPU memory than Fast-WAM under matched inference measurements. On LIBERO, LoopedWAM-E achieves 96.0% success in 27.6 ms or 95.2% in 24.7 ms on an H100, providing multiple accuracy-latency operating points from a single checkpoint; with two sampling steps and four action loops, it stays within 0.5 points of LoopedWAM on all three benchmarks. Ablations demonstrate that weight-shared depth preserves competitive performance, while aggressive action-depth reduction requires explicit budget-aware training. On a real AgileX PiPER arm, LoopedWAM succeeds in 71.3% of 80 trials across four tasks.

### Paper [89]
Title: Fast and Accurate Body Estimation in Pixels, World Space and Scenes
URL: https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.25801
Authors: Jinkun Cao
Abstract: We present FAB (Fast and Accurate Body estimation), a human body estimator that jointly improves inference efficiency and body-recovery fidelity. Rather than predicting only parametric mesh-model parameters, FAB directly predicts human skeleton joints and dense surface points. In images, it predicts dense 2D and 3D body points alongside a parametric Momentum Human Rig (MHR) head, then fits SMPL in one learned pass followed by two Gauss-Newton steps. Its ViT-L student, FAB-L, distilled from a fine-tuned SAM 3D Body, runs in real time for one person on an RTX 4070 Ti SUPER, from detection to a fitted body, at 4.6x the speed of SAM 3D Body's eager-PyTorch body-only path, while improving accuracy on most camera-space benchmarks. The same network predicts heel, toe, hand, and knee contacts, which connect the body to the scene. FAB also estimates world-space motion, fuses static-scene depth around the visible person, and aligns body and scene using predicted contacts. For human-to-humanoid real-to-sim transfer, an in-model G1 head and FAB-Retarget, a motion-level retargeter that reads dense points or the fitted mesh, map motion to a Unitree G1. FAB-Retarget produces references that follow the person more closely than specialized retargeters including SOMA, GMR, UMR, and OmniRetarget, and substantially more closely than Fast SAM 3D Body retargeted with our sequence IK. Project page: https://www.jinkuncao.com/FAB.

### Paper [90]
Title: Controlling the Humanoid Robot H1-2 With a Simulated Biologically Plausible Network
URL: https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.26074
Authors: Fatima Atwi (LAAS-GEPETTO, Nantes Univ - ECN), Constant Roux (LAAS-GEPETTO), Christian Bergaud (LAAS-MEMS), Olivier Stasse (LAAS-GEPETTO)
Abstract: Artificial Spiking Neural Networks have gained remarkable attention in the last few years due to their proven ability in simulating biological neural cultures and producing similar activity and trends. Nevertheless, whether they can be trained for humanoid locomotion while retaining biological plausibility and robust performance at once remains an open question. We study this problem by proposing a biologically inspired spiking network, trained by different learning schemes and tested on the H1-2 Unitree Humanoid. These schemes differ in how recurrent weights are trained. Ordered from most to least biologically faithful, they are: a fixed reservoir mimicking an in-vitro neural culture, where recurrent weights are kept frozen and only a linear readout is trained, the same network but with its synapses trained by reward-modulated spike timing dependent plasticity and eligibility propagation, and finally backpropagation through time as a non biological baseline for comparison. All schemes relied on distillation of a reinforcement learning expert policy with dataset aggregation. We show that e-prop reached 100% survival rate matching backpropagation through time, whereas both the fixed reservoir and the reward-modulated STDP, though the closest to biological cultures, plateaued at a much lower performance (~43.7% and ~11% survival). We explore in further detail which trends a real culture could and could not produce until now. Finally we demonstrate brief bipedal stepping on a full size humanoid H1-2.

### Paper [91]
Title: HumanCOIN: A 12,500-Hour Dataset for Human Intent in Egocentric Manipulation
URL: https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.26434
Authors: Shihan Wu, You Liu, Chunyu He, Minghui Qin, Longhan Jiao, Jinbang Guo, Shaoxuan Xie, Kaixi Tian, Wenjie Chen, Jingrui Pang, Yuanhang Zhang, Yichi Zhang, Miao Liu, Tengfei Pan, Yance Jiao, Yunyan Zhang, Yihang Li, Yongjian Shen, Li Liu, Kai Zhu, Chen Fang, Jianxing Chen, Sihan Liu, Xiaofeng Wang, Jie Zhao, Jing Li, Yueqi Wang, Yichen Tang, Wei Li, Zhexuan Yang, Yue Cao, Ji Zhao, Kai Wang, Dongsheng Jiang, Yongwei Wang, Wei Mu, Jiangmiao Pang, Maoqing Yao, Wenchao Ding, Zheng Zhu, Zhongpu Xia, Dalong Du, Jianwei Niu, Dahui Gao, Zhiwei Yu, Bo Lei, Huan Yang, Yonghua Lin, Tiejun Huang, Zhongyuan Wang, Guocai Yao
Abstract: First-person manipulation datasets treat the observation-action pair as the atomic unit and compress the generation and execution of an action into a policy network's weights, rendering the intent state behind the action unobservable. Human motor control research shows that intent mediates between observation and action and leaves observable traces along the intent pathway from decision to effect: gaze reaches the target before the hand, muscle load builds before contact, and cortical activity precedes the motor command. To record this pathway at scale, we present HumanCOIN, a first-person manipulation dataset built around human intent: 12,500 hours across 38 scene classes with three tiers of proxies. At the output end, the L1 attention tier (10,000 h) infers gaze from headband camera video, exposing the operator's next object of attention and reach before the hand moves. Midway, the L2 exertion tier (2,000 h) records surface EMG from armbands, making load expectations readable before execution is complete. At the origin, the L3 cognition tier (500 h) records scalp EEG, giving early readouts of task decisions and monitoring. Each tier inherits all proxies from the tiers below. The dataset further provides multi-view RGB images, depth maps and point clouds, 6-DoF head and wrist pose, and step-level action and object annotations. The controlled L3 setting additionally measures gaze, hand pose and finger kinematics directly. Clock and calibration data are released with the dataset so that any read rate can be re-aligned. The three tiers cover three stations, from visual orienting to cortical preparation, making the process behind visual-action pairs available for modeling at dataset scale. Project page: https://flagopen.github.io/HumanCOIN.

### Paper [92]
Title: One Prior, Two Dynamics: Repurposing Video Diffusion Transformers for Self-Supervised Latent Action Learning
URL: https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.26617
Authors: Yuxin Jiang, Wenqiang Sun, Jiaqi Li, Yuchao Gu, Lvmin Zhang, Ivor Tsang, Mike Zheng Shou
Abstract: Videos reveal how the world changes, but rarely the actions that cause those changes. Latent action learning recovers such supervision from visual transitions, yet often learns the underlying dynamics largely from scratch. We ask whether a pretrained video generator can instead provide the prior for both inverse and forward dynamics. We introduce O$^{2}$LaF, built on a simple principle: one prior, two dynamics. We initialize both models from the same pretrained video DiT and adapt them to their distinct roles: a feed-forward transition encoder with learnable action queries for inverse dynamics, and a causal, action-conditioned video generator for forward dynamics. By controlling information access and optimizing both dynamics end-to-end through a compact bottleneck, O$^{2}$LaF learns transferable actions from unlabeled videos. Across navigation and robotic manipulation, O$^{2}$LaF sets a new state of the art in few-shot action probing and enables stronger zero-shot action transfer across visual contexts and embodiments. Beyond generative video priors, we further extend our framework to predictive representation priors such as V-JEPA2, achieving competitive performance. Our findings suggest a broader paradigm for latent-action learning: repurposing pretrained video models to discover transferable actions and predict their consequences.

### Paper [93]
Title: FlowDrive: moderated flow matching with data balancing for trajectory planning
URL: https://arxiv.org/abs/2509.21961
Authors: Lingguang Wang, \"Omer \c{S}ahin Ta\c{s}, Marlon Steiner, Nick Le Large, Christoph Stiller
Abstract: Learning-based planners are sensitive to the long-tailed distribution of driving data. Common maneuvers dominate datasets, while dangerous or rare scenarios are sparse. This imbalance can bias models toward the frequent cases and degrade performance on critical scenarios. To tackle this problem, we compare balancing strategies for sampling training data and find reweighting by trajectory pattern an effective approach. We then present FlowDrive, a flow-matching trajectory planner that learns a conditional rectified flow to map noise directly to trajectory distributions with few flow-matching steps. We further introduce moderated, in-the-loop guidance that injects small perturbation between flow steps to systematically increase trajectory diversity while remaining scene-consistent. On nuPlan and the interaction-focused interPlan benchmarks, FlowDrive achieves state-of-the-art results among learning-based planners and approaches methods with rule-based refinements. After adding moderated guidance and light post-processing (FlowDrive*), it achieves overall state-of-the-art performance across nearly all benchmark splits. Our code is available at https://github.com/einsteinguang/flow_drive_planner.

### Paper [94]
Title: LCLA: Language-Conditioned Latent Alignment for Vision-Language Navigation
URL: https://arxiv.org/abs/2602.07629
Authors: Nitesh Subedi, Adam Haroon, Samuel Tetteh, Prajwal Koirala, Cody Fleming, Soumik Sarkar
Abstract: We propose LCLA (Language-Conditioned Latent Alignment), a framework for vision-language navigation that learns modular perception-action interfaces by aligning sensory observations to a latent representation of an expert policy. The expert is first trained with privileged state information, inducing a latent space sufficient for control, after which its latent interface and action head are frozen. A lightweight adapter is then trained to map raw visual-language observations, via a frozen vision-language model, into the expert's latent space, reducing the problem of visuomotor learning to supervised latent alignment rather than end-to-end policy optimization. This decoupling enforces a stable contract between perception and control, enabling expert behavior to be reused across sensing modalities and environmental variations. We instantiate LCLA and evaluate it on a vision-language indoor navigation task, where aligned latent spaces yield strong in-distribution performance and robust zero-shot generalization to unseen environments, lighting conditions, and viewpoints while remaining lightweight at inference time.

### Paper [95]
Title: SimVLA: Attributing Gains in VLA Models Through Controlled Ablation
URL: https://arxiv.org/abs/2602.18224
Authors: Yuankai Luo, Woping Chen, Tong Liang, Zhenguo Li
Abstract: Vision-Language-Action (VLA) models often bundle architectural changes with different pretraining data, backbone scales, and optimization recipes, obscuring what actually drives progress. We introduce SimVLA, a deliberately minimal VLA, a standard vision-language backbone with a lightweight continuous-action head, as a controlled testbed for this attribution problem; our contribution is methodological and empirical (a controlled attribution study and a minimal-baseline calibration), not a new technique. Across LIBERO, CALVIN, WidowX, and Google Robot, one-knob-at-a-time ablations show that training dynamics are the largest measured driver, producing performance swings of $\Delta$$\approx$54--94\%, while task configuration and architecture have smaller effects under matched settings. Despite using a small backbone and no additional large-scale robot-data VLA pretraining before benchmark-specific training, SimVLA reaches 98.6\% average success on LIBERO, remains competitive across the other benchmarks, and transfers to held-out real-robot scenes. These results suggest that simple, carefully controlled VLA baselines remain underexplored and provide a calibration point for future architectural claims within the VLM-encoder plus lightweight-head family. Website: https://frontierrobo.github.io/SimVLA

### Paper [96]
