Title: An Autonomous Sea Turtle Robot that Follows Marine Animals
URL: https://arxiv.org/abs/2602.21389
Authors: Zach J. Patterson, Emily Sologuren, Levi Cai, Daniel Kim, Alaa Maalouf, Pascal Spino, Daniela Rus
Abstract: Autonomous robots can transform how we observe marine ecosystems, enabling vision-based focal follows (animal tracking) at scale, but close-range operation in reefs and other cluttered habitats remains difficult. Vehicles must maneuver safely near animals and fragile structures while coping with currents, variable lighting and limited sensing. Previous approaches simplify these problems by leveraging soft materials and bioinspired swimming designs, but such platforms remain limited in terms of deployable autonomy. Here we present an approach to vision-based autonomy that advances the state of the art in biomimetic marine robots and we demonstrate our approach on a sea turtle-inspired underwater robot. The robot combines robust depth-heading stabilization with obstacle avoidance and target-centric control, enabling it to track and interact with moving objects in complex terrain. We validate the approach in controlled pool experiments and in an artificial coral reef at the New England Aquarium, demonstrating stable operation and reliable tracking of fast-moving marine animals. To the best of our knowledge, this is the first integrated biomimetic robotic system, combining novel hardware, control, and field experiments, deployed to autonomously follow real marine animals. With all perception and control running onboard, the robot can hold a commanded depth, navigate around obstacles (91% success rate in the aquarium exhibit with complex lighting and visual features including glass panels) and successfully identify and follow sea turtles fully autonomously. An additional operator-initialized tracking mode extends beyond just sea turtles, including fast-moving rays, barracuda, and human divers. Together, these results establish a practical route toward bioinspired underwater robots capable of autonomous exploration and close-range animal monitoring in sensitive ecosystems.

### Paper [97]
Title: Learning Object-Centric Spatial Reasoning for Sequential Manipulation in Cluttered Environments
URL: https://arxiv.org/abs/2603.02511
Authors: Chrisantus Eze, Ryan C Julian, Christopher Crick
Abstract: Sequential manipulation in clutter requires selecting which objects to remove and determining how to manipulate them. Learning both decisions jointly can complicate training and make failures difficult to diagnose. We present Unveiler, a modular framework that separates scene reasoning and object selection from action execution through a discrete object-index interface. Its Spatial Relationship Encoder (SRE) selects the best object to grasp, while a separate Action Decoder generates orientation-discretized push-grasp actions. The SRE is trained in two stages: imitation learning from heuristic demonstrations, followed by search-based policy improvement using simulated object removals. This training procedure requires no real-robot training data and allows the SRE to be paired with a different execution policy. We evaluate Unveiler in PyBullet simulation and on a physical Dofbot-Pro. In real-robot experiments, it retrieves two occluded targets in the prescribed order in 90\% of scenes, compared with 20-30\% for the heuristic, GPT-4o, and the imitation-only variant. In PyBullet, Unveiler achieves the highest task-completion rate across all six clutter-density and occlusion conditions, reaching 90.0\% under full occlusion with 6-9 objects, compared with 67.5\% for the heuristic and 60.0\% for ThinkGrasp. These results demonstrate the potential of modular, object-centric spatial reasoning for sequential manipulation in clutter.

### Paper [98]
Title: RoboForge: Cross-space Motion Refinement for Physically Executable Humanoid Whole-body Tracking
URL: https://arxiv.org/abs/2603.17927
Authors: Xichen Yuan, Zhe Li, Zhenzhe Zhang, Yangyang Wei, Xinying Guo, Shanghang Zhang, Jianfei Yang
Abstract: Whole-body motion tracking has rapidly expanded the skill repertoire of humanoid robots, yet nearly all of this progress has come from stronger tracking policies, while the motion references they imitate are treated as fixed supervision. We argue that reference quality is a first-order factor in tracking performance. Human motion data carry capture and estimation artifacts, and retargeting adds physical inconsistencies due to the human-robot embodiment gap; discarding such motions sacrifices valuable skill content, whereas retaining them uncorrected undermines policy learning. We present RoboForge, a data-centric framework that resolves this dilemma by refining references instead of filtering them, repairing each error in the space where it arises. In human motion space, a quality-guided diffusion model localizes corrupted segments and inpaints only those segments, leaving the rest of the motion intact. In robot motion space, kinematic repair corrects foot-contact and self-collision defects, and annealed sampling-based optimization under physics rollouts turns kinematically plausible references into dynamically executable ones. RoboForge requires no change to the tracker: it improves three retargeting methods and four tracking policies, raising the success rate of BeyondMimic from 89.51% to 96.20% while reducing the mean per-joint position error (MPJPE-L) by 31.6%. Refined references also accelerate policy learning, restore tracking performance from heavily corrupted inputs, and enable more than 30 high-dynamic motions, including flips and the Thomas flare, on a real Unitree G1.

### Paper [99]
Title: Agent-Centric Observation Adaptation for Robust Visual Control under Dynamic Perturbations
URL: https://arxiv.org/abs/2604.24661
Authors: Zhengru Fang, Yu Guo, Huanhuan Lou, Fei Liu, Yuang Zhang, Yihang Tao, Senkang Hu, Wenbo Ding, Yuguang Fang
Abstract: Visual control maps visual streams to actions in reinforcement learning and vision-language-action (VLA) policies. Changing weather, noise, blur, and compression can obscure different task cues within one episode. Existing control benchmarks mainly test scene distractions, while generic restorers optimize whole-image fidelity. We introduce the Visual Degraded Control Suite (VDCS) to evaluate seven degradations under Markov switching. We propose Agent-Centric Observations with Mixture-of-Experts (ACO-MoE), which routes each frame to a repair expert, restores RGB content, and composes task-relevant foregrounds. On eight VDCS tasks, corruption lowers DreamerV3's mean return from 769 to 562. ACO-MoE raises it to 730, recovering 81.2% of the lost return. It also improves returns on DMC-GB and RoboSuite and retains 70% to 97% of clean-input mean return under six unseen perturbation families. We train ACO-MoE on robot demonstrations and deploy it with a 3B-parameter VLA policy on a Piper arm for cube placement in blue and white boxes and cup placement on a coaster. On the blue-box and cup tasks, motion blur and physical low light with added noise reduce success from 100% to at most 10%. ACO-MoE raises success to 75% to 95% over 20 trials per task and condition.

### Paper [100]
Title: 3D Generation for Embodied AI and Robotic Simulation: A Survey
URL: https://arxiv.org/abs/2604.26509
Authors: Tianwei Ye, Yifan Mao, Minwen Liao, Jian Liu, Chunchao Guo, Dazhao Du, Quanxin Shou, Fangqi Zhu, Song Guo
Abstract: Embodied AI and robotic systems increasingly depend on scalable, diverse, and physically grounded 3D content for simulation-based training and real-world deployment. While 3D generative modeling has advanced rapidly, embodied applications impose requirements far beyond visual realism: generated objects must carry kinematic structure and material properties, scenes must support interaction and task execution, and the resulting content must bridge the gap between simulation and reality. This survey reviews 3D generation for embodied AI and organizes the literature around three roles that 3D generation plays in embodied systems. In Data Generator, 3D generation produces simulation-ready objects and assets, including articulated, physically grounded, and deformable content for downstream interaction; in Simulation Environments, it constructs interactive and task-oriented worlds, spanning structure-aware, controllable, and agentic scene generation; and in Sim2Real Bridge, it supports digital twin reconstruction, data augmentation, and synthetic demonstrations for downstream robot learning and real-world transfer. We also show that the field is shifting from visual realism toward interaction readiness, and we identify the main bottlenecks, including limited physical annotations, the gap between geometric quality and physical validity, fragmented evaluation, and the persistent sim-to-real divide, that must be addressed for 3D generation to become a dependable foundation for embodied intelligence. Our project page is at https://3dgen4robot.github.io.

### Paper [101]
Title: SixthSense: Proprioception-Only Whole-Body Wrench Estimation for Humanoids
URL: https://arxiv.org/abs/2605.01427
Authors: Xingzhou Chen, Xiayan Xu, Yan Ning, Jiyu Yu, Yizheng Zhang, Siyi Qian, Lingzhu Xiang, Jiahao Chen, Ling Shi, Yuquan Wang, Haodong Zhang
Abstract: Humanoid robots are entering the physical world at scale, yet are often criticized as oversized toys---good at dancing, but lacking the force-interaction capabilities for practical tasks. Reliable whole-body contact perception is difficult because floating-base dynamics make proprioceptive wrench recovery ambiguous, while model-based estimators require accurate dynamics and support-force inputs. We propose SixthSense, which uses conditional flow matching to model sparse spatiotemporal fields of link-wise contact gates and 6-D wrenches conditioned on proprioceptive and IMU histories. We train one estimator on pooled data from lying, standing, walking, and motion tracking. Across extensive simulation experiments, SixthSense outperforms learned and model-based baselines in overall contact estimation and remains robust to observation noise. We further establish a real-robot benchmark with our newly collected Unitree G1 dataset, where SixthSense achieves the strongest overall performance among learned estimators and requires approximately 50\,ms per inference. Controlled ambiguity experiments show that its samples recover multiple dynamically plausible contact modes with improved local physical consistency. We finally deploy SixthSense for collision detection and contact-triggered physical human--robot interaction.

### Paper [102]
Title: Topology-Driven Anti-Entanglement Control for Soft Robots
URL: https://arxiv.org/abs/2605.05236
Authors: Haoyang Le, Shengxuan Wang, Mohan Chen, Shuo Feng
Abstract: In the field of precision manufacturing in complex constrained environments, the role of soft robots is increasingly prominent, and the realization of anti-winding control based on multi-intelligent body reinforcement learning has become a research hotspot. One of the core problems at present is to coordinate multiple robots to complete the unwinding operation in a highly constrained environment. The existing distributed training framework faces some observability challenges in high-density barrier and unstable environments, resulting in poor learning results. This paper proposes a topology-driven Multi-Agent Reinforcement Learning (TD-MARL) framework to coordinate multi-robot systems to avoid entanglement. Specifically, the critical network adopts centralized learning, so that each intelligent body can perceive the strategies of other intelligent bodies by sharing the topological state, thus alleviating the training instability caused by complex interactions; eliminating the demand for communication resources between robots through distributed execution, Upgrade system reliability; the integrated topological security layer uses topological invariants to accurately assess and mitigate the risk of entanglement to avoid the strategy from falling into local difficulties. Finally, the full simulation experiments carried out in the real simulation environment show that the method is better than the current advanced deep reinforcement learning (DRL) method in terms of convergence and anti-winding effect.

### Paper [103]
Title: MyoChallenge 2025: A New Benchmark for Human Athletic Intelligence
URL: https://arxiv.org/abs/2605.15650
Authors: Cheryl Wang, Chun Kwang Tan, Balint K. Hodossy, Eric Lyu, Jun Guo, Wentao Zhao, Huaping Liu, Chengkun Li, Merkourios Simos, Bianca Ziliotto, Alexander Mathis, Siyuan Liu, Jiahao Chen, Shanlin Zhong, Bo Jiang, Ci Song, Yaoye Zhu, Chenhui Zuo, Yanan Sui, Mohamed Irfan Refai, Massimo Sartori, Guillaume Durandau, Vikash Kumar, Vittorio Caggiano
Abstract: Athletic performance represents the pinnacle of human motor intelligence, demanding rapid choices, precise control, agility, and coordinated physical execution. Replicating this seamless combination of capabilities remains elusive in current artificial intelligence and robotic systems. Concurrently, understanding the biological mastery of these movements is hindered because complex muscle coordination is rarely measured in vivo due to the limitations of physical equipment. To bridge this fundamental gap in understanding, MyoChallenge at NeurIPS 2025 established a pioneering benchmark for motor control intelligence in sports, leveraging high-fidelity musculoskeletal models within physics simulation combined with machine learning-driven algorithms. The competition introduces two distinct tracks emphasizing either upper or lower limbs control: a table tennis rally task utilizing a biomechanic upper limb composed of an arm with a hand and a trunk; and a soccer penalty kick using a biomechanic model of legs and a trunk. Marking the fourth iteration of the MyoChallenge series, this event attracted almost 70 teams and over 560 submissions globally, uniting a diverse community ranging from physicians and neuroscientists to machine learning experts. The competition facilitated the development of several state-of-the-art control algorithms for a musculoskeletal system capable of sports agility, leveraging techniques such as physics-based motion planners, on-policy behaviour cloning, hierarchical planning, and muscle synergies. By integrating standardized tasks and physiologically realistic models into the open-source framework of MyoSuite, MyoChallenge'25 serves as a reproducible and reusable testbed to accelerate interdisciplinary research across machine learning, biomechanics, sports science, and neuroscience. Project page: https://www.myosuite.org//myochallenge/myochallenge-2025.

### Paper [104]
Title: Orchestrating Wheeled Mobile Robots Online without Conflicts
URL: https://arxiv.org/abs/2605.18441
Authors: Jianghong Dong, Yifeng Zhang, Wenhan Cao, Jiawei Wang, Mengchi Cai, Keqiang Li, Guillaume Sartoretti
Abstract: Formation control of wheeled mobile robots (WMRs) has been widely studied due to its broad applications. However, existing studies primarily focus on tracking predefined formations, with limited adaptability to varying environments. To address this limitation, we propose CFOO, a Conflict-Free Online Orchestration framework for continuous formation navigation of WMRs that integrates centralized formation generation with distributed formation maintenance. The upper layer monitors the environment in real time and generates adaptive formations as needed, using the proposed TCF-R2T (Trajectory-Conflict-Free Robot-to-Target assignment) algorithm to compute optimal assignments in polynomial time for timely, conflict-free formation transitions. At the lower layer, each WMR continuously employs JSTP (Joint Spatio-Temporal trajectory Planning) to maintain the given formation by jointly optimizing spatial positions and segment durations, improving coordination over spatial-only optimization with fixed durations. By coordinating these two layers, CFOO orchestrates WMRs to adapt their formations to varying environments and thus enable continuous navigation. Moreover, we design a Time-Varying Formation Reference (TVFR) mechanism to facilitate smooth transitions between successive desired formations. Both simulation and real-world experiments validate the effectiveness and adaptability of CFOO. Experimental videos are available on our project website: https://dongjh20.github.io/CFOO.

### Paper [105]
Title: Flatness Preserves Instruction Following in Vision-Language-Action Models
URL: https://arxiv.org/abs/2606.23641
Authors: Haochen Zhang, Yonatan Bisk
Abstract: Vision-language-action (VLA) models have the potential for open-world generalization by leveraging pretrained vision-language representations, yet downstream finetuning on limited robot data often degrades these representations. This leads to brittle action policies that ignore language instructions in favor of visual shortcuts, despite the base VLM having this knowledge, which is a failure mode we term instruction blindness. We hypothesize that standard finetuning with limited data applies gradients to a sparse set of points, which manifests as a sharp loss landscape with high-curvature minima. We analyze this effect, then show that regularization through explicit flatness-preserving optimization is a simple yet effective technique while finetuning on the exact same data. Specifically, we demonstrate that applying sharpness-aware minimization (SAM) during VLA finetuning significantly improves instruction following by over 55% across multiple simulation and real-world benchmarks without additional data, architectural modification, or pretraining. Explicitly preserving flatness better preserves pretrained knowledge compared to other regularization techniques that simply anchor model weights or constrain learning. We further analyze the effect of selective sharpness, quantify its effects, and show that such approaches are complementary to existing guidance techniques.

### Paper [106]
Title: Semantic Role-Structured World Model for Physical Interaction Planning
URL: https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.22294
Authors: Juntao Cheng, Yijun Shen, Qunchao Jin, Xiansheng Chen, Wei Liu, Rui Qian, Jingyi Peng, Yunsong Zhou, Zhiwei Yu
Abstract: World models for robotic manipulation must capture how actions transform a scene in ways that are meaningful for task execution. Existing object-centric approaches preserve the transient identity of entities, while leaving the evolving task structure that gives those dynamics meaning largely implicit. We introduce SemRole, a world model that represents scene evolution through semantic roles: a task-conditioned structure explicitly encoding the functional status of scene entities, their task-relevant relations, and the current phase of execution. Conditioned on language, visual observations, and proprioception, SemRole uses this structured state to govern action generation and action-conditioned dynamics, predicting interaction events, relation satisfaction, and phase progression. At inference time, these predictions guide phase-aware action selection and local repair of invalid rollouts. We instantiate SemRole on both lightweight LeWM and pretrained Cosmos backbones, improving closed-loop success from 45.5% to 83.0% on LIBERO, 42.9% to 55.2% on RoboCasa, and 55.0% to 81.0% on real-robot Galbot transfer. Across backbones and domains, explicitly modeling task-semantic structure yields substantially stronger world-model-based manipulation.

### Paper [107]
Title: A Physics-Consistent Benchmark for Contact-Rich Human-Robot Interaction in Assistive Care
URL: https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.02402
Authors: Chengxiao He, Shenghai Yuan, Liuqun Fan, Shenzhe Zhu
Abstract: Conventional task-level evaluation asks whether a robot policy completes a specified action, but can miss failures that emerge only during physical human contact. This limitation is critical in contact-rich assistive tasks, where meaningful evaluation requires a physically responsive human, interaction-quality assessment beyond task success, and a leak-free observer-scorer protocol. We introduce a physics-consistent benchmark for contact-rich human-robot interaction, instantiated in robot-assisted bathing. The benchmark combines a deformable, passively responding human, physics-aware scores alongside task-level success, and a frozen vision-only / scorer-only evaluation protocol. To establish physical validity, region-wise simulated responses are calibrated against force-indentation measurements from Franka impedance pushes on a medical-care manikin. Under a frozen T1-T7 protocol with 140 runs per method, an LLM-augmented state machine (State Machine) achieves 72.9% task success but drops to 56.4% after correct-region and force-safety screening; VoxPoser produces lighter and more stable contact but completes only 27.9% of trials; and zero-shot pi0.5 achieves 0.7% task success with no correct-region or safety-gated successes. These results show that task completion alone does not imply physically valid contact and motivate physics-aware screening before deployment of contact-rich assistive robot policies.

### Paper [108]
Title: A Multimodal Label Forecasting Method for Aperiodic Visuo-Motor Time Series
URL: https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.07930
Authors: Borui He, Garrett E Katz
Abstract: Deep learning models have been increasingly applied to Time Series Forecasting (TSF) in recent years. Transformer-based and MLP-based models have both been used effectively on many real-world TSF regression benchmarks, and there is ongoing debate as to which family of methods is best. While these benchmarks have drawn much attention, it is also worth noting that many current datasets and methods assume approximate periodicity in the time series. In this work, we focus on a new TSF task without periodicity: anticipating falls during humanoid locomotion, on the basis of egocentric vision and proprioception. When the locomotion trajectories are sufficiently diverse, periodicity is violated. We contribute two new benchmark datasets (one from simulation, one from real hardware), showing that periodicity is violated and recent deep TSF methods struggle on these benchmarks. We also propose a novel deep learning architecture that exploits both endogenous and exogenous variables and a training process that rigorously enforces i.i.d sampling of training examples. Our results show statistically significant improvement over prior art in multiple experimental conditions, by 12.73% or more on the real data and 10.40% or more on the simulation data. Code and datasets will be available upon acceptance.

### Paper [109]
Title: GLoTouch: Global-to-Local Haptic Perception Using a Parallel Gripper for Object Search, Recognition, and Grasping Without External Vision
URL: https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.27695
Authors: Zonglin Li, Wanruo Zhang, Yiming Wang, Kun Song, Xinyi Zhou, Daolin Ma
Abstract: Perceiving objects in the environment is a fundamental capability of autonomous robots. In dark or low-light environments, external cameras often fail to reliably perceive object positions and geometry; when visual sensing is unavailable, completing target search, recognition, and grasping through touch alone becomes a key robot manipulation capability. This task must simultaneously address container-scale spatial exploration and object-scale fine-grained geometric perception, which is particularly challenging for low-degree-of-freedom parallel grippers. However, a unified framework remains lacking for connecting container-scale spatial exploration with object-scale fine-grained geometric perception and grasping. To address this challenge, we present \textbf{GLoTouch}, a global-to-local haptic perception and manipulation framework built on a parallel gripper. In the global stage, the gripper holds a passive long-reach probe, combining force measurements with known tool geometry to localize contacts and actively estimate candidate-object positions, coarse contours, and heights. In the local stage, the robot sets down the probe and uses the bilateral visuotactile sensors on the same gripper to directly acquire local haptic observations, which are matched against a given target 3-D model without object-specific training. We evaluate the framework in both simulation and real-robot experiments. Source code will be open-sourced.

### Paper [110]
Title: RAVEL: Asynchronous Rolling Inference for Flow-Based Vision-Language-Action Models
URL: https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.34170
Authors: Yuhan Chen, Ke Yu, Pengfei Liu, Shuxun Wang, Yi Yang, Linchao Zhu
Abstract: Flow-based vision-language-action (VLA) models are highly effective for generalist robot manipulation, yet their reliance on computationally expensive VLM encoding and multi-step iterative action generation imposes a significant latency bottleneck. The resulting inference latency makes it difficult for robots to respond quickly, especially in dynamic environments. We address this limitation with RAVEL (Rolling Asynchronous VLA Enabling Low-Latency Control), an asynchronous inference framework that addresses the computational bottlenecks of both the VLM backbone and the action expert. To reduce the delay from multi-step action denoising, RAVEL allows near-term actions to be executed after a single additional denoising step by carrying partially denoised future actions forward in a rolling buffer. To avoid blocking on slow VLM encoding, RAVEL decouples VLM encoding from rolling action generation, allowing the action expert to operate continuously using the latest available VLM context, while a lightweight Fast Observation Pathway (FOP) directly conditions the action expert on current observations. Across simulated and real-world manipulation tasks, RAVEL consistently achieves substantially lower response latency while maintaining the task capability of the underlying VLA, enabling high-frequency and responsive closed-loop control.

### Paper [111]
Title: Video2SwimFish: An Automated Pipeline for Reconstructing Controllable Fish Models and Biological Locomotion from Real Fish Videos
URL: https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.38966
Authors: Hangong Chen, Linfeng Cheng, Tahsin Zaman Jilan, Jiaye Wu, Lee Caesar, Ian Fuller Yantian Zha
Abstract: We present Video2SwimFish, an automated pipeline and benchmark for building controllable fish assets from real-fish videos for underwater embodied AI. Given synchronized multi-view videos of an individual fish, the pipeline generates a metrically scaled deformable mesh from a VLM-selected canonical frame, builds internal articulation adapted to that individual's morphology through a VLM actor-critic loop, and extracts a Biological Locomotion Manifold (BLM). The BLM provides a low-dimensional action space bounded by real-fish motion. Compared with learning directly in joint space, it lets the individual swimming policy of each reconstructed fish converge faster and reach a higher task success rate. We release two paired datasets: synchronized top- and front-view recordings of 120 individual fish across 6 species, and the controllable assets and individual swimming policies derived from them. Because every asset is tied to the animal it came from, the dataset supports a benchmark that evaluates locomotion learning not only on task success but on fidelity to that individual's locomotion behavior. We evaluate on two tasks, 3D trajectory following and target reaching, and a case study in which a simulated BlueROV underwater robot captures the assets. We further validate biological realism with a human study in which fish experts rate the simulated fish, as a complement to the automated metrics. We find that task success and locomotion fidelity do not necessarily improve together, and we identify faithful reproduction of individual animal locomotion as an open challenge for the community. Project website: https://hangongchen.github.io/video2swimfish-web/

### Paper [112]
Title: RMRRT: Riemannian Barrier Metric RRT for Inequality-Aware Steering on Equality Manifolds
URL: https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.06863
Authors: Minhyeong Kang, Sanghyun Kim
Abstract: This paper presents a motion planning framework that unifies equality and inequality constraints within a single geometric formulation for sampling-based planning in high-dimensional robotic systems. In conventional sampling-based planners, equality constraints are typically enforced through projection, whereas inequality constraints are handled separately through binary validity checks such as collision testing, often leading to inefficient exploration. To address this limitation, we propose Riemannian Barrier Metric RRT (RMRRT), which constructs a unified local geometry for planning on equality-constrained manifolds. RMRRT first builds an ambient barrier metric from inequality-sensitive barrier terms and then induces a tangent-space metric via a G-orthogonal projection associated with the equality constraints. The resulting tangent-space metric is used consistently in both steering and nearest-neighbor selection, biasing exploration away from nearby inequality boundaries while preserving first-order equality consistency. In this work, the metric is instantiated from signed-distance-based geometric proxy inequalities to provide collision-informative tangent-space directions; hard feasibility is enforced separately through standard validity checks. Experimental results show that RMRRT achieves a 100% success rate across diverse constrained manipulation tasks in both simulation and real-world settings, while reducing planning time relative to representative constrained planning baselines. Ablation studies further demonstrate that the proposed metric improves exploration quality by reducing rejected samples and shortening path length. Experiment videos and source code are available at: https://rcilab.khu.ac.kr/rmrrt/

### Paper [113]
Title: PILOT: Guiding Long-Horizon World-Model Planning for Robot Manipulation with Imagined Subgoals and Policy Priors
URL: https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.14335
Authors: Yang Liu, Hanchen Cui, Karthik Desingh
Abstract: Latent world-model planning has shown promising results in goal-conditioned robotic manipulation, but remains challenging for long-horizon planning due to limited temporal guidance and inefficient action search. A distant goal image offers little guidance for preparatory actions, capturing intermediate subgoal images by hand is impractical, and uninformed sampling makes action search inefficient. We propose PILOT (Policy-Initialized Local Optimization with Temporal subgoals), which brings two foundation models into latent world-model planning: a video model turns a language instruction into visual subgoals, and a vision-language-action policy initializes the cross-entropy method (CEM). CEM refines action sequences toward each subgoal with an action-conditioned world model, and no goal image is needed at test time. With both models fine-tuned on 40 demonstrations, PILOT achieves 68.8% success on four real UR5e tasks, compared with 55.0% without policy initialization, 13.8% for policy-only execution, and 0.0% for direct final-goal planning. In simulation, PILOT exceeds direct execution of the same policy by 30 percentage points on long Push-T tasks and by 22 percentage points on LIBERO-10. Subgoals and the policy prior are complementary: better subgoals and stronger priors each raise success, and even a weak policy that succeeds alone on only 3.3% of medium Push-T tasks is an effective prior.

### Paper [114]
Title: Evo-DexBench: Benchmarking Generalization across Related Tasks for Dexterous Manipulation
URL: https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.21180
Authors: Bing Cheng, Yiran Mao, Zewei Ye, Yifan Jin, Xinyi Chen, Ruifeng Tu, Shuhuai Wang, Tao Lin, Zhimu Zhou, Yuxin Du, Fangwei Zhong, Junchi Yan, Bo Zhao
Abstract: General-purpose dexterous manipulation requires policies to transfer knowledge from related tasks to new tasks with only a few target demonstrations. Existing benchmarks often provide too many target demonstrations and leave the differences between source and target tasks uncontrolled. As a result, they cannot clearly distinguish knowledge transfer from task memorization. Our key insight is the Core-Gen principle. Core tasks are related tasks that share a functional interaction and provide source knowledge, while a Gen task is a novel target within the same functional family that has only a few demonstrations. This setup tests whether a policy can reuse functional interaction knowledge. Based on this principle, we propose Evo-DexBench, which controls both source-task relatedness and target-task exposure. It comprises 30 tasks in six functional families, three everyday layouts per task, and 1,248 multimodal demonstrations collected via VR teleoperation with simulated Shadow Hands equipped with 123 taxels. We first compare few-shot adaptation strategies and find that joint Core + Gen training makes the most effective use of Core knowledge. Under this setting, we evaluate six representative policies. The best-performing policy, $\pi_{0.5}$, achieves a mean success rate of only 24.4%, revealing the limitations of existing methods in transferring functional interaction knowledge. Further analysis shows that on the tested Gen targets, functionally matched Core data yields larger success-rate gains than mismatched data, and that with matched Core data, 8 Gen demonstrations reach the performance of training on 16--32 Gen demonstrations alone. These results indicate that Evo-DexBench separates knowledge transfer from task memorization under controlled conditions and effectively evaluates the generalization of dexterous manipulation policies across related tasks.

### Paper [115]
Title: SuperMimic: Scaling Humanoid Whole-Body Control with One Differentiable World Model
URL: https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.28480
Authors: Qiayuan Liao, Yiyang Shao, Junfeng Long, Kevin Zakka, Roberto Horowitz, Pieter Abbeel, Koushil Sreenath
Abstract: Tracking large human-motion datasets has become the dominant route to general, expressive humanoid whole-body control, but scaling across many motions is expensive. Current pipelines use model-free reinforcement learning, either merging per-motion specialists through lossy distillation, or brute-forcing a single policy with cluster-scale compute. SuperMimic amortizes learning across all motions with one differentiable world model of their shared dynamics, fit from logged transitions of a standard rigid-body simulator. The world model and the tracking policy train together in one loop, and backpropagation-through-time over short imagined rollouts gives the policy analytic gradients. Fast imagined rollouts and analytic gradients make policy learning efficient. In 3 hours on a single GPU, SuperMimic surpasses the return of a state-of-the-art motion tracker baseline trained for 7 days on 128 GPUs. It reduces GPU-hours by more than $1000\times$ and fails on only 25 of 129,514 benchmark motions, versus 179 failures for the baseline. After 9.8 hours on two GPUs, SuperMimic achieves zero failures. From 1 to 264 hours of motion, the world model's prediction error stays stable, and GPU-hours to a target return grow slowly. Our policy transfers to a physical Unitree G1 across diverse and acrobatic motions. SuperMimic removes compute as the bottleneck to scaling humanoid whole-body control, making it practical to measure motion-tracking scaling laws.

### Paper [116]
Title: Rollout then Recycle: 3,718 Labelled Real-Robot Episodes from Benchmarking 7 Policies on 12 Tasks
URL: https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.03168
Authors: Praveen Selvaraj, Lorenzo Uttini, Ville Kuosmanen
Abstract: We release all 3,718 episodes from the ArmnetBench v0.1 evaluation (arXiv:2607.24481), which contains 600 teleoperated demonstrations and 3,118 rollouts of seven imitation-learning and vision-language-action policies on 12 tabletop tasks, recorded on low-cost SO-101 arms; 73% of the rollouts are failures. Every policy for a task was trained or fine-tuned on the same 50 demonstrations and rolled out on the same cell, so the corpus contains many policies' failures on shared tasks. An operator labelled every rollout successful, suboptimal or failure. Each episode has three camera views, joint states and actions and is available on the Hugging Face Hub in LeRobot v3.0 and a reward-model format. This note documents how the episodes were collected and the limitations that could affect their use.

### Paper [117]
Title: ConDR: Conservative Reachability through Neural Hamilton-Jacobi Subsolutions
URL: https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.06802
Authors: Kendra Givens, Taylor T. Johnson, Bryan C. Ward, Thomas Beckers
Abstract: Hamilton-Jacobi (HJ) Reachability identifies the unsafe states of a dynamical system from which it can be forced into failure under worst-case disturbance. However, classical grid-based solvers scale exponentially with state dimension. DeepReach improves scalability by replacing the grid with a neural value function trained on the HJ-Isaacs (HJI) variational inequality (VI), but penalizes both sides of the residual equally. This allows the learned value to lie above the true one and unsafe states to be misclassified as safe. We propose Conservative DeepReach (ConDR) which aims to learn a subsolution of the HJI VI such that the learned unsafe set contains the true one. ConDR enforces the obstacle branch and terminal condition by construction, pushes the HJI residual to a safety margin $\epsilon$ below zero with a one-sided loss, and prevents the degenerate solution of classifying everything as unsafe. We prove that the exact solution of the margin-shifted VI that ConDR trains toward is a subsolution of the original VI so its unsafe set contains the true one and its value lies within $\epsilon\tau$ of the true value function. We compare against DeepReach and MADR in the established systems of Air3D, Air6D, a 9D multi-vehicle system, and a 13D quadrotor. ConDR reports the lowest rate of unsafe state misclassified and the lowest closed-loop collision rates with at most a small increase in intervention. On Air3D and Air6D, ConDR tolerates model mismatch that scales with $\epsilon$. At matched false-negative rates, ConDR's unsafe sets are smaller than the baselines showing the safety is not obtained by trivial over-approximation.

### Paper [118]
Title: On Agentic Manipulation Policies
URL: https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.09892
Authors: Chen Chen, Omid Bagherieh, Naveen Nikamanth Appiah Balaji, Jesse T. Gonzalez, Negin Heravi, Fahad Islam, Xueting Li, Jean-Baptiste Regli, Kejia Ren, Petr \v{S}vec, David J. Yoon, Matthias M\"uller, Vladlen Koltun
Abstract: Recent work on robot manipulation has focused on specialist policies trained on demonstrations. We show that generalist AI models can control manipulators more reliably than specialist policies such as vision-language-action models (VLAs) and world-action models (WAMs). We systematically analyze agentic manipulation policies (AMPs), in which a generalist AI model commands the manipulator directly, adapting its actions as the task unfolds. We present AMP1, a reference harness that runs unchanged across single-arm, bimanual, and mobile manipulation. AMP1 achieves state-of-the-art results on the RoboLab-120 and CaP-Gym benchmarks, with no demonstrations or task-specific training. On RoboLab-120, AMP1 reaches an 85.6% success rate zero-shot, compared to 49.2% and 42.9% for leading VLA and WAM policies. The agent succeeds zero-shot in 104 of 108 real-world trials spanning twelve tasks and three robot instances, reducing failure rates by 20x and 15x relative to strong VLA and WAM baselines. The basic harness supplies no grasping, pushing, or placing skills; the agent develops its own strategies, such as searching for hidden objects, using objects as tools, and calibrating its cameras. With the addition of a persistent memory store, the agent can also learn from experience: an augmented harness with a memory representation that is carried across episodes lowers the failure rate on RoboDojo bimanual manipulation tasks by 4.6x relative to zero-shot deployment. Our analysis suggests that AMPs are substantially more general and robust than specialist manipulation policies, providing a promising foundation for robot manipulation. See https://apple-aiml-research.github.io/ml-amp/ for code and videos.

### Paper [119]
Title: Fast Inference-Time Policy Refinement via Action-Affine World Models
URL: https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.15011
Authors: Run Wang, Liang Wu, J\'an Drgo\v{n}a, Yilin Mo
Abstract: Imitation policies can fail through compounding errors, while existing refinement methods often require additional on-policy learning or expensive online planning. Our key insight is to model the response to small corrections around a policy proposal in an affine form that enables fast optimization. We refine a frozen imitation policy using an \textit{action-affine world model} (A\textsuperscript{2}WM), trained offline from perturbed policy rollouts. Conditioned on history and the proposal, the model directly predicts a nominal trajectory and a linear action response over the full horizon. With quadratic costs and linear constraints, refinement becomes a cheap quadratic program (QP), requiring only one model evaluation and one QP solve per control step. To the best of our knowledge, we are the first to combine an action-affine world model with QP-based inference-time refinement of frozen imitation policies. Across four manipulation tasks, our framework achieves the highest success rate among evaluated baselines, adding only 4.2 ms of mean refinement latency per control step, excluding base-policy sampling.

### Paper [120]
Title: When Does Cross-View Consistency Matter in Multi-View Appearance Augmentation? A Controlled Study for Visuomotor Policies
URL: https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.17751
Authors: Zixuan Jiang
Abstract: Multi-view visual augmentation often seeks consistency across cameras, but it is unclear which forms of consistency matter for downstream control. We use simulation-state replay to manipulate cross-view coupling while preserving the per-camera perturbation distributions, physical trajectories, and action labels. Across seven LIBERO manipulation tasks, marginal-matched changes in global appearance, ghost injection, and target color have small average costs for a late-fusion Diffusion Policy: point estimates are near zero or negative, and the largest upper endpoint of the 90% posterior credible intervals is 2.55 percentage points. In contrast, geometrically anti-coupled observations reduce success by 3.30 percentage points relative to coupled observations (90% CrI [0.64, 6.28]), with displacement magnitude, direction marginals, and the number of affected views matched. Camera interventions reveal substantial dependence on wrist-view geometry, while feature analysis shows appearance sensitivity patterns associated with the factors randomized during training. ACT shows a higher target-color cost estimate than DP, with uncertainty that includes zero and sensitivity to task composition. Within the tested tasks and policy configurations, these findings motivate selective consistency constraints: appearance coupling can sometimes be relaxed, whereas task-relevant geometry and cross-view spatial correspondence warrant protection.

### Paper [121]
Title: Vision-Language-Action Models: A Life-Cycle Survey of Design, Training and Deployment
URL: https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.18372
Authors: Suvarna Kadam (Witsense AI), Soran Parsa (University of Huddersfield)
Abstract: Vision-language-action (VLA) models adapt web-pretrained vision-language models to output robot actions, so that web knowledge carries over to physical control. In under four years the field has grown from RT-2, the first VLA, to open models that fine-tune on a single consumer GPU and proprietary generalists that clean unseen homes. VLA research joins robotics, computer vision and language modelling, so this survey is written for researchers and practitioners from each of these fields. It reviews 89 works from December 2022 to October 2026, mainly on manipulation and navigation, plus humanoid control, world-action models and evaluation. Seven design axes follow a VLA's life cycle: model design (inputs and memory, architecture, action output, intermediate predictions), training (pretraining data, post-training) and deployment (real-time execution). We compare 22 models on these axes, with their training data, model size, inference speed and hardware. Three trends emerge. (i) Training data has broadened beyond teleoperated robot data (about 10,000 hours for $\pi_0$) to community-collected episodes and egocentric human video (20,000 hours for GR00T N1.7). (ii) Model sizes have split into two groups: most recent models have 3-7 billion parameters; a new group under one billion runs on consumer GPUs. (iii) Generalist VLAs handling both manipulation and navigation are emerging, such as UniVLA and Qwen-VLA. We also compare VLAs with world-action models, which predict future observations as well as actions, and review evidence that some VLAs with near-perfect benchmark scores fail after small scene changes. A practitioner's guide gives a default for each design decision, and a glossary supports readers new to the field.

### Paper [122]
Title: Modeling the Frictional Contact of Continuum Robots with Environments through Linear Complementarity Problem
URL: https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.18546
Authors: Jia Shen, Junhyoung Ha, Yue Chen
Abstract: Accurate friction modeling in continuum robots is essential for precise motion control and path planning. However, current approaches make predictions only for the mechanism's internal friction that is limited by the boundary forces of the Coulomb friction cone, which fail to characterize the static friction and motion hysteresis. In this paper, we propose a novel friction model based on complementarity constraints under the Coulomb friction assumption. This model predicts various friction effects caused by environmental interactions, including both static and sliding frictions and hysteresis behavior, as well as the corresponding robot deformation induced by these interactions, which are crucial for the practical motion planning and control of continuum robots. Additionally, real-time implementation of this model can be achieved, meeting the demand for computational efficiency in robotic applications. Experimental validations demonstrated that this model is able to reduce the maximum tip position error from 45.4 mm in the frictionless model to 5.85 mm in our friction-included model with a 287.9 mm-long tendon-driven continuum robot.

### Paper [123]
Title: ThinkWAM: Rethinking Latent Futures for Action Refinement
URL: https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.21258
Authors: Haoyun Tang, Baqiao Deng, Enjin Zhou, Songwen Tian, Keyao Xu, Bowen Jing, Kun Wang, Ke Ma, Zhandong Mei, Huibin Li
Abstract: World-action models (WAMs) couple action generation with predictions of future states. An initial action chunk may be flawed. A future prediction that is not conditioned on the chunk is identical for every proposal and thus carries no proposal-specific signal, limiting its usefulness for refinement before execution. We introduce ThinkWAM, a latent WAM that establishes bidirectional feedback before execution through a proposal-rethink-refine loop: the policy first proposes a complete action chunk, the proposal corrects multi-horizon future latents through a learned residual, and the corrected latents are injected into a second pass of the same action head, which generates the executable chunk. An affordance-guided observation-action memory and a near-fine, far-coarse multi-horizon prediction scheme, both motivated by predictive coding, support this loop, and all prediction stays in latent space. ThinkWAM achieves 98.70% success on LIBERO, 84.76% on LIBERO-Plus without OOD fine-tuning, 85.75%/65.85% on RoboTwin 2.0 Clean/Random, and 65.17% on RoboCasa24. ThinkWAM achieves 64.4% mean success across three real-world OOD conditions. The mean two-pass inference latency per action chunk is 153.48 ms on LIBERO, 164.61 ms on RoboTwin, and 101.2 ms on the Galaxea A1Z. Ablations show complementary gains from rethinking and affordance guidance. Diagnostics indicate that proposal-conditioned corrections change both predicted futures and action trajectories, showing that the corrected future representations improve the final action.

### Paper [124]
Title: DailyBrick: Language-Guided Synthesis of Brick Assembly Instructions
URL: https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.22132
Authors: Hang Zhang, Qijian Tian, Yuan Xie, Xuhong Wang, Xin Tan
Abstract: Generating a LEGO model from a user request is more than a plausible 3D shape generation. LEGO models require physical support per brick, and each brick should be placed unblocked. Current methods address this issue by training an expert large language model. We reduce the problem of generating extremely long context on all brick connections to that of writing a program that builds LEGO models like a human, considering each intermediate step could be executed. We introduce DailyBrick , a multi-stage agentic system that produces a LEGO model together with a brick-by-brick assembly instruction for human building and robot assembly. We design a multi-stage harness with roles to help LLMs generate more effectively. A versioned construction SDK compiles multi-level decisions, from semantic subparts to individual bricks, into canonical operations. An external Meta-Reviewer agent replays and inspects the generated package, verifies the model and its construction process, identifies failures, traces them to the responsible stage, and routes them to bounded repairs or to editable candidates for later skill or tool updates. Extensive experiments demonstrate that DailyBrick generates LEGO models based on simple user requests better than recent agentic systems, and show its utility in downstream applications such as robot manipulations on model assembly from instructions and brick sets (https://spatialagentlab.github.io/DailyBrick.github.io/).

### Paper [125]
Title: RESCUE Jumper: A Centimeter-Scale Rolling and Jumping Robot for Search and Rescue
URL: https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.23531
Authors: Kiran Marques, Shashwat Singh, Ben D'Ambrosia, Zeynep Temel, Melisa Orta Martinez
Abstract: Centimeter-scale robots are promising for urban search and rescue because they can enter confined spaces that are inaccessible or unsafe for humans. However, their small size limits obstacle traversal after deployment. This paper presents the RESCUE Jumper, a 125 g mobile robot with a 6.2 cm width and 6.2 cm diameter wheels that combines ground rolling with jumping to traverse steps and gaps that exceeds its rolling capability. The RESCUE Jumper uses its two actuators for both rolling mode and jumping by reversing the direction of wheel motion. Forward wheel motion enables rolling, while reverse wheel motion winds a spring steel tail to store elastic potential energy and release it for jumping. The RESCUE Jumper varies tail winding speed to control jump direction, transitioning from backward to forward jumps as winding speed increases from 0.75 rad/s to 8.73 rad/s. The launch angle of the jumps ranges from 45 degree to -11 degree with respect to the vertical axis, reaching a maximum height of 9.2 cm and a maximum lateral displacement of 11.2 cm. We model the winding speed-dependent jumping behavior in three phases: tail release to determine the contact point and robot orientation, tail straightening until liftoff, and ballistic flight to predict jump height and lateral displacement. Finally, qualitative tests highlight the robot's locomotion in cluttered, real-world environments. The model and experiments presented in this work can inform future designs of space-constrained jump mechanisms in centimeter-scale robots for search and rescue and inspection in unstructured environments.

### Paper [126]
Title: CoT-WAM: Collaborative Training of Vision-Language Models and World-Action Models via Plan Latents
URL: https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.23803
Authors: Jinhao Zhang, Yushen Zuo, Ke Rui, Weitao Zhou, Haibao Yu, Honglong Tian, Xiaoheng Ma, Jie Mei, Jiawei Wang, Minglei Li
Abstract: General robot manipulation requires both semantic competence and physical foresight. World action models (WAMs) acquire an understanding of and predictive capability over the physical world by jointly modeling visual foresight and action distributions. However, existing WAMs still lack semantic competence: they have limited ability to perform open-world scene understanding, semantic reasoning, and long-horizon planning. Although vision-language models (VLMs) provide these capabilities, effectively integrating their semantic priors into WAM prediction and control remains challenging. To address this challenge, we propose CoT-WAM, a jointly trained WAM-VLM framework. CoT-WAM uses a learnable embedding to exchange information between the VLM and the WAM, which is supervised by textual and goal-image signals during training to encode rich intent information. A tailored two-stage training strategy enables joint optimization and more effective collaboration between the VLM and WAM. We evaluate CoT-WAM on six benchmarks covering control performance, zero-shot generalization, and long-horizon planning. CoT-WAM achieves SOTA performance on LIBERO, CALVIN, and RoboCasa-Kitchen, and demonstrates stronger zero-shot generalization on LIBERO-Plus and LIBERO-Pro without fine-tuning. On RoboCasa365, it further improves long-horizon planning over baselines with and without external planners. Real-world experiments further demonstrate the effectiveness of our method in practical deployments. Code is available at: https://github.com/jhz1192/CoT-WAM.git

### Paper [127]
Title: Ego2X: Contact-aware Transfer from Egocentric Human Data to Any Robot
URL: https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.24138
Authors: Xinke Lyu, Bowen Yang, Zhicheng Wang
Abstract: Embodied models depend on real interaction data, yet collecting it on physical robots is slow and labor-intensive. Recent ego-to-robot pipelines retarget first-person view human videos to robot data via transferring hand and arm motion. They judge fidelity mainly by visual appearance. However, trajectories often fail in terms of physics: fingers hover beside objects, and grippers close on empty space. We argue that how the object moves and the contacts that drive the motion count. Thus, we present Ego2X, an object-centric framework that converts monocular egocentric video into physically executable robot data across dexterous hands and parallel grippers. Ego2X centers on CORE (Contact-centric Object-interaction REpresentation), which records each contact on the object by its surface anchor, as well as relative force, grasp mode, and interaction state. Throughout the pipeline, CORE corrects hand-object drift via object-relative constraints, steers inverse kinematics toward contacts the robot must realize, and preserves held objects during arm erasure and robot compositing. On HOT3D, contact-driven correction reduces in-contact hand depth error by 33.7%, while on EgoDex, contact-driven retargeting realizes 62.9% of annotated contacts, surpassing the official HuRo pipeline (27.9%). In RoboTwin 2.0, post-training X-VLA on Ego2X episodes alone, without simulator demonstrations, raises task success rate from 40.5% zero-shot to 67.0% across 200 paired scenes. We will publicly release our conversion pipeline and CORE-annotated dataset for dexterous hands and parallel grippers.

### Paper [128]
Title: Efficient World-Model Planning via Physics-Guided Search Distillation
URL: https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.24171
Authors: Hongyu Zhang, Xiaoshuai Hao, Heye Huang, Qingwen Meng, Wenjun Shi, Jianao Xu, Xin Hu, Xuanru Jin, Jingyi Zhang, Baicang Guo
Abstract: World models support autonomous driving by evaluating candidate trajectories through predicted future scene evolution. However, accurate future prediction does not ensure road-compliant execution; when all candidates violate road boundaries, re-ranking cannot select a feasible trajectory. We propose Physics-Guided Search Distillation (PGSD), a single-view planning framework that combines future representations and execution feedback for verified beyond-pool repair through three components: (1) Execution-Aware Selection checks ego-vehicle road compliance under simulated execution and selects the highest-scoring feasible trajectory; (2) a Clearance-Aware Search Teacher generates beyond-pool corrections and selects demonstrations with larger minimum road-boundary clearance during execution to buffer imitation errors; and (3) a Future-Conditioned Repair Student predicts one correction from rewarder features and execution feedback for single-rollout verification. The pretrained planner and rewarder remain frozen; no future scenes are generated online. On NAVSIM v1, single-view PGSD achieves 90.97 PDMS, exceeding all compared prior single-view planners and retaining 73.2% of the search teacher's PDMS gain over execution-aware selection. On NAVSIM v2 navhard, it reaches 38.96 EPDMS, improving on WorldDrive by 11.24 points (40.5%). The student replaces up to ten search rollouts with a single verification rollout per repair trigger. HUGSIM Hard examples provide qualitative evidence of zero-shot transfer.

### Paper [129]
Title: Homeward: No Place Like the Training View
URL: https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.24455
Authors: Mingyang Lyu, Yinqian Sun, Gongce Cao, Guobin Shen, Yuhao Zhang, Lu Jia, Chenfei Yan, Qian Zhang, Pin Lyu, Chen Gao, Bo Wang
Abstract: Vision-language-action (VLA) policies implicitly assume that third-person cameras remain fixed at their data-collection poses; once a camera is bumped, remounted, or swapped at deployment, task success degrades precipitously even though the underlying task and robot remain unchanged. We present Homeward, a training-free, plug-and-play observation adapter that canonicalizes observations from an uncalibrated deployment camera back into the training view for frozen VLA policies. Our design stems from a closed-loop diagnostic paradox in simulation: while re-rendering perturbed views into the training camera with oracle geometry recovers much of the lost performance, substituting an off-the-shelf feed-forward 3D foundation model can leave the policy worse off than applying no correction at all. This degradation concentrates on the thin, articulated robot arm, which feed-forward depth prediction tears and scatters - yet the arm's exact 3D state is already known from robot kinematics. Homeward exploits this asymmetry: it estimates what is unknown - camera parameters and static scene depth - from the live RGB frame and a single training-view reference, and renders what is known - the moving robot links - directly from the kinematic model. Evaluated across $\sim$300,000 closed-loop episodes spanning four frozen VLAs, Homeward boosts success across all 1,599 camera variants of LIBERO-Plus from 62.5%, 76.5%, 68.0%, and 3.6% to 77.6%, 95.1%, 91.2%, and 26.4% for OpenVLA-OFT, $\pi_{0.5}$, $\pi_0$, and OpenVLA, respectively. Without any scene fine-tuning or deployment camera calibration, Homeward extends to 6-DoF random placements, focal-length changes, and, with per-step pose estimation, continuous intra-episode camera drift, while matching or surpassing a calibrated, scene-trained view-synthesis baseline on three of four policies on LIBERO-Plus.

### Paper [130]
Title: Security of Deep Reinforcement Learning for Autonomous Driving: A Survey
URL: https://arxiv.org/abs/2212.06123
Authors: Ambra Demontis, Srishti Gupta, Maura Pintor, Luca Demetrio, Kathrin Grosse, Hsiao-Ying Lin, Chengfang Fang, Battista Biggio, Fabio Roli
Abstract: Reinforcement learning (RL) enables agents to learn optimal behaviors through interaction with their environment and has been increasingly deployed in safety-critical applications, including autonomous driving. Despite its promise, RL is susceptible to attacks designed either to compromise policy learning or to induce erroneous decisions by trained agents. Although the literature on RL security has grown rapidly and several surveys exist, existing categorizations often fall short in guiding the selection of appropriate defenses for specific systems. In this work, we present a comprehensive survey of 86 recent studies on RL security, addressing these limitations by systematically categorizing attacks and defenses according to defined threat models and single- versus multi-agent settings. Furthermore, we examine the relevance and applicability of state-of-the-art attacks and defense mechanisms within the context of autonomous driving, providing insights to inform the design of robust RL systems.

### Paper [131]
Title: Curvature-aware 3D length estimation of greenhouse cucumbers using RGB-D imaging and cubic spline arc-length integration
URL: https://arxiv.org/abs/2606.22439
Authors: Manveen Kaur, Rajmeet Singh, Saeed Mozaffri, Majid Ahmadi, Shahpour Alirezaee
Abstract: Commercial greenhouse cucumber production is graded by fruit length, which drives harvest scheduling, labour allocation, and logistics. Manual measurement with thread or caliper is accurate but infeasible at commercial scale. This paper presents CucumberVision, a non-contact length estimation framework using an Intel RealSense D435 RGB-D camera. A YOLO26n instance segmentation model locates cucumbers, and SAM (ViT-B backbone) refines each detection to a pixel-precise mask. Five methods are evaluated under matched conditions: (M1) a dominant-axis skeleton scan-line baseline; (M2) PCA on the bounding-box depth point cloud; (M3) SAM mask with medial-axis skeletonisation; (M4) a hybrid keypoint-guided approach using a YOLO26-pose model predicting five anatomical landmarks (KP0--KP4) with piecewise 3D arc-length; and (M5) a novel medial arc spline method fitting a cubic spline through the 3D medial axis of the SAM mask and computing arc length by trapezoidal integration -- the first such application to elongated vegetable measurement. All methods share five-frame burst depth averaging, colour-stream intrinsic alignment, and adaptive method selection with cascading fallbacks ensuring 100% coverage. A benchmark of 48 captures across seven cucumbers in three size categories (small ~8 cm, medium ~13 cm, large ~25 cm) with thread-based ground truth establishes a significant accuracy hierarchy: M1 (MAPE 9.68%) > M2 (5.31%) > M4 (5.51%) > M3 (5.82%) > M5 (4.13%). M5 significantly outperforms all competitors at Bonferroni-corrected alpha=0.0125. A secondary contribution is identifying a 12--18% length underestimation caused by using depth-stream rather than colour-stream intrinsics after rs.align(rs.stream.color) -- an under-reported error source. The complete system is released open source and runs in real time on a single consumer-grade GPU.

### Paper [132]
Title: Depth-Only Open-Vocabulary 3D Semantic Segmentation For Privacy-Preserving Robotic Applications
URL: https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.00978
Authors: Xuying Huang, Sicong Pan, Maren Bennewitz
Abstract: Privacy-preserving perception is increasingly important for robotic systems operating in real-world indoor environments, yet it remains underexplored in open-vocabulary 3D semantic segmentation. We study this problem under an RGB-prohibited deployment setting motivated by scene-specific visual information disclosure, where real RGB observations are unavailable during scene acquisition and fusion. To reflect this deployment constraint on existing 3D datasets, we adopt a stricter depth-only evaluation protocol that re-runs scene fusion without RGB and exposes only the resulting depth-derived geometry to the segmentation pipeline. This constraint removes appearance cues that are often critical for open-vocabulary recognition, making depth-only predictions more uncertain and less reliable. To address this challenge, we propose UTTO, a model-agnostic uncertainty-guided test-time optimization framework that uses structured predictive uncertainty as a reliability signal to refine predictions from frozen open-vocabulary 3D backbones. Experiments across ScanNet and Matterport3D demonstrate consistent improvements over multiple depth-only backbones. Privacy recoverability analyses and a real-robot semantic goal grounding case study further support the proposed privacy-constrained setting and applicability.

### Paper [133]
Title: Towards end-to-end optimization in multimaterial 3D printing
URL: https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.13174
Authors: Xue-Ling Luo, Steven Yang, Jingye Tan, Robert F. Shepherd, Noy Cohen, Nikolaos Bouklas
Abstract: Multimaterial 3D printing enables the fabrication of functionally graded components, but optimizing their spatial material distribution alongside structural topology remains a formidable challenge due to high-dimensional design spaces and complex constitutive modeling. Using neural network constitutive laws in topology optimization requires their tangents and design sensitivities, which current approaches obtain by automatic differentiation through the network or derivation by hand. This paper combines sparsification, which reduces a trained physics-augmented neural network to a compact, interpretable closed-form law, with symbolic differentiation, enabling an end-to-end workflow from a new continuously varying multimaterial system to its mechanical simulation and design. By extracting closed-form, composition-aware hyperelastic constitutive laws from sparsified neural networks trained on experimental data, this approach facilitates exact symbolic differentiation of the terms required by the adjoint state method implemented with FEniCSx, circumventing the bottlenecks of applying neural network constitutive models in analysis and optimization. This pipeline is deployed on soft robotic gripper applications, demonstrating continuous composition optimization for highly anisotropic contact responses, and the concurrent optimization of macroscopic topology and material distribution under non-failure stretch constraints. This study is a step toward replacing conventional constitutive modeling and laborious empirical prototyping, establishing interpretable machine-learning models as practical, robust design primitives for advanced multimaterial additive manufacturing.

### Paper [134]
Title: GroundAnything: Reconciling Parallel Decoding with Precise Visual Grounding at Flash Speed
URL: https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.39600
Authors: Qize Yu, Lianrui Fan, Bowen Ping, Xini Ding, Zetian Song, Junbo Niu, Kaixuan Wang, Tianxing Chen, Yue Chen, Minghua He, Yuran Wang, Jie Huang, Haojun Zhang, Min Chen, Hao Li, Wenxuan Song, Ruihai Wu, Xianming Liu, Shilong Liu, Shuchang Zhou, Ping Luo, Shiyu Huang
Abstract: Autoregressive (AR) grounding models serialize spatial predictions, introducing sequential latency and imposing a causal order on output tokens. We view grounding as visual evidence extraction: objects, locations, and spatial relations are jointly constrained by the image and query, yet their dependencies do not imply an intrinsic left-to-right generation order. This distinction makes bidirectional diffusion a natural fit, allowing spatial hypotheses to emerge in parallel and be jointly refined through iterative denoising. We introduce GroundAnything, a 4B-parameter grounding foundation model that reconciles fast parallel decoding with precise localization through blockwise denoising. Training combines grounding pretraining from public datasets and dedicated data engines, direct AR-to-diffusion conversion with joint AR and diffusion objectives, supervised fine-tuning, and GRPO-based reinforcement post-training. Across 30 grounding benchmarks, our autoregressive variant, GroundAnything-VLM, establishes a new overall state of the art among similarly sized models at 72.42%, remaining competitive with GPT-6 Astra (71.35%). With entropy-guided decoding, GroundAnything also surpasses the prior state of the art at this scale, averaging 61.75% versus 53.32% for the fast MTP-based LocateAnything model. We further explore decoding strategies, showing that an optional self-speculative mode achieves a 4.51$\times$ speedup over the AR counterpart with a 0.74 percentage-point drop in COCO F1mIoU. Infrastructure experiments show that progressive inference optimizations translate parallel decoding into practical speedups. These gains support efficient visual grounding in latency-sensitive real-world systems.

### Paper [135]
Title: GroundingPI: A Grounding Foundation Model towards Physical Intelligence with Visual Primitives
URL: https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.39601
Authors: Qize Yu, Lianrui Fan, Boyu Chen, Jiaqi Liang, Xini Ding, Yue Chen, Zetian Song, Yuran Wang, Yi Zou, Kaixuan Wang, Tianxing Chen, Wenxuan Song, Bohan Zhou, Mingleyang Li, Siqiao Huang, Yuqi Ye, Caigao Jiang, Wei Wei, Ruihai Wu, Hang Zhang, Yixiao Ge, Shuchang Zhou, Shilong Liu, Xianming Liu, Ping Luo, Shiyu Huang
Abstract: Precise grounding matters. It specifies which object is the target and where that object is, even in clutter and for tiny objects, and it has to be fast enough for closed-loop control. Yet vision-language-action (VLA) and world-action models (WAMs) take perception from general-purpose vision-language and video-generation backbones, which still fail in these settings. We introduce GroundingPI, a 4B grounding foundation model that generates points and boxes as quantized coordinates in a shared vocabulary. Training combines multimodal and spatial pretraining, supervised fine-tuning, and reinforcement learning with GRPO, using supervision from public datasets and dedicated data engines. Against 44 baselines across 34 grounding benchmarks spanning 11 perceptual capabilities, GroundingPI establishes a new state of the art, averaging 73.68%, above the larger GPT-6 Astra (71.54%). As a downstream visual backbone, GroundingPI improves performance on robotic manipulation and autonomous driving. Across RoboTwin 2.0 and RoboCasa-GR1, it outperforms all evaluated backbones in all four out-of-distribution settings, with relative gains of up to 24.8% over the strongest baseline. On RoboCasa-GR1, GroundingPI trained with 50% of the demonstrations outperforms those baselines trained with 75%. Its mean open-loop L2 error on nuScenes is 0.296 m. We systematically analyze GroundingPI's pretraining in scale and data composition. Grounding quality and downstream manipulation improve with pretraining scale, with generalization tracking perceptual gains most closely. Analyzing the data recipe across these 11 perceptual capabilities shows dense grounding's substantial benefits for both, and OCR's potential as a catalyst for perceptual learning. These results support grounding as a perceptual foundation, and dedicated perceptual pretraining as a promising direction for foundation models of physical intelligence.

### Paper [136]
Title: StableGrasp: Reconstructing Physically Stable Human Hand Grasps from Single Images
URL: https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.09195
Authors: Han Jiang, Georgios Pavlakos, Qixing Huang, Etienne Vouga
Abstract: Reconstructing a physically stable human grasp from a single RGB image is challenging because physically modeling grasps is itself difficult, and the problem requires estimating not only a visually constrained hand pose but also a single static hand control that stabilizes the grasp. Existing methods either model only visual hand geometry without considering physics, or are evaluated with incomplete physical modeling, which limits the physical validity of the resulting grasps. In this paper, we present StableGrasp, a differentiable simulation-based optimization framework that explicitly separates the visual hand pose from a control target that determines the grasping forces. Our method jointly optimizes hand geometry and control by minimizing the kinetic energy of the grasp in a differentiable simulator, while regularizing the hand geometry to preserve visual consistency and geometric plausibility. The reconstructed grasps are substantially more stable under physical simulation with complete physical modeling, while remaining visually consistent with the input images and geometrically plausible. Experiments show that our approach produces far more stable grasps than alternative hand-control strategies, benefiting visual-only grasp reconstruction pipelines by turning their outputs into physically stable grasps.

### Paper [137]
Title: Composable Driving Style for End-to-End Autonomous Driving
URL: https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.14802
Authors: Guantian Zheng, Lidong Yu, Tianyu Gao, Jiashu Li, Jiaxing Chen, Wilfried Ariel Mulyawan, Chen Lv
Abstract: End-to-end driving policies can achieve strong task performance while offering limited control over their driving style. Driving style describes how a vehicle accelerates, brakes, and maintains distance. Policies trained without explicit preference conditioning lack a direct mechanism for adjusting these behaviors at inference. We propose YOUDrive, a vision--language--action planner for continuous and composable driving-style control. Each teacher's behavior is distilled into a task vector, a learned parameter update that induces a style direction in the planner's representation. These directions can be scaled and combined at inference without retraining the backbone. Conditioned on these directions, a flow-matching decoder models a multimodal trajectory distribution and generates kinematically feasible motion alternatives. We introduce the Style Alignment Score (SAS) to describe human-referenced kinematic behavior after safety and task gating. On NAVSIM navtest, a single coefficient monotonically controls assertiveness across six teacher-derived personas, while PDMS remains above 0.90 across the tested individual and blended configurations, reaching 0.954. Further analyses reveal systematic policy-specific kinematics after controlling for scene context, whereas the evaluated discrete style labels provide limited additional explanatory power. Code, checkpoints, and the metric will be released.

### Paper [138]
Title: Kairos: A Unified PDDL Planner for Robots: Plans in Milliseconds, with a Guarantee for Every Answer
URL: https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.39454
Authors: Francisco Mart\'in Rico, Esther Aguado Gonz\'alez, Francisco Miguel Moreno Olivo, Rodrigo P\'erez Rodr\'iguez, Jos\'e Miguel Guerrero Hern\'andez, Francisco J. Rodr\'iguez Lera
Abstract: Robots that plan while they act need a task planner that reads the models their designers write, answers within milliseconds, and says what each answer means. Models are written in the Planning Domain Definition Language (PDDL), which has grown from classical planning to durative actions, numeric resources, time windows and continuous change, while planners cover one fragment each and differ in what their failures mean. The contribution in artificial intelligence is Kairos, a single planner, not a portfolio of existing planners, that covers the whole language and derives what each answer guarantees from the search that produced it. We prove when the exhaustion of a search shows that no plan exists, and give a relaxation of temporal tasks that proves unsolvability where temporal search cannot. For the compression of durative actions, we give a criterion on the values that actions read, prove that compression is sound, and show that it is not complete, so its failure is never reported as a proof. An independent validator checks every plan. The engineering application is on-line task planning for robots: Kairos is open source, packaged for the Robot Operating System, and the default planner of a widely used planning framework. On 1,756 public tasks from nine suites, Kairos solves more tasks than the strongest evaluated planner of each suite within 100 milliseconds, 1 second and 30 seconds; it proves ten of eleven unsolvable tasks, and handles each of the seventeen language features probed, where no other planner handles more than eight.

### Paper [139]
Title: ReactWorld: Structuring Interaction Responses for Embodied Video Generation
URL: https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.06120
Authors: Hanwei Liu, Huijia Zhang, Hanzhang Wang, Guangyu Liu, Hanbei Zhan, Linjie Hu, Bin Xiao, Xuan Zhou, Dong Zhang
Abstract: Embodied video world models predict scene changes caused by robot actions, yet generated object responses may remain inconsistent with task intent and contact dynamics. Critical responses in embodied scenes often concentrate in local regions and specific time intervals. Inspired by chain-of-thought reasoning, we introduce ReactWorld, an embodied video generation model guided by structured interaction anticipation. In the generation backbone's feature space, an interaction response field predictor anticipates how the interaction will unfold, explicitly describing participant roles, future motion response intensity, and local motion changes. A response feedback adapter forms increments to object features from the difference between the object branch's outputs with predicted and zeroed local dynamics inputs. It also converts predicted displacements into positional biases for temporal attention. These two mechanisms guide object feature updates and information retrieval across frames, respectively. Through interaction anticipation and generative feedback, ReactWorld achieves an average score of 77.51 on RBench, outperforming PhysisForcing by 3.29 points with improvements in all nine categories. On EZS-Bench, it achieves a Domain score of 86.98 and an average score of 81.87.

### Paper [140]
Title: TransGHOST: Real-Time, Synthesis-Free Reconstruction of Instrument-Occluded Regions in Ophthalmic Microsurgery
URL: https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.13971
Authors: Niran Nataraj, Maina Sogabe, Norihiko Ito, Kenji Kawashima
Abstract: Intraoperative scene perception in ophthalmic microsurgery is limited by instrument occlusion: when a tool enters the high-magnification field, it conceals tissue from both the surgeon and the perception components of robot-assisted systems that process the video. Generative inpainting can remove the occluder but replaces the missing image content with synthesis, which is unsuitable when visual provenance matters. Many video inpainters also depend on future frames and cannot operate online. We present TransGHOST, a causal reference-guided reconstruction pipeline whose non-generative safe mode operates in real time. Every restored value is obtained by resampling a recorded frame using geometric transforms fitted only to visible regions. The system provides per-pixel confidence and provenance and marks unsupported pixels as abstentions. We evaluate TransGHOST on a new veterinary clinical corpus of canine cataract surgery with pixel-level instrument and anatomy labels. Only the occluded region is scored, and each scoring target is verified to differ from the reference supplied to the system. Safe mode reaches 31.89 dB masked PSNR and 0.915 masked SSIM at 51.7 ms median and 85.5 ms 95th-percentile latency. On a pre-declared held-out cohort using instrument cutouts excluded from segmentation training, it reaches 29.83 dB masked PSNR, while matched-mask evaluation on validation shows a 3.45 dB advantage over LaMa. Region-aware reference selection improves masked PSNR by 3.60 dB over recency when candidate references can themselves be occluded. Reference-age analysis further defines the operating interval for the brief, repeated occlusions targeted by TransGHOST.

### Paper [141]
Title: Closing the Analyze-to-Design Gap: An Authorization-Bounded Agent for Self-Driving Laboratories
URL: https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.25043
