Through a procedure we term "Human-AI Spec-Solution Co-Optimization," we iteratively elicit clinical experts' definitions and judgments while aligning the AI pipeline with their intended criteria. We retrospectively applied this framework at an urban safety-net hospital to length of stay and 30-day unplanned readmissions. The final pipelines screen patient charts for potential factors and filter for clinically relevant ones by self-rating, and these self-ratings achieved over 70% concordance with human experts (within one Likert point) on held-out encounters. When scaled up hospital-wide, the pipelines identified a superset of the candidate factors found in prior, primarily manual analyses. By providing auditable reasoning traces and quantifying factor prevalence, the pipelines support a more quantitative and reproducible way to prioritize factors for prospective QI interventions.

### Paper [378]
Title: Post-Reasoning: Improving the Performance of Non-Thinking Models at Negligible Cost
URL: https://arxiv.org/abs/2605.06165
Authors: Richmond Sin Jing Xuan, Rishabh Bhardwaj, Soujanya Poria
Abstract: As the widespread adoption of Large Language Models (LLMs) accelerates, token consumption from intermediate reasoning traces increasingly contributes to inference latency and operational cost. Recent studies suggest that many real-world tasks require little to no explicit reasoning, with additional reasoning sometimes even degrading performance. In this work, we propose Post-Reasoning, a simple yet effective approach that improves instruction-tuned models by conditioning them to justify their answers after generating the final response. By design, it enables the final answer to be obtained with no reasoning tokens before the answer and with negligible latency and token cost, while still improving performance through simple instruction augmentation. We evaluate Post-Reasoning across 117 model benchmark settings spanning 13 open and proprietary models, 7 model families, and 9 diverse reasoning and knowledge-intensive benchmarks, including AMC, HMMT, GSM8K, GPQA, MMLU-Pro, and BIG-Bench Hard. Post-Reasoning improves performance in 84.62% of evaluated settings, achieving a mean relative improvement of 15.57%. Furthermore, we propose supervised post-reason tuning, which further improves performance in 91.11% of evaluated settings, and exceeds the prompt-based post-reasoning baseline by an average of 7.17%, demonstrating that post-reasoning can be effectively internalized through training. Ultimately, Post-Reasoning establishes a new performance ceiling for direct-answer capabilities.

### Paper [379]
Title: Structured Role-Aware Policy Optimization for Multimodal Reasoning
URL: https://arxiv.org/abs/2605.07274
Authors: Bingqing Jiang, Guoxi Zhang, Jasper Wang, Bingning Wang, Difan Zou
Abstract: Reinforcement learning from verifiable rewards (RLVR), especially with Group Relative Policy Optimization (GRPO), has shown strong potential for improving the reasoning capabilities of large vision-language models (LVLMs). However, in multimodal reasoning, final-answer rewards are typically assigned at the sequence level and do not distinguish the functional roles of different tokens, making it difficult to determine whether a correct answer is supported by task-relevant visual evidence. In this paper, we revisit multimodal RLVR from the perspective of role-aware token-level credit assignment, where structured responses are decomposed into perception tokens for extracting visual evidence and reasoning tokens for deriving answers from that evidence. Based on this perspective, we propose Structured Role-aware Policy Optimization (SRPO), which refines the sequence-level GRPO advantage into role-aware token-level advantages without changing the reward function. Specifically, SRPO assigns role-specific credit by using self-distilled on-policy contrasts: perception tokens are emphasized according to their visual dependency under original versus corrupted visual inputs, while reasoning tokens are emphasized according to their consistency with the generated perception. These role-specific signals are further unified through a shared trajectory-level baseline, yielding positive token weights that adjust relative update magnitudes while preserving the original GRPO reward and advantage signs, without requiring external reward models or separate teachers. Experiments across diverse multimodal reasoning benchmarks show that SRPO improves evidence-grounded reasoning, highlighting the importance of moving beyond uniform sequence-level credit toward role-aware optimization for reliable multimodal reasoning.

### Paper [380]
Title: Insights Generator: Systematic Corpus-Level Trace Diagnostics for LLM Agents
URL: https://arxiv.org/abs/2605.21347
Authors: Akshay Manglik, Vijay S. Kalmath, Jason Qin, Apaar Shanker, Kaustubh Deshpande, Yash Maurya, Veronica Chatrath, Levi Lentz, Yuan Xue
Abstract: Diagnosing failures in LLM agents remains largely manual. Practitioners inspect a small subset of execution traces, form ad-hoc hypotheses, and iterate. This process misses patterns that only emerge across trace populations and does not scale to production corpora where individual traces span tens of thousands of tokens. We formalize the problem of corpus-level trace diagnostics. Given a corpus of execution traces, the goal is to produce grounded natural-language insights that characterize systematic behavioral patterns across trace groups, each linked to supporting evidence. We present the Insights Generator (IG), a multi-agent system that answers diagnostic questions by proposing and testing hypotheses across the trace corpus to produce an evidence-backed insights report. We evaluate IG across qualitative and objective dimensions, spanning rubric-based report assessment and downstream performance improvements achieved by implementing IG insights. Human experts using IG reports improve scaffold performance by 30.4 pp over the unmodified baseline scaffold, and coding agents leveraging IG-derived insights show consistent and stable gains. Across benchmarks, IG's scout-investigator architecture produces findings comparable in detection coverage to competing approaches, while domain experts rated IG reports as leading depth and evidence quality.

### Paper [381]
Title: From "Weak" Signals to Strong Models: Preference Delta Aggregation with LoRA Merging
URL: https://arxiv.org/abs/2606.00357
Authors: Qi Sun, Siyue Zhang, Yulin Chen, Yuxiang Xue, Ru Peng, Chen Zhao
Abstract: Training strong large language models (LLMs) requires high-quality supervision, which is often scarce. Recent work shows that paired preference data from weak-weaker model pairs (e.g., Qwen3 4B over 1.7B), despite the limited quality of individual responses, can provide an effective supervision signal through relative quality deltas, which we term a "weak" signal. This motivates a key research question: can multiple "weak" signals be constructively aggregated for improving strong models (e.g., Qwen3 8B)? To this end, we propose Preference Delta Aggregation (PDA), the first framework that derives a preference delta from each weak-weaker model pair, instantiates it as a LoRA adapter learned through preference optimization, and aggregates the resulting deltas via LoRA merging. To improve the composition of independently trained adapters, we introduce Geometric Alignment Merging (GAM), which aligns their left and right singular bases through orthogonal Procrustes rotations and averages the aligned factors. Evaluations on knowledge reasoning and agentic search show that PDA-GAM exceeds the strongest individual delta, with further gains along the evaluated sequences of additional deltas. Using three reasoning deltas and four agentic deltas, PDA-GAM improves Qwen3-8B by 6.8 and 7.3 points on average, respectively, exceeding the strongest single-delta baseline by 2.1 and 4.3 points. These configurations achieve the highest average scores among the evaluated merging methods, with similar gains observed on a second student model. Further analysis suggests that these gains come from composing complementary capabilities encoded across distinct preference deltas. Code is available at https://github.com/AlbertQiSun/Preference-Delta-Aggregation.

### Paper [382]
Title: VESTA: Visual Exploration with Statistical Tool Agents
URL: https://arxiv.org/abs/2606.00384
Authors: William Rudman, Abhishek Divekar, Kanishk Jain, Sebastian Joseph, Stella S. R. Offner, Matthew Lease, Kyle Mahowald, Greg Durrett, Junyi Jessy Li
Abstract: Fitting quantitative models to data is a central step in scientific workflows, yet it remains one of the least automated. Recent agent-based systems leverage language and vision-language models (VLMs) to propose statistical models, but these systems struggle on challenging modeling tasks. To address these limitations, we introduce VESTA (Visual Exploration with Statistical Tool Agents), a framework that equips VLMs with a dynamically growing toolkit to guide model refinement through data transformations, hypothesis-driven visualizations, and statistical tests. Unlike prior systems that rely on iterative critique alone, VESTA explores data before and during refinement by selecting or creating reusable diagnostic tools. We evaluate VESTA using three toolkit configurations: no tools, static expert-written tools, and dynamic model-written tools. To support this evaluation, we introduce DAWN (Dataset for Automated Workflows and Numerical Modeling), a benchmark targeting distribution fitting and time series modeling with varying difficulty tiers that culminates in real-world astronomy tasks including modeling initial mass functions and gravitational-wave chirp signals. VESTA's dynamic tool creation outperforms prior agentic pipelines for statistical analysis and visual tool creation, with the largest gains on complex and domain-specific tasks. Against strong agentic coding harnesses (Claude Code, Codex, and OpenCode), VESTA is better on all time series tasks and on complex distribution fitting tasks, though the harnesses achieve better fits on simpler distributions. Further, dynamically generated tools are substantially more sophisticated than those produced by existing visual tool-creation systems, covering more diagnostic categories per function and strongly preferring visual outputs that the VESTA VLM critic can reason over directly.

### Paper [383]
Title: Business World Model
URL: https://arxiv.org/abs/2606.10044
Authors: Cecil Pang, Hiroki Sayama
Abstract: World model has emerged as a powerful paradigm in artificial intelligence, enabling agents to represent their environments, predict future states, and evaluate possible actions before acting. However, existing world models have largely been developed for visual or physical domains such as computer vision, robotics, gaming, and autonomous driving, which are governed by relatively stable dynamics. These formulations are not directly applicable to business practice, where the relevant environment is semantic, organizational, and market driven. Outcomes depend on context-sensitive factors such as customer behavior, pricing, competition, and regulation. This paper introduces the concept and architecture of a Business World Model, a world model specialized for business and organizational environments. We propose a business-semantics-centric formulation in which states, dynamics, and feasible actions are linked to business entities, their attributes, and their relationships. Within this framework, intelligent agents can simulate alternative action sequences, estimate their effects on future business outcomes, and evaluate trade-offs under uncertainty and business constraints. We present a reference design and open-source implementation in which LLM agents interpret natural-language business goals and explain results, while a deterministic, metadata-driven simulation engine integrating machine learning models, business rules, and action spaces performs all calculations. A churn case study of a digital subscription business demonstrates that the system recommends options meeting the churn-reduction target within the revenue constraint. This work establishes a conceptual foundation for autonomous business systems capable of moving from instruction-based execution toward goal-driven planning, optimization, and execution.

### Paper [384]
Title: Multi-Objective Structured Pruning of LLMs for Latency and Model Size Optimization
URL: https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.22583
Authors: Muhammad Junaid Ali, Smail Niar, El-Ghazali Talbi
Abstract: Large Language Models (LLMs) have achieved widespread adoption because of their strong reasoning and query-response capabilities. However, deploying them in embedded and edge computing environments remains challenging because of strict latency, memory, and energy constraints. Their large parameter counts and computational demands hinder efficient execution on resource-constrained platforms. Although model pruning has emerged as a viable solution for reducing scale while preserving performance, jointly optimizing layers, attention heads, and Multi-Layer Perceptron (MLP) dimensions remains highly complex. Exhaustively exploring this combined design space is computationally expensive and often leads to local optima or unstable configurations. To address these limitations, we propose a hardware-aware, multi-objective structured pruning framework. The proposed two-stage method explicitly targets latency and model size for efficient deployment on edge devices. In the coarse-grained stage, multi-objective depth pruning removes entire attention and MLP blocks to reduce computational load and memory usage. In the subsequent fine-grained stage, Parallel Bayesian Optimization (PBO) searches for the optimal layer-wise pruning ratios for pruning under latency constraints, while importance-based strategies rank the specific components to be pruned within each layer's allocated budget. Experimental results show that our approach reduces model complexity with minimal impact on commonsense reasoning tasks and zero-shot performance. Our method achieves a favorable trade-off among accuracy, latency, and model size, making it suitable for edge deployment. Across multiple LLMs at 37.5% and 50% pruning ratios, the proposed approach achieves better performance on commonsense reasoning tasks than existing methods while significantly reducing inference cost.

### Paper [385]
Title: Tokengeist: Multi-Turn Attribution Tracing in Agentic Conversations
URL: https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.22610
Authors: Jessica Tang, Shraddha Barke, Sharad Agarwal
Abstract: When a model answers after several turns of dialogue and tool use, an overseer needs to know which earlier turns and intermediate steps it rests on. Attribution is the natural primitive for answering this, but existing context attribution methods process the full transcript in a single pass: they recover surface-level dependencies while missing the layered structure of multi-turn traces, in which an assistant turn is itself a derived artifact that condenses or transforms earlier context. We introduce multi-turn context attribution (MTCA): given a target span in a model response, the task of tracing attribution backward across turns to identify not only which prior turns were directly relevant, but also how those turns themselves depended on earlier context. We propose Tokengeist, an attribution-method-agnostic and scalable framework that recovers full dependency paths by casting attribution as a recursive traversal of a directed acyclic graph over conversation turns. We will release MTCABench, a benchmark of 3,970 target spans across 685 multi-turn conversations, annotated with gold provenance graphs reaching depths of up to 17, across four dependency types. Across four open-weight models, flat attribution methods fail to recover multi-hop dependencies, with source recall improving from 11-38% under flat attribution to 82-94% under Tokengeist. Our results reveal a systematic failure mode of single-pass attribution, which we term provenance collapse, where attribution terminates at the nearest assistant restatement instead of the originating event.

### Paper [386]
Title: Cautious Context Steering for Language Model Personalization
URL: https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.05813
Authors: Gihoon Kim, Jeyoung Lee, Suhan Woo, Jangwon Oh, Sekwon Oh, Minsu Jeon, Euntai Kim
Abstract: User context can personalize a language model without per-user fine-tuning, but its influence is not equally helpful at every token. Context Steering (CoS) uses a fixed steering strength throughout generation and requires two LM forward passes per step. We propose Cautious Context Steering (CCS), a lightweight adapter that learns when and how strongly to steer a frozen LM using user context. We train the adapter through cautious distillation, using oracle targets built from predictions with and without context. It learns to match the steered distribution where context helps predict the user's preferred token and the context-free base distribution elsewhere. The CCS adapter outperforms both training-free context-based methods and trained personalization baselines in preference alignment across benchmarks. We also introduce GSM8K-P to evaluate task accuracy and task-specific format preferences together. CCS achieves high format adherence while maintaining task accuracy, offering a better balance than the baselines. The CCS adapter adds less than 0.4% of the backbone's parameters. At inference, each decoding step uses one context-conditioned LM forward pass and a lightweight adapter evaluation, reducing generation time on PRISM by approximately 35-36% compared with CoS.

### Paper [387]
Title: CHORUS: Coverage-Guided Agentic RL and Policy Consolidation for Testbench Stimulus Generation
URL: https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.10090
Authors: Hejia Zhang, Sheng Lu, Zhijing Wu, Yitong Ma, Zhongming Yu, Wentao Ni, Chia-Tung Ho, Jishen Zhao
Abstract: Testbenches written by large language models can compile and run while leaving entire datapaths idle. We present CHORUS, the first framework to combine coverage-guided agentic reinforcement learning (RL) with policy consolidation for testbench stimulus generation. We train LLM4Cov's final supervised checkpoint to draft testbenches and repair its lowest-scoring draft using simulator feedback, raising CVDP-ECov agentic Pass@1 from 69.2% to 85.5%. Applying the same training to three checkpoints yields similar overall pass rates, yet the policies succeed on different designs. Averaging their weights combines these strengths in a single dense 4B model that reaches 86.7% agentic Pass@1. When simulator rewards select the teacher for each training prompt, the distilled 4B model reaches 88.0%, 13.5 percentage points above DeepSeek-R1 (671B).

### Paper [388]
Title: Fool's Gold: Defensive Deception Against Safety-Removal Attacks on Open-Weight Models
URL: https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.17202
Authors: Mark Russinovich
Abstract: Safety alignment in open-weight language models is trivially removable: abliteration projects a refusal-mediating direction out of the weights in minutes, and no release-time defense we are aware of prevents it durably. What cannot be prevented can be deceived. Our defense, decoy hardening ("Fool's Gold"), concedes the refusal strip and poisons its payoff: once refusal is stripped, most answers to hazardous operational requests are confident, fluent decoys whose critical elements are falsified. Decoys are trained inside a differentiable simulation of the attack, expressing only in the attacked state; a refusal pin and benign leash hold clean-state behavior to the original. We instantiate it on seven models from five families (9B-122B, dense and mixture-of-experts). On the six models passing our pre-registered efficacy gate, 0.51-0.90 of attacked-state responses to held-out prompts are decoys, +0.27-0.84 attributable to the defense; all six stay within registered benign-behavior and capability budgets; the seventh (smaller) fails the gate (boundary case). Rates replicate on a frozen test split or untouched strata. The claim is epistemic: without independent ground truth, no observation surface we tested separates falsified answers from correct ones - on external red-team benchmarks' CBRNE-adjacent slice, the defended 122B is fatally wrong on 0.82-0.86 of matched-quality answers vs at most 0.10 undefended. Repeated sampling does not restore trust: element-wise consensus at K=64 reconstructs a fully usable procedure on 0.083-0.625 of prompts where the instrument validates, vs 0.58-0.96 undefended, with no label-free way to tell the regimes apart; on the weakest such model the claim is per-draw only. We evaluate chemical and biological hazards; the defense does not address in-context jailbreaks and protects only the initially released defended weights.

### Paper [389]
Title: Measurement-Budget Allocation in Quantum Learning with Finite-Shot Generalization Guarantees
URL: https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.24891
Authors: Ferhat Ozgur Catak
Abstract: On near-term quantum hardware, estimating a Born probability requires repeated circuit executions. A quantum learning experiment with a fixed measurement budget $B$ must therefore decide how many distinct training states $n$ to use and how many shots $S$ to spend on each. We study this trade-off for binary quantum classifiers whose measurement operator $M$ is learned from the training data and evaluated with fresh measurement outcomes. We prove a distribution-free generalization bound for bounded, $L$-Lipschitz losses with a sample term of order $\sqrt{d/n}$ and a shot term of order $\sqrt{(\log n)/S}$; under $B=nS$ the two terms move in opposite directions. Minimizing a closed-form surrogate of the bound gives the allocation rule $n^* = 2\sqrt{2dB/\log(4B/\delta)}$, $S^* = B/n^*$, whose bound value is within 0.1% of the exact numerical minimum on all budgets we evaluate, and a worst-case rate of $B^{-1/4}$. PennyLane simulations with 2- and 4-qubit variational circuits on nine synthetic benchmarks show that the empirical gap stays below the bound in all configurations, although the bound is vacuous at the simulated budgets and becomes non-vacuous only for larger budgets (e.g., $B \gtrsim 5000$ for $d=4$). For a loss that is concave in the score, the empirical gap is U-shaped under a fixed budget and the measured shot contribution matches its analytical value; for the absolute loss, shot noise is unbiased and no shot-limited regime appears. The rule can be used to plan finite-shot evaluations before an experiment is run.

### Paper [390]
Title: Balance of Benchmarks: Evidence Retention and Multiplicity-Aware Model Comparison
URL: https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.30044
Authors: Jhen-Ke Lin, Hong-Yun Lin
Abstract: Benchmark aggregation requires decisions about how much evidence to use and how much influence each benchmark receives. Curated suites control influence through selection, which can exclude informative observations. A broader pool can retain those observations. With equal weighting, repeated or closely related benchmarks gain influence as their number grows. We introduce Balance of Benchmarks (BoB) to retain eligible observations and control their influence separately. We fit benchmark-specific response curves to place different difficulty ranges on a shared ability scale and use description similarity to downweight benchmarks in crowded neighborhoods. We test retention and density weighting in separate interventions on WildScores, a sparse collection of 148 developer-reported benchmarks, using 14 Artificial Analysis benchmarks as a controlled reference. With the model population fixed and entire source-lineage families held out, suite expansion improves held-out prediction under BoB and equal-weight equating on both WildScores and BIG-bench. Under substantial reporting overlap, density weighting improves robustness to multiplicity. Complete re-listings yield smaller mean rank displacement under BoB (0.63 versus 1.95 for equal-weight equating), with benefits also in paraphrased re-listings and supported affine fits. The benefit can reverse if models report different benchmarks, because a single weight per column gives models that report only part of a group less than its intended share. We therefore compute each model's weights from the benchmarks it reports. This rule removes the reversal in controlled re-listings and keeps held-out prediction close to that of equal weighting. These comparisons separate evidence retention, benchmark influence, and reporting coverage and show why repeated entries need to be handled per model.

### Paper [391]
Title: EvoSkill Injection: Red-Teaming Autonomous Skill Generation and Evolution in Self-Evolving Agents
URL: https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.30429
Authors: Doyun Kim, Chanwoo Kim, Sugyeong Eo, Yeo-Chan Yoon, Chanjun Park
Abstract: LLM-based agent systems increasingly adopt skill-based architectures to reduce repetitive reasoning costs and improve stable, efficient task execution. Recent studies propose self-evolving agents that autonomously generate, refine, and reuse skills from past experiences to enable continuous capability evolution. However, autonomous skill evolution introduces a new attack surface in which malicious capabilities are generated, stored, and reused as legitimate skills. In this paper, we define EvoSkill Injection as a threat model targeting the autonomous skill generation and evolution pipeline of self-evolving agents. We further propose SARGE (Red-teaming Autonomous Skill Generation and Evolution in self-evolving agents), a red-teaming framework for evaluating this threat model through iterative generation, escalation, and reinforcement interactions. To support our framework, we construct EvoSkillBench, a benchmark dataset of malicious interaction trajectories for inducing malicious skill formation in self-evolving agents, and introduce EvoSkillSafetyBench, a post-attack benchmark for evaluating whether injected malicious skills are subsequently retrieved and activated as harmful behaviors. Our evaluation shows that SARGE induces malicious skill formation and that injected skills are persistently stored and repeatedly activated, highlighting the risk of persistent capability corruption.

### Paper [392]
Title: Homeostatic Continual Learning
URL: https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.13771
Authors: Yue Jin
Abstract: I formulate a Continual Learning problem for an autonomous agent that imitates the one animals face. The agent has intents to satisfy. It experiences contexts where different combinations of features are active and impact intent outcomes. I propose a method named ``Homeostatic Continual Learning'' that imitates the one animals use. The agent learns incrementally environment data and features relevant for their intents from the impact of contexts, gradually completes its model and policy and performs well in more and more contexts. The core of the method is to find outliers in the environment data when the agent experiences an outlier in its intent outcome after the context changes. This method enables the agent to learn continuously in changing contexts without catastrophic forgetting. When pursuing many intents concurrently, the agent may build a transformer-like world model with the method where it factorizes the objects in the world into features, abstract objects into comparable instances of concepts and map concepts to intents through features. The world model is composed of semantic nodes and explainable by nature. I discuss the works needed to render the method practical, the connections to many fields in Artificial Intelligence and the broader implications of the method.

### Paper [393]
Title: LimiX-2: A Contextual Mechanism Network Towards General Structured-Data Intelligence
URL: https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.17488
Authors: Xingxuan Zhang, Gang Ren, Hao Yuan, Hao Zou, Hongze Tan, Hui Wang, Jianhao Song, Jiansheng Li, Jiayao Zhang, Jinghan Zhang, Kaifang Li, Lang Mo, Li Mao, Mingchao Hao, Nuo Xu, Rui Ding, Ruiji Zhang, Shuyang Li, Siyu Mei, Tianyang Zhang, Weiyang Mu, Yancheng Dong, Yongxian Wei, Yuan Xue, Yuanrui Wang, Yue He, Zijia Yang, Ziyun Li, Dongzhe Li, Fuqiang Wang, Jiandong Liu, Jiawei Chen, Jiaxin Du, Kaijie Cheng, Kehan Li, Lei Sun, Linjun Zhou, Ningbo Dai, Qi Wang, Renzhe Xu, Shaoxing Du, Shumeng Yang, Wang Lu, Wenjing Chu, Xiannan Huang, Xiaoyu Lin, Xing Ai, Xinyan Han, Xuanyue Li, Xuanyue Su, Xukun Zhang, Yan Lu, Yaxin Zhang, Yi Qin, Yifei Huang, Yihan Xu, Yongle Lv, Yuanyuan Jiang, Yushan Han, Peng Cui
Abstract: We introduce LimiX-2, a new model in the LimiX family, developed through model and data scaling guided by our previously established scaling laws. LimiX-2 adopts the Contextual Mechanism Networks (CMNs) paradigm and is pretrained with Context-Conditional Masked Modeling (CCMM). CMNs shifts the organizing principle of in-context learning from target-centric prediction to mechanism-oriented joint modeling. Rather than centering the network on the $p(y \mid x, D_{\mathrm{context}})$ objective of conventional tabular PFNs, it is designed around learning $p(x, y \mid D_{\mathrm{context}})$, a context-dependent representation of the joint structure underlying data generation. Pretraining uses synthetic datasets generated by structural causal models (SCMs) spanning diverse graph structures, functional mechanisms, and observation processes. Evaluations on TabArena, TALENT, and BCCO show that LimiX-2 outperforms current dataset-specific models and tabular foundation models. Beyond predictive performance, the CMN paradigm also promotes causal awareness in LimiX-2: its feature attention encodes direct causal relationships, enabling accurate causal skeleton recovery.

### Paper [394]
Title: QVAC Genesis III: A Large-Scale, High-Quality Open Synthetic STEM Corpus for Efficient Language Model Pre-Training
URL: https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.19513
Authors: Davide Vitabile, N. Ranjan, Akshay Nambiar, Kamal K. Gupta, Amril Nazir
Abstract: High-quality pre-training data is a critical bottleneck for educational and STEM-specific language models targeting edge AI and on-device deployment where token budgets are tightly constrained. While major organizations train ever-larger models on private corpora, the open ecosystem lacks STEM-focused synthetic datasets that deliver high per-token learning value efficiently for small models. To address this gap, we introduce QVAC Genesis III, a 191.43B-token, STEM-focused multi-domain synthetic corpus covering 19 domains across several difficulty levels and different educational styles. QVAC Genesis III is built via a dual generation strategy that performs targeted teacher distillation using a weak edge-scale student model as signal: the student's failures are converted into corrective explanations, while its successes are expanded into contrastive option-level reasoning over all answer choices. We further introduce an LLM-as-a-parser evaluation protocol that extracts final answers from free-form outputs and tracks both accuracy and answer validity. To validate the effectiveness of our QVAC Genesis III data, we conduct controlled from-scratch ablations with 1.7B-parameter models, showing that models trained with QVAC Genesis III consistently outperform both models trained with the open-source synthetic corpus Cosmopedia-v2 and the publicly released Cosmo-1B model across ARC, GPQA Diamond, and MMLU STEM benchmarks, achieving up to +28.57% on ARC-E and +21.35% on ARC-C, while reaching a Valid Answer Rate of up to 99.45%.

### Paper [395]
Title: Designer-RSI: Evolving Procedural Memory for Graphic Design Agent
URL: https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.22086
Authors: Hongyang Du, Lan Yan, Christian Flores, Asim Kadav
Abstract: Deployed software agents face a continuous stream of user requests while their foundation-model weights remain frozen between releases. Recursive self-improvement (RSI) via external memory offers a practical path to continual adaptation, yet prior methods largely assume programmatic verifiers, cheap retries, or a single global prompt. Creative software breaks these assumptions, as open-ended visual briefs lack ground truth, VLM judges are noisy, and multi-step tool executions are costly and confounded by external assets. We present Designer-RSI, which evolves a modular SKILL.md skill bank from unlabeled design trajectories for an agent spanning over 230 raster, vector, and layout tools. Designer-RSI couples widening (acquiring new skills for recurring capability gaps) with deepening (revising failing skills by contrasting successful and failed runs), and screens updates through a Pareto-style matched replay gate that requires strict gains on sampled briefs without local regressions. Across held-out product briefs, eight external benchmarks, and two backbones, traffic-driven evolution outperforms static documentation skills, lifts execution success up to 99.8%, improves requirement fulfillment, and reaches up to a 67.6% blind win rate over the base agent. Ablations further show that skill acquisition and revision are strongly synergistic: neither suffices alone, and reliable gains require evolving coverage and execution quality together.

### Paper [396]
Title: Harness-Zero: Harness Distillation via Agent-as-Harness
URL: https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.24974
Authors: Haoran Ye, Yuxing Lu, Haonan Dong, Zhaochen Su, Guojie Song
Abstract: Agent harnesses, the external systems that mediate model-environment interaction, can substantially improve agent performance, but their gains remain tied to the harness at deployment. Because the best harness varies across domains, instances, and models, a general-purpose agent must either settle for a suboptimal shared harness or route among an ever-growing set of specialized ones. We therefore study agent harness distillation: using a domain- or instance-optimized harness as training-time guidance and transferring the behaviors it induces into model weights, so that its gains survive under a single fixed target harness. The challenge is that the two harnesses differ in action space and available information, so guidance from the optimized harness cannot serve directly as supervision for the target one. We introduce Harness-Zero, which enables harness distillation through agent-as-harness. Guided by the optimized harness, a harnessing agent corrects student responses before execution in the target harness's action space, turning harness guidance into training demonstrations. Fine-tuning on the resulting trajectories internalizes harness-induced behavior into the model, so the specialized harness can be removed at deployment. Our experiments spanning knowledge work, tool use, and science domains show that: (1) For frontier LLMs using the same evolved harness, agent-as-harness outperforms code-as-harness. (2) With the specialized harness removed at deployment, Harness-Zero improves the base model's macro-average task success from 23.3% to 44.3%, even exceeding the 41.7% it reaches with that harness still attached, while reducing inference token usage by 27%-86%. (3) Harness-Zero recovers harness-induced behaviors absent from the base model, with 82.3% average recovery across 28 patterns in the three domains.

### Paper [397]
Title: Evaluating Real-Time Voice Agents: A Survey from Component Quality to Grounded Outcomes
URL: https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.30798
Authors: Shivam Negi, Arpit Rawat, Rashi Jain
Abstract: Real-time voice agents must decide when to speak, recover from disruption, and act only when the conversation requires it. But currently their evaluation is fragmented and is split across speech quality, turn-taking, tool calling, and task completion. We review 136 primary works published up to October 2, 2026, and code each on two axes: the capability an evaluation exercises, namely timing, recovery, grounded outcome, and, where relevant, multiparty conduct; and the strength of its evidence, namely evaluation conditions and grading instrument. The coding locates the gaps. Only 13 of 91 evaluation works check external state or action traces, only 5 of 21 grounded-outcome benchmarks use real human speech, and only 2 of 26 multiparty benchmarks verify state. Three claims follow. Architecture constrains deployment but does not determine results. It has been observed that benchmarks can verify outcomes, but few do. Evaluating an agent solely through one-on-one user interaction leaves critical gaps: it cannot confirm if the agent is speaking to the correct person, verify that person's authority, or identify the proper moments for strategic silence. From the taxonomy we derive TRG, short for Timing-Recovery-Grounded, a minimum reporting standard, and apply it to three published reports with different architectures. In one, tool-selection F1 is 82.5% while exact-call success is 33.0%, with no evidence of external task state. None of the three reports measures task state after an interruption. TRG governs what is reported and assigns no composite score. Per-source codes and tooling accompany the submission.

### Paper [398]
Title: DeepEdu-F: A Framework for Efficient and Scalable Agentic LLMs in Vietnamese Education
URL: https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.31568
Authors: Quang Nguyen, Hieu Nguyen, Toan Pham, Hien Hoang, Cong Tran, Nam Vu
Abstract: Cloud AI assistants send student data to foreign servers, conflicting with data-sovereignty laws such as Vietnam's Decree 53, and are not grounded in the national curriculum, so they often hallucinate on local content. Self-hosting an open model keeps data on-premise but faces two costs that weight quantization does not remove. An AI tutoring session revisits the same long material every turn, so once prefix caching removes the prefill cost, decoding over a growing KV Cache dominates response time; and grounding the tutor in local knowledge by fine-tuning is too costly to repeat as that knowledge changes. We present DeepEdu-F, a framework for the Vietnamese AI tutor DeepEdu with two components. LastSet selects the critical KV Cache entries once per turn from the question's attention, decodes the whole turn from that set, and keeps the full cache so later questions can select again. REPAIR (Reliable Evidence-based Playbook Adaptation and Insight Revision) curates a verified playbook from past interactions instead of updating weights. LastSet decodes up to 3.1x faster than dense decoding at a 256k-token context, faster than four sparse baselines, with accuracy close to full attention. Compared with ACE (ADD), REPAIR raises final accuracy from 70.0% to 80.5% on Formula and from 53.6% to 59.7% on FiNER, while mean task/scenario goal completion across AppWorld's normal and challenge splits increases from 9.28% to 16.28%.

### Paper [399]
Title: One Latent, Many Tokens: Jointly Learning Compressed Embeddings for Efficient Language Diffusion
URL: https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.33698
Authors: Yulin Yuan, Ying Zhang, Xiangming Meng
Abstract: Most continuous diffusion language models process one latent position per token at each sampling step, making generation expensive. Two-stage methods lower the cost by reducing the latent length, but they fix the compressed embedding space before training the diffusion model. Embeddings from the fixed space can be difficult to model with diffusion and decode reliably into tokens, which limits generation quality after compression. To address this problem, we introduce JPEG-DLM (Joint-embedding Prediction for Efficient Generation with Diffusion Language Model), which jointly trains a compressor, a flow matching model and a decoding module. With joint-embedding prediction, JPEG-DLM learns compressed embeddings that are more structured, easier to model with diffusion and reliably decodable into tokens.JPEG-DLM achieves the highest throughput among recent diffusion and flow models on LM1B and OWT, with a Gen-PPL lower than most of them. At a compression rate of 0.5 on OWT, it reaches a Gen-PPL of 34.52 and approximately 2.3 times ELF's throughput. These results suggest that jointly learning compressed embeddings offers a promising path toward efficient diffusion language modeling.Code is available at https://github.com/infiniteYuanyl/JPEG-DLM.

### Paper [400]
Title: A General Harness for Protein Foundation Model Fitness Prediction
URL: https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.34654
Authors: Yang Tan, Qijia Tian, Gangyu Sun, Bozitao Zhong, Mingchen Li, Yuanxi Yu, Nanqing Dong, Liang Hong
Abstract: Accurate fitness prediction is central to protein engineering and understanding sequence-function relationships. With advances in deep learning, protein foundation models (PFMs) have become widely used for this task. Recent analyses, however, show that these models share preferences reflecting their training corpora, while unreliable inputs can further distort fitness predictions. Family-specific evolutionary evidence and structural context can help address these limitations by providing complementary constraints on model scores, motivating VenusREM-Harness (VRH), a general, model-agnostic, training-free Retrieval-Enhanced Mutation harness. It fuses frozen model scores with multiple sequence alignment (MSA) evidence according to model uncertainty, then applies gated background correction and score shrinkage based on structural confidence and solvent exposure. Across 1,211 assays and 3.1 million measured variants from ProteinGym, VenusMutHub, and the newly curated viral benchmark VenusViroHub, all 71 configurations improve Spearman correlation on all 3 benchmarks by 0.073 on average, with broad gains across 5 metrics. Extended analyses relate retrieval gains to model-MSA preference differences, assess domain-level gains and immune-escape cases, and quantify computational speedups. Built with VRH, VenusREM2 is the first to rank highest in all function, taxon, MSA-depth, and mutation-depth categories, with a ProteinGym Average Spearman of 0.556, 0.038 above the prior best.

### Paper [401]
Title: UpliftMem: Learning Set-Level Uplift for Agent Memory Retrieval
URL: https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.36805
Authors: Mengkun Liang, Haoran Qiang, Guannan Liu, Junjie Wu
Abstract: Large language model (LLM) agents draw on external memory to guide new tasks, raising the question of which memories to retrieve together to improve execution. Learning set values requires outcome feedback, but evaluating each untested alternative costs an additional rollout. We introduce \textsc{UpliftMem}, which learns set-level execution uplift relative to the same executor without memory. Our analysis of how retrieval preferences limit feedback coverage motivates targeted probes. Under a correlated Gaussian surrogate, we derive a closed-form expected value of sample information (EVSI) criterion that prioritizes probes by their expected benefit to local retrieval decisions. The feedback trains a shared scorer with the executor frozen, and inference requires no probing. Across ALFWorld, WebShop, and BigCodeBench, \textsc{UpliftMem} achieves best or joint-best success rates among evaluated methods. On fixed-store MemSyco-Bench evaluations, decision accuracy increases from 41.7\% to 66.5\% with Qwen3-4B and from 44.4\% to 68.8\% with Qwen3-8B. At matched probe budgets, EVSI improves decision accuracy over random probing by 10.6 and 13.9 percentage points, respectively.

### Paper [402]
Title: Can Computation from Earlier Problems Help LLMs Solve New Ones?
URL: https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.39394
Authors: Jipei He, Wenhui Tan, Xiaoyi Yu, Enver Sangineto, Fiorenzo Parascandolo, Rita Cucchiara, Ruihua Song
Abstract: Large language models often solve independent problems in the same conversation. Can computation from earlier problems help them solve new ones? Preliminary experiments show that retained history can raise or lower later-turn accuracy, even within the same domain. To trace this effect, we hold each problem fixed and vary its preceding history. The resulting changes in the model's internal states contain a recurring, problem-dependent component. To improve reasoning with retained history, we introduce STAIR (Stale-Token Attention for Inter-query Reuse). It captures attention keys and values from earlier response generation in a read-only bank. STAIR learns to reflect current query vectors to redirect attention over this bank during prompt processing. The backbone and stored entries remain fixed; only 12,288 parameters are trained. Across three Qwen models and four reasoning benchmarks, STAIR improves average later-turn accuracy by up to 11.67 percentage points over the unmodified model with history. The math-trained Qwen3.5-9B controller also improves code generation on HumanEval+ without further training.

### Paper [403]
Title: MultiWorldBench: Do Independently Controlled Views Describe One Shared World?
URL: https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.11723
Authors: Zhangbo Xu, Ruoxi Zhang, Rui Hu, Yisong Wang, Xitong Li, Jingdong Wang
Abstract: Multiplayer world models must ensure that independently controlled views remain consistent with a single shared and persistent world. We introduce MultiWorldBench, a diagnostic Minecraft benchmark comprising 495 case configurations across seven task suites and ten capabilities, including independent control, cross-view motion, shared-state synchronization, persistence, structural reasoning, concurrent interaction, and delayed revisit. Using Engine GT as a reference, we evaluate two multiplayer world models, Solaris (MP) and Gamma-World, together with a single-player generation variant, Solaris (SP). Among the generated systems, Gamma-World achieves the highest ten-capability average at 21.39, followed by Solaris (MP) at 20.88 and Solaris (SP) at 10.06, while Engine GT reaches 91.69. Gamma-World performs better on several control, shared-state, and revisit capabilities, whereas Solaris (MP) leads in cross-view motion and race-condition consistency. Nevertheless, all generated systems score at most 8.00 on state persistence and 1.33 on structural consistency, and none succeeds in spatial reasoning or building-identity preservation. Human pairwise evaluation yields the same overall system ranking and shows strong alignment with the automatic evaluation, with a mean dimension-level Spearman correlation of 0.95. These results show that current multiplayer world models still exhibit a substantial gap relative to Engine GT in maintaining a coherent shared world.

### Paper [404]
Title: Releasing the Inner Jev: A Simple Recipe for Open Decision Models
URL: https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.17205
Authors: Jonathan Geuter, Andrew Cheng, David Alvarez-Melis
Abstract: LLM-based decision-making systems, also called System One models, have recently gained popularity, most notably through Jev (Almeida, 2026). Given a typed input state, a question, and a set of options, they return a calibrated probability for each option in a single forward pass, without text generation. We show that open instruction-tuned LLMs can achieve performance comparable to Jev without any additional training, by reading out answers from letters (A, B, C, etc.) in the model's output logits at the last token position. Next, we propose a simple supervised LoRA fine-tuning recipe that can improve their performance further. We evaluate four instruction-tuned LLMs: Gemma 4 31B, Gemma 4 12B, Qwen3.6-27B, and Qwen3.5-4B. Furthermore, we introduce OpenDecisions, a novel 2,352-question validation dataset designed to reflect the task composition of JevBench (JevBench maintainers, 2026) and which computes a Capability score based on accuracy and calibration. Without fine-tuning, Gemma 4 31B scores 79.6 on OpenDecisions, compared to Jev's 80.1. After fine-tuning, it scores 89.0, outperforming Jev by 8.9 Capability points and on par with the strongest open decision model we evaluated. The same recipe improves the three other models by 7.8 to 15.7 points. On a suite of public decision datasets, our models achieve accuracy and calibration comparable to Jev. Our results show that open LLMs already possess System One capabilities, and with simple fine-tuning they outperform Jev on a wide range of decision tasks. We release our models, training and serving code, OpenDecisions, and DecisionBank, our training dataset consisting of 261,561 decisions.

### Paper [405]
Title: ResearchFlow-Eval: Tracing the Execution Gap in End-to-End Multi-Claim Autoresearch
URL: https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.18088
Authors: Peng Jiang, Dongyang Li, Quanying Liu, Xiaoxuan Jia
Abstract: Autoresearch systems increasingly undertake entire research projects. In practice, scientific research requires systems to pursue multiple interdependent claims through a continuous, multi-stage workflow and revise experiments as evidence accumulates. Existing evaluations of autoresearch systems, however, typically score isolated tasks or final artifacts, leaving these process-level capabilities largely unmeasured. We therefore introduce ResearchFlow-Eval, a fine-grained framework for assessing continuous scientific execution and iteration at both the stage and claim levels. Each evaluation is grounded in a published anchor paper, from which we derive stage-specific rubrics that decompose each research stage into dimensions and separately scored rubric items. Using these rubrics, we score each research stage and examine experimental execution in detail, focusing on multi-claim completion and revisions across attempts. We apply the evaluation framework to general-purpose agent harnesses, skill-augmented harnesses, and systems with research-specific orchestration. Averaged over two runs per system, each scored by two LLM judges, experimental execution lags behind design and planning for every evaluated system. Within execution, systems struggle to produce high-quality results and meet claim requirements, and repeated attempts rarely yield measurable improvements. Moreover, neither skill augmentation nor research-specific orchestration reliably yields higher scores. ResearchFlow-Eval thus extends autoresearch evaluation beyond isolated, single-objective runs to continuous workflows in which systems pursue multiple claims and refine their work across iterations.

### Paper [406]
Title: What the Trace Shows and What the Label Says: Auditing Behavioral Detectors in Agent Evaluation
URL: https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.30491
Authors: Idunnuoluwa A. Adeniji,  Shilpika, Michael E. Papka
Abstract: LLM agents are often evaluated by reducing execution traces to labels such as final-answer correctness, tool verification, and fault propagation. These labels are measurements produced by parsers, behavioral detectors, and harness rules, yet evaluations rarely report whether a measurement actually fits the trace it describes. We study what happens when those measurements do not fully capture the trace's behavior. Agents solve GSM8K problems with a calculator whose outputs are occasionally corrupted, which gives ground truth for what the tool returned, what the model later used, and what the detectors reported; a chemistry task with search and retrieval tests the case where the model cannot recompute the tool's output. Across eight models and up to 1,000 traces per model, final correctness, visible re-verification, and fault propagation disagree in systematic ways. The most common gap is the silent tool-output override: the model replaces a corrupted value without calling the tool again, so the error stops propagating while a verification detector records no re-check. Human review finds verification labels the least reliable (\k{appa} =0.29), and checking depends on the task: the same calculator that several models re-checked on arithmetic was never re-checked on chemistry. Execution limits and serving failures also change what is measured. These findings motivate measurement transparency: an automated label should be reported with its evidence, whether the detector's question applied, whether the run was censored, and any human corrections. Our trace viewer makes this information inspectable alongside the reported results.

### Paper [407]
Title: Noise Steering: Exploiting Wake Steering and Acoustic Directivity for Noise-Constrained Wind Farm Control via Reinforcement Learning
URL: https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.36888
Authors: Mart\'in de Frutos, Baptiste Corban, Claire Bizon Monroc, Ana Bu\v{s}i\'c, Donatien Dubuc, Jiamin Zhu, Esteban Ferrer
Abstract: Existing noise mitigation strategies for wind farms usually consist in reducing turbines power output, without accounting for the wake interactions between them and the noise emission directivity. This leads to over-conservative policies and unexploited wind power. Yet, turbine yaw can simultaneously affect wake interactions and the direction of noise emission, offering an additional degree of freedom for farm-level noise control. We therefore propose Noise Steering, a model-free reinforcement learning (RL) strategy that jointly controls turbine yaw angles and power setpoints to maximise farm power while satisfying a local noise constraint in a defined region. To enable training at a reduced computational cost, a low-fidelity acoustic model is coupled with the steady-state FLORIS simulator. The RL agent effectively learns how to redirect wakes and noise directivity away from the protected region while redistributing power across the farm. On a six-turbine test farm, the policy satisfies the 40 dB noise constraint throughout the full range of turbulent winds, with an average power deficit of only 2% relative to greedy unimpeded control, and up to 7.7% at high wind speeds. In comparison, the industry-standard noise-reduced operation (NRO) baseline here generates an average power loss of 17%. When deployed in the higher-fidelity FASTFarm simulator, the policy largely retains its performance under steady and turbulent inflow, despite differences in simulation fidelity. These results demonstrate the potential of combining yaw control and power derating through RL to improve the power-noise trade-off in wind farm operation.

### Paper [408]
Title: The Real Cost of Context: Predicting Compute Time and Energy Consumption in LLM Prefill and Decode Phases
URL: https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.39621
Authors: Lars Krupp, Daniel Gei{\ss}ler, Francisco M. Calatrava-Nicolas, Stefan Gerd Fritsch, Paul Lukowicz, Jakob Karolus
Abstract: The integration of Large Language Models (LLMs) into agentic architectures has substantially increased token consumption, raising concerns about their energy footprint. Yet, estimating the energy consumption of proprietary API-based models remains challenging, and existing approaches focus primarily on generated output tokens while overlooking the impact of the large input contexts characteristic of agentic workflows. To address this gap, we systematically profile compute time and energy consumption across a wide range of prefill (input) and decode (output) token lengths. Using generalized linear models, we show that decode compute time and energy scale linearly with output length, whereas prefill compute time and energy exhibit both linear and quadratic scaling with input length. These findings demonstrate that input context becomes a relevant factor for large-context workloads. Furthermore, we show that compute time serves as a reliable proxy for energy consumption when direct energy measurements are unavailable. Our mapping framework enables precise energy estimation and supports more sustainable deployment decisions for agentic AI.

### Paper [409]
Title: An Augmented Lagrangian Approach to Lifted Bellman Linear Programming
URL: https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.14183
Authors: Jongchan Park, Hyukjun Yang, Narim Jeong, Donghwan Lee
Abstract: Temporal-difference critics regress onto a bootstrapped target and usually need a target network. Lifted Bellman Linear Programming (LBLP) instead expresses the in-sample optimal values of offline RL as the solution to a linear program over the dataset. Its neural solver ALBUM relaxes the constraints into hinge penalties with fixed coefficients, and its guarantees are conditions on coefficients chosen in advance. We solve the same program with the method of multipliers: the coefficients become multipliers learned by dual ascent, with one penalty parameter $\rho$. In the convex case the fixed points are the KKT pairs of LBLP, and at every stationary point the applied constraint weights balance the objective exactly. The critic can serve a Gaussian actor, a flow actor, and best-of-N sampling, and for the Gaussian actor we propose an advantage-normalized DDPG+BC objective. With a single $Q$ and $V$ and no target network, our AL-LBLP with best-of-N reaches 60.1\% average success on the 50 state-based OGBench tasks. On long-horizon tasks, it reaches 81.5% on humanoidmaze-giant and 79.8% on antmaze-giant. It does not collapse up to batch size 8192.

### Paper [410]
Title: Small Heads, Strong Decisions: A Controlled Study of Typed-Decision Models
URL: https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.15592
Authors: Ritvik Rastogi
Abstract: In September 2026, TypeSafe announced Jev as the first model in its System One category: unstructured state goes in, and typed, calibrated decisions that software can use directly come out. Jev's architecture and training data are unpublished, but many open typed-decision models have followed. They answer structured questions about a piece of text, such as a yes/no check, a choice among labelled options or a graded score, by reading the text with a language model and scoring the options with a small readout. Each pairs a different readout with a different backbone, training mix and recipe, so it is unclear which design choices matter. We re-implement five readouts and train all of them under one protocol: the same backbone, the same 15 public English training sources, the same recipe with a per-head learning-rate sweep, and the same seed. Our experiments demonstrate that: The complexity of the head does not affect accuracy: on Qwen3-0.6B every readout reaches 81.3-82.0% in-domain accuracy, and a 0.5M-parameter cosine head, which scores each option by its cosine similarity to the question, matches the 27M-parameter joint schema head adapted from Clef in-domain at half its inference time. Reading all of a text's questions from one prompt also matches one prompt per question in accuracy, with 1.6 times faster inference and 2.1-2.5 times faster training on one H100. The backbone's size matters more than either choice: moving from Qwen3-0.6B to Qwen3-4B adds 4 points in-domain and 11 points on held-out sources, while the base, instruct and thinking checkpoints of the same model tie. Checkpoints trained further for embedding or reranking bring no gain. We recommend spending effort on the backbone and keeping the head minimal.

### Paper [411]
Title: Classical Energies Learn to Reason 1,000x Faster than Looped Models
URL: https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.17309
Authors: Benjamin Hoover, Hugh Blayney, James C. R. Whittington
Abstract: Looped models have emerged as a prominent paradigm for reasoning, with successes on simple reasoning puzzles such as Sudoku-Extreme and Maze-Unique. However, these models can be unstable, slow to train, and use many parameters and FLOPs to solve simple puzzles. Descent on an energy not only mitigates these issues but also offers advantages: built-in early stopping, compute that adapts to problem difficulty, fast training without differentiating through a solver, and interpretable weights that learn the constraints of the puzzle. Here we demonstrate this with a learned version of Hopfield & Tank's energy from the 80s, which we term CRAM, solves 99.996% of Sudoku-Extreme and 100% of Maze-Unique test puzzles after 42 ms and 132 ms of training on one GPU, at least 1,000x faster than looped models on these tasks. CRAM also matches baseline looped models at their highest accuracy on Sudoku-Extreme using at least 282x fewer inference FLOPs. Our work suggests two things. First, that energies are an extremely promising direction for training reasoning from data. Second, a caution against using trivially solvable benchmarks like Sudoku and Maze to test the scalability of looped reasoning techniques.

### Paper [412]
Title: Imitation Bootstrapped Multi-Agent Reinforcement Learning
URL: https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.18543
Authors: Tony H. Zhang, Connor Mattson, Daniel S. Brown
Abstract: Demonstrations are an effective way to accelerate single-agent reinforcement learning (RL), but extending this idea to multi-agent reinforcement learning (MARL) is non-trivial as it is difficult and often impossible for a single person to teleoperate multiple agents simultaneously. Round-Robin Behavior Cloning (R2BC) addresses this by letting a single demonstrator control one agent at a time while the others execute their current policies. However, the resulting imitation policies are limited by the quality of the demonstrations. We propose Imitation Bootstrapped Multi-Agent Reinforcement Learning (IBMARL), a framework that extends Imitation Bootstrapped Reinforcement Learning (IBRL) to cooperative teams and uses R2BC to train the imitation policies. At each time step, every agent receives one action proposal from its imitation policy and one from its RL policy, and an ensemble of centralized critics selects among the per-agent combinations of the two. A gated imitation penalty, a soft Q-filtered behavior cloning loss, regularizes each RL policy toward its imitation policy only where the critics value the imitation action more, so that after training each agent executes its RL policy alone, without the centralized critics. On four cooperative multi-agent RL benchmarks, IBMARL surpasses its imitation policies $2.6$-$4.2\times$ faster than demonstration-based baselines on two tasks, is the only method to surpass it on the other two, and exceeds the demonstrator itself on two of the four. With real human demonstrations IBMARL still surpasses imitation learning alone, and does so more than twice as fast as the best baseline.

### Paper [413]
Title: How to make a moral agent: sentimentalism and rationalism as lenses on AI
URL: https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.18714
Authors: David Gottlieb, Jared Moore
Abstract: Can AI systems be moral agents? That is, can they undertake actions that can be correctly assessed as morally right or wrong? There is no settled normative criterion of moral agency that applies neatly outside paradigm human cases. Because of the difficulties with a normative account, we instead propose a research program to explore descriptive moral agency. Most basically, we ask, what are the capabilities we humans possess that underlie our moral agency? To do so, we draw on historical scholarship in philosophy and moral psychology to isolate candidate capabilities. Then we suggest refining candidate capabilities so that they are testable in the context of AI systems. Finally, we offer specific empirical recommendations to test AI systems, especially large language models (LLMs), for those refined capabilities. Our approach offers several benefits. Firstly, it may demonstrate whether the behaviors of AI systems are aligned, or in a broad sense desirable. Secondly, the debate may inform policy questions (e.g., what models should be deployed in which contexts?). Thirdly, our approach may complement engineering and design efforts: when capabilities can be precisely characterized, they can inform the development of models. Fourthly, applying our moral reasoning concepts in the new context of AI systems should improve our understanding of those concepts.

### Paper [414]
Title: Controllable Chains of Thought Can Reveal Less, Even Without Evasion Training
URL: https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.21799
Authors: Zheng-Xin Yong, Benjamin Arnav, Miles Wang, Tomek Korbak, Olivia Watkins
Abstract: Chain-of-thought (CoT) monitoring is a promising tool for detecting hidden goals, off-task behavior, and other failures in AI agents. One risk is that models could deliberately shape their reasoning to make it opaque to monitors. We stress-test this risk through persona-control training: supervised fine-tuning on mathematical reasoning traces rewritten in the voices of diverse personas. This induces a broader ability to control CoT, even though training contains no monitor-evasion examples. In covert side-task evaluations, traces in which a gpt-oss-20b model organism completes the hidden side task contain less monitorable evidence than the base model's. Averaged across evaluations and CoT lengths, weighted by side-task-success counts, the CoT monitorability metric g-mean$^2$ falls from 0.94 for the base model to 0.68 for the model organism without a hiding instruction, and to 0.22 when explicitly instructed to hide. In intervention evaluations, explicit hiding often drives monitorability near zero. The effect extends to a Kimi-K2.6 model organism in agentic environments, though hidden objectives requiring extended reasoning or longer trajectories remain more monitorable than simple single-step side tasks. We further discover that targeted transparency prompts can substantially recover monitorability, sometimes surpassing the base model. These model organisms provide a testbed for studying monitorability failures and potential mitigations. Our code can be found on https://github.com/yongzx/cot-control-model-organism.

### Paper [415]
Title: Auditors Struggle to Catch Sabotage In End-to-End Transcripts of AI Safety Research
URL: https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.21832
Authors: Keshav Shenoy, Anthony Zang, Eric Gan, Jacqueline Tay, Amy Ngo, Sebastian Prasanna, Julian Stastny, Vivek Hebbar
Abstract: As AI systems are increasingly used to automate AI R&amp;D, misaligned systems could sabotage research intended to improve model safety and alignment. To stress-test defenses against such sabotage, we use a human expert as a proxy saboteur on AI safety research. We conduct a red-team/blue-team game where a human researcher runs AI safety experiments from scratch and auditors try to tell which were sabotaged and how. Given access to the full research transcript and the ability to run experiments, human auditors mostly fail to distinguish sabotaged from benign research: they achieve an AUROC of 0.59, not significantly better than chance, and fully identify the specific sabotage in only 17% of cases. Despite not being allowed to run experiments, the best frontier models likely outperform human auditors (Fable 5.1: AUROC 0.73, 32% full identification), while weaker models score near or below them. When the saboteur chooses which experiments are attacked, rather than being required to sabotage every question given, sabotage identification drops further. Our results suggest that research sabotage by expert-level agents is difficult to detect with current auditing methods and provide a new setting for studying research sabotage.

### Paper [416]
Title: Think Once, Act Fast: Cost-Effective Recursive Self-Improvement for Decision Model Harness
URL: https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.22432
Authors: Siqi Lai, Haitao Li, Hao Liu
Abstract: Fast System-1 models enable responsive actions but struggle with complex or unfamiliar tasks. Large language model (LLM)-based System-2 provides strong reasoning capabilities, yet repeated invocation incurs substantial latency and computational costs. To bridge this gap, we propose JevRSI, a System-1/System-2 collaboration framework that transforms LLM reasoning into transferable decision support through recursive harness improvement. A Jev-style System-1 model enables low-latency action selection, while an asynchronous System-2 LLM develops a modular harness. The harness integrates procedures, state memory, and world models to provide decision contexts spanning the past, present, and future. Utility-driven retrieval selects modules based on both contextual relevance and their utility on previous tasks. Environmental feedback guides targeted revisions and enables recursive self-improvement. Experiments across three datasets show that JevRSI consistently improves decision quality, matching or surpassing LLM agents in several settings. It preserves low-latency execution with favorable performance-cost trade-offs. Moreover, System-2 development interventions decrease as the harness evolves, amortizing System-2 costs through continuous improvement. Our project is available at https://github.com/usail-hkust/JevRSI.

### Paper [417]
Title: Born for the Task: Rethinking Quantum Chemistry with Foresighted Agentic Computing
URL: https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.22897
Authors: Haozhi Han, Junjie Yang, Ruge Zhang, Zihe Huang, Pengyi Xu, Bo Zhang, Xianyi Zhang, Yunquan Zhang, Ting Cao, Yunxin Liu, Yifeng Chen, Kun Li
Abstract: Quantum chemistry relies on general-purpose software, whose generality leaves many large-scale calculations beyond practical resource budgets. Coding agents make task-specific programs cheap to write, but not efficient ones easy to find: whether a computational strategy is accurate and fast enough becomes clear only after it actually runs at the target scale. We introduce MakoBORN, a foresighted agentic computing framework that synthesizes high performance quantum chemistry programs on demand. MakoBORN anticipates the behavior of each strategy before execution, extrapolating its error from representative small systems and predicting its runtime from the target-scale workload and measured hardware throughputs. Building on these predictions, MakoBORN unfolds the preset schemes of existing packages into independently configurable algorithmic components, selects the fastest strategy within the error tolerance from the predicted accuracy-runtime Pareto frontier, and guides coding agents to compile it with component-level performance predictions. For a Hartree-Fock calculation with 50,024 basis functions, MakoBORN finishes in 1.41 h on a single CPU node, 77$\times$ faster than the estimated runtime of a general-purpose baseline and still 9.8$\times$ faster with development time included. On a single GPU, MakoBORN completes the ubiquitin calculation in 2.1 h, the same order of runtime as the GPU-accelerated package Mako on 64 GPUs, and shortens the B-DNA duplex calculation from an estimated two months to 8.1 h (173$\times$).

### Paper [418]
Title: PhysScope: Agentic Multimodal Physics Reasoning with Grounded Visual Memory
URL: https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.23010
Authors: Haoyang Liu, Kecheng Liang, Xinyu Li, Weixing Chen, Yang Liu
Abstract: Vision-language models (VLMs) can correctly interpret physics diagrams yet apply constraints outside the states or conditions in which they hold, causing premise errors to propagate through subsequent calculations. We introduce PhysScope, an inference-time visual harness that supports self-correction of these scope mismatches without updating model weights. PhysScope enables the model to link physical scopes directly to its visual observations and explicitly constructs derived views to support reasoning. The harness imposes explicit constraints on state distinctions, conditions, and physical scopes during model reasoning, and checks agent-proposed revisions against sources and scopes. Accepted revisions invalidate registered dependents, thereby maintaining scope-aware working memory. On PhysAlign, PhysScope achieves higher solving scores than direct solving across all six evaluated VLMs. With Gemini-3.8-flash, it outperforms Canvas-CoT by 9.34 percentage points and substantially improves solving accuracy on the out-of-distribution (OOD) PhysUniBench benchmark. Ablation studies support explicit scope management and dependency maintenance as design principles for multimodal physics reasoning agents.

### Paper [419]
Title: Why Sub-4-Bit Vision CNNs Collapse and Where Mixed-Precision Recovery Stops
URL: https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.23445
Authors: Michael Schroeder
Abstract: Deep vision models operating in resource-constrained environments depend heavily on low-bit quantization, yet compressing weights below 4 bits frequently induces catastrophic accuracy collapse. While mixed-precision recovery strategies aim to rescue accuracy by selectively retaining high-precision parameters, the fundamental mechanics of where and why these models collapse remain poorly understood. In this study, we systematically analyze the anatomy of quantization collapse across standard Vision CNNs (including ResNet and EfficientNet backbones) evaluated on the full 50,000-image ImageNet-1k validation set. First, we map the exact progression of degradation, demonstrating that collapse is highly non-linear and concentrated in late-stage pointwise projections and depthwise layers. Second, we introduce a structured, multi-step recovery waterfall that evaluates selective integer precision (INT4/INT8) and selective floating-point scales. We show that keeping less than 5% of critical layer activations in 8-bit precision recovers up to 92% of the accuracy lost during aggressive sub-4-bit compression. Finally, we bridge the hardware reality gap by evaluating deployment performance on standard ARM and x86 CPU architectures. We show that while low-bit quantization reduces memory storage requirements, standard runtime software bit-unpacking introduces a 1.48x to 21.5x execution slowdown depending on batch size, highlighting a critical trade-off between model footprint and actual edge throughput. Our findings provide a practical blueprint for navigating the limits of sub-4-bit deployment without sacrificing hardware performance.

### Paper [420]
Title: FedE2E: One-Shot Federated Learning for End-to-End Autonomous Driving with Closed-Loop Evaluation
URL: https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.24254
Authors: Tao Lian, Antonio M. L\'opez
Abstract: Federated learning enables collaborative model training without sharing datasets. However, existing federated learning methods for autonomous driving typically require multi-round communication and lack closed-loop evaluation, while one-shot methods mainly focus on perception tasks. To address these limitations, we propose \method, a one-shot federated learning method for end-to-end autonomous driving. It comprises multi-client calibration, anchor-based feature alignment, and token-wise consensus distillation to distill knowledge from heterogeneous models. Closed-loop evaluation on Bench2Drive demonstrates the effectiveness of \method in both one-shot and multi-round federated learning. In the one-shot setting, it achieves a Driving Score of 55.85, which is 4.07 points higher than prior work. In the multi-round setting, it reaches 60.35 after five communication rounds, the best performance among all compared methods under the same number of communication rounds.

### Paper [421]
Title: Self-Rewarded Reinforcement Learning for Better-Structured Medical Explanations in Small Language Models
URL: https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.24650
Authors: Prakhar Mishra, Bhavya Yajush Awasthi,  Tanuja,  Nishant
Abstract: Small language models (SLMs) can run on commodity hardware and support privacy-preserving, on-premises clinical deployment, but their medical explanations are often short and poorly structured compared with large language models. Recent supervised approaches such as Meerkat transfer chain-of-thought reasoning into 7B-8B backbones, yet their training objective does not directly target the style or organization of the generated rationales. Building on the instruction-tuned Mistral-7B backbone, we investigate whether a lightweight reinforcement learning (RL) step can improve explanation structure without human preference labels. We apply Proximal Policy Optimization (PPO) with LoRA adaptation to 2000 Meerkat-derived medical prompts, guided by a simple self-supervised reward that favors longer, lexically diverse, and well-punctuated responses while penalizing very short ones. Training for about 170 PPO steps on a single NVIDIA A100 GPU yields a stable increase in the average reward. Quantitative and qualitative comparisons show that the RL-tuned model produces longer, more organized, multi-point explanations while preserving the underlying medical content. We discuss the promise and the limitations of self-rewarded RL for shaping the style of medical explanations.

### Paper [422]
Title: WorldRuler: Interactive World Modeling under Configurable Rules
URL: https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.24869
Authors: Mingliang Zhai, Chuanhao Li, Jiaming Tan, Zhen Li, Yuwei Wu, Yunde Jia, Zian Meng, Yuanyang Yin, Kaipeng Zhang
Abstract: Rules govern how player actions change interactive worlds. Different rules create distinct gameplay experiences within similar scenes and action spaces. For customizable gameplay, generative world models must follow user-defined rules beyond real-world behaviors. However, realizing user-defined rules presents two challenges: Rule-State Persistence and Rule-to-Visual Realization. The former requires preserving rule definitions, dependencies, and world states across saving and loading, so that restored worlds follow the established rules. The latter requires accurately realizing rule-induced changes on designated objects and attributes, particularly when affected objects are unseen. In this paper, we propose WorldRuler, which organizes rule definitions, dependencies, and object states in an executable 3D rule graph and connects rule execution, video generation, and perceptual feedback in a closed loop. The graph supports joint saving and restoration of rules and world states, while dynamic object binding grounds rule-specified entities in visual instances and uses stored states to constrain generation from their first appearance. We further introduce object attribute state replacement and counterfactual reconstruction training, enabling accurate attribute control and compatible appearance and motion changes while limiting interference with unrelated content. We develop an automated data collection and annotation pipeline using GTA-V and Habitat to construct RuleVerse, a dataset of rule-driven interactions with detailed state and event annotations, including cases where rules take effect before affected objects become visible. Extensive experiments demonstrate the effectiveness of WorldRuler in preserving rule-state consistency across saving and loading and accurately realizing rule outcomes on designated objects and attributes.

### Paper [423]
Title: Ensemble Logic
URL: https://arxiv.org/abs/2408.14443
Authors: Guo-Qiang Zhang
Abstract: Ensemble Logic (EL) is a family of modal logics whose modal parameters are terms with quantified variables. We interpret formulas over metric monoids, that is, commutative monoids equipped with translation-invariant metrics. Displacement evaluates a formula at a translated point, while bounded existence and bounded universality evaluate it at some or every point of a window of prescribed length. Quantification over points and lengths allows the same variable to occur in the parameters of several operators. This provides a common framework for temporal and spatial reasoning in discrete, dense and multi-dimensional settings. Our main result establishes a sharp undecidability boundary for satisfiability over the natural numbers: satisfiability is undecidable already for formulas $\exists N\,\exists n\,\Psi$ in which $\Psi$ is built from the letters of a fixed alphabet by conjunction, disjunction, displacement and bounded universality. We prove this by a reduction from Post's Correspondence Problem. We also present results, in part from companion work, that place this theorem in the general theory of EL. These include an atomic-displacement normal form for covariant windows and composition laws for discrete and dense time.
