Across four split backbones, ReBA turns fragile single-ratio balance into stable balance across resolutions, tilings, and image counts without hurting accuracy. Large modality gaps in released native MoEs also show its promise for native post-training. Code is available at https://github.com/ZiangWu-77/ReBA.

### Paper [470]
Title: Omni-modal decomposition autoencoders learn full-stack wearable disentangled representations
URL: https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.07385
Authors: Ioannis Ziogas, Ensieh Khazaei, Bilal Taha, Aamna Al Shehhi, Ahsan H. Khandoker, Leontios J. Hadjileontiadis, Dimitrios Hatzinakos
Abstract: Learning disentangled representations is key for general-purpose multi-modal wearable models, yet existing approaches rarely combine classification, disentanglement, fusion, and generation of heterogeneous multi-modal time series in a single model. We introduce Omni-modal Variational Decomposition Autoencoders (OmniDecVAEs), a framework that learns multi-purpose representations from arbitrarily many modalities with a single shared encoder-decoder. OmniDecVAEs extend DecVAEs by learning time-frequency latent subspaces per modality through a multi-view self-supervised decomposition loss and a component-wise conditional decoder. On the omni-modal human activity recognition (HAR) HARWE dataset (up to thirty channels) and the public UCI HAR and WESAD datasets, OmniDecVAEs learn the most disentangled representations for unseen subjects (DCI disentanglement up to 3.0 percentage points (pp) higher). On HARWE, they improve subject-dependent HAR and identity recognition by 1.1 pp and 5.8 pp, and outperform supervised end-to-end models by +31.6 pp and +22.3 pp on unseen subjects of multi-device datasets. Moreover, their generative capacity improves reconstruction mean squared error over multi-modal VAEs (34-74% improvement) and their synthetic data improve affect recognition on unseen subjects (2 pp). With 4.1M parameters, invariant to the number of modalities (21xfewer than a multi-branch VAE), OmniDecVAEs are scalable, single-model full-stack learners for wearable representations.

### Paper [471]
Title: NL2AGBench: Benchmarking LLM Auto-Formalization for AlphaGeometry
URL: https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.28481
Authors: Samuel Xiao, Judy Song, Rory Hu, Ziliang Zong
Abstract: Recent advances in large language models (LLMs) have demonstrated strong capabilities in natural language understanding and mathematical reasoning. However, their ability to translate informal mathematical problems into formal representations remains underexplored. This limitation is particularly important for neuro-symbolic geometry systems such as AlphaGeometry, whose theorem-proving engine requires inputs in a specialized domain-specific language (DSL). Although AlphaGeometry achieves near-IMO gold-medalist performance, manually converting natural-language problems into its formal syntax remains a significant usability bottleneck. To address this challenge, we introduce the Natural Language to AlphaGeometry Benchmark (NL2AGBench), which evaluates LLMs in translating English geometry problems into AlphaGeometry-compatible formal representations. NL2AGBench uses execution-based verification within AlphaGeometry to assess translation quality rather than relying solely on textual similarity. We evaluate ten state-of-the-art open- and closed-source LLMs across multiple parameter scales and analyze executable translation accuracy, syntactic correctness, and error characteristics. Our experiments reveal a substantial performance gap between closed- and open-source models: leading closed-source models achieve executable translation rates above 80%, while even the largest open-source models struggle to consistently preserve geometric constraints and produce valid formalizations. We introduce an error taxonomy distinguishing syntax and logic errors and investigate mitigation strategies, including few-shot prompting, fine-tuning, and human-guided hinting, which yield measurable improvements across multiple model families.

### Paper [472]
Title: From Portraits to Pathology: Probing Diagnostic Overreach in Vision-Language Models
URL: https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.05540
Authors: Karan Dua, Amit Agarwal, Hitesh Laxmichand Patel, Hansa Meghwani, Jyotika Singh, Ranjeet Gupta, Graham Horwood, Tao Sheng, Avi Sil, Sujith Ravi, Dan Roth
Abstract: Many medical conditions require clinical assessment of symptoms, history, and functioning rather than judgments from appearance. We study whether vision-language models (VLMs) abstain from facial attribution of autism spectrum disorder (ASD), attention-deficit/hyperactivity disorder (ADHD), major depressive disorder (MDD), and generalized anxiety disorder (GAD).
  To support this study, we construct PARITY (Paired Assessment with Reused Identity), a demographically balanced dataset for testing whether changing facial expressions alters diagnostic judgments, while holding identity and other visual factors as constant as possible.
  Our comprehensive evaluation of 27 open- and closed-weight models reveals inconsistent abstention across expressions, conditions, and task formats. Models turn facial expressions into unsupported medical judgments: this behavior is widespread among open-weight VLMs and extends to safety-trained frontier models that abstain on individual portraits yet make diagnostic judgments when comparing portraits of the same identity. Safeguard checks reveal further gaps: clinical instructions do not consistently prevent these judgments, and a guardrail model rarely flags paired requests as unsafe. Semantic analysis further shows that most baseline open-model refusals fail to explain both the insufficiency of facial evidence and the need for clinical assessment. Unsupported judgments and seemingly authoritative explanations can cause distress, reinforce stigma, and offer false reassurance, underscoring the need for stronger safety measures in model training and deployment.

### Paper [473]
Title: Towards Embodied Air-Ground Cooperative Object Search: Benchmark, Dataset and Agentic Method
URL: https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.08402
Authors: Boao Yu, Zimo Chen, Junreng Rao, Yue Hu, Zhengqiu Zhu, Yong Zhao, Rusheng Ju
Abstract: Air-Ground Object Search (AGOS) in urban environments is a challenging embodied task, which requires an Unmanned Aerial Vehicle (UAV) and an Unmanned Ground Vehicle (UGV) to jointly search for and verify a specified target vehicle from multi-view visual references. To study this underexplored problem, we introduce AGOS-Bench, the first dedicated benchmark for evaluating whether general-purpose Vision-Language Models (VLMs) can integrate aerial discoveries and ground-level verification through UAV-UGV cooperation. We further provide AGOS-Dataset as the companion resource of exemplary trajectories constructed by an automatic pipeline. It consists of 7.7k episodes for searching objects of diverse categories and attributes, spanning three difficulty levels. To address the AGOS task, we propose AGOS-Agent, a training-free and tool-augmented approach. The agentic method relieves VLMs from complex and dynamic coordination via a deliberate search-handoff-verify cooperation protocol, only demanding VLMs for scene understanding and decision-making. Extensive experiments on nine VLMs show that AGOS-Agent improves overall success rate for eight of the nine evaluated backbones while reducing decision steps for all nine. On the hard split, the SR and SPL of Gemini-3.6-Flash increase from 8.6% to 55.7% and from 7.6% to 44.0%, respectively.

### Paper [474]
Title: The Missing Complement: State-Conditioned Minimal Sufficient Evidence for Coding Agents
URL: https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.20050
Authors: Zhexi Feng, Ruiyi Zhang, Yongbo Yang, Pengtao Xie
Abstract: Retrieval assembles repository context by ranking passages for relevance to the current query. A coding agent halfway through an issue has already read much of what such a ranker returns. Relevance is scored per passage, but sufficiency belongs to the set: independently scored passages can fill the budget with support for one requirement while another goes unmet. We formulate state-conditioned minimal sufficient evidence recovery: given a captured agent state, recover a compact evidence combination supplying what its next decision still lacks. SERBench measures this on 500 held-out states from 45 repositories, recording what the agent has seen, crediting only sets that satisfy every annotated evidence requirement of the current decision, and separating set recovery from candidate discovery. MSS-Complement treats acquisition as set construction, not ranking. Three semantic calls propose a jointly sufficient set, search for what it lacks, and return 4-8 intact source units within 6,144 tokens. One configuration, fixed on calibration data, recovers a complete set for 73.0% of those states at five items and 80.6% at eight, against 61.4% and 72.4% for Qwen3 embedding with reranking. A matched control ranking by similarity alone recovers fewer complete sets, placing the margin over it in the set-level policy, not the computation. The lead persists from frozen repository source with no gold-derived pool. On AMA-Bench it answers from a 76.2% smaller answer prompt, with accuracy 2.08 points above that benchmark's own memory agent. Removing one required group from a complete set costs repair-localization precision under two executors. Retrieval for agents is better posed as recovering what a decision lacks than re-ranking what an issue resembles.

### Paper [475]
Title: Paint-Anything: Unified Any-Color Control for Image Generation and Editing
URL: https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.20816
Authors: Ji Xie, Dewei Zhou, Xinyu Huang, Zhennan Chen, Xun Wang
Abstract: Professional design requires any-color control: the ability to specify an object's target color with any 24-bit hex value for image generation and editing. Prior work has explored color generation, editing, and colorization, but often relies on dedicated color representations or specialized inference procedures. Advances in large language models offer a simpler starting point: even compact models can associate hex values with color semantics. We present Paint-Anything, which learns a shared hex-prompt interface for generation and editing through object-level color supervision. We develop a data pipeline that constructs Paint-500K from real images through object grounding, perceptual color labeling, and editing-pair synthesis. Since shadows make real-image labels only approximate colors, we complement this supervision with pure-color anchors whose pixels exactly match their paired hex values. These anchors are used only at high-noise timesteps, leaving low-noise training to natural images. We further introduce Any Color Benchmark (ACBench), comprising ACBench-T2I and ACBench-Edit, to measure object-level hex color fidelity across both tasks. On FLUX.2-4B, Paint-Anything improves ACBench-T2I and ACBench-Edit scores by 85.3% and 28.3%, respectively, relative to the base model, with ablations supporting the training recipe. It also achieves the highest average CompColor score among the compared methods.

### Paper [476]
Title: KernelOPT: Dispatch-Aware Agentic Search for GPU Kernel Optimization
URL: https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.30059
Authors: Aheli Poddar, Sanskar Prasad, Arindam Samanta, Subha Chakraborty, Vishal Goyal, Rohit Singh Rathaur
Abstract: Deep learning inference and training performance depends critically on GPU kernel efficiency. Modern compilers such as PyTorch Inductor automatically generate GPU kernels from high-level model code, but frequently underperform expert-written implementations by wide margins. Recent LLM-assisted optimizers explore these opportunities through kernel generation and refinement; yet translating isolated kernel improvements into model-level gains remains a challenge. We present KernelOPT, a profiling-guided multi-agent system for GPU kernel optimization. It inspects generated Triton kernels and external library calls in compiler artifacts to guide synthesis and refinement, then assembles and evaluates the resulting model. Four stages of validation combine execution checks, multi-seed numerical comparison, model-level checking with a float64 fallback and end-to-end performance gating ($\gamma{=}1.03$). The system supports PyTorch nn.Modules, standalone Triton kernels, and Helion kernels. It is evaluated on all 250 KernelBench problems on NVIDIA H200 which includes 100 single-operator problems (Level1), 100 composite operators (Level2), and 50 model architectures (Level3). KernelOPT achieves geometric mean speedups over torch.compile of 1.40$\times$, 1.15$\times$ and 1.07$\times$ respectively across L1, L2 and L3. Among problems exceeding 1.03$\times$ speedup, the geomeans are 2.54$\times$ (36 L1), 1.84$\times$ (23 L2), and 1.37$\times$ (11 L3). These gains include arithmetic-precision changes and task-specific algebraic simplifications under the evaluated numerical tolerances. The evaluation also reveals discrepancies between isolated kernel improvements and end-to-end performance, motivating model-level measurement alongside kernel-level optimization. Code is available at https://github.com/TorchedHat/KernelOPT.

### Paper [477]
Title: Chameleon: Dynamic Format Adapter for Efficient Diffusion
URL: https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.33496
Authors: Arnab Sanyal, Sandeep Chinchali
Abstract: Post-training quantization (PTQ) is the standard way to run modern diffusion models on memory-constrained accelerators, yet every existing diffusion PTQ scheme fixes the $\mathit{number\ format}$ in advance and only tunes the scale, zero point, or per-layer bit-width. At a fixed bit-width the best format depends on the distribution being encoded, and that distribution differs across weight channels, across layers, and along the diffusion timestep, where activation distributions slide from heavy-tailed and noise-dominated to tightly clustered and structured. We propose Chameleon, a PTQ framework that holds the bit-width fixed and treats the format itself as a discrete variable, chosen per weight channel and per activation layer, optionally refined per timestep bucket. Activation formats come from {INT8, FP8 E4M3, FP8 E5M2, MXFP8, MXINT8}, selected ahead of time from two cheap statistics (empirical kurtosis and the closed-form diffusion SNR) and stored in a lookup table; weight formats come from {INT8, MXINT8} at 8 bits or {INT4, NF4, FP4 E2M1, MXINT4, MXFP4} at 4 bits, selected offline by reconstruction error. An architectural fork adapts the same selection layer to multi-step UNets, single-step distilled models, and Diffusion Transformers. Most of the gain comes from the per-layer choice; the timestep refinement helps mainly at 6-bit activations. Across SDXL, SDXL-Turbo, and PixArt-$\alpha$ on COCO-2014, Chameleon achieves the best FID in all six backbone $\times$ bit-width settings, with CLIP within 0.24 of the FP16 reference and the best of all quantized methods at $W_{4}A_{8}$. On PixArt-$\alpha$, a real mixed-format kernel runs 1.09$\times$ faster than FP16 end-to-end with 3$\times$ smaller weights.

### Paper [478]
Title: Channel-Dependent State Space Model for Multivariate Time Series Forecasting
URL: https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.36453
Authors: Yu-Cheng Wu, Fan-Keng Sun, Li-Chun Lu, Duane S. Boning
Abstract: Multivariate time series forecasting (MTSF) is critical across many real-world domains. Existing deep learning approaches fall into two paradigms with distinct limitations: channel-independent (CI) methods ignore cross-variable dependencies and model only temporal dynamics, while channel-dependent (CD) methods consider both but typically rely on architectural compromises to mitigate overfitting and computational overhead. We therefore propose Chameleon, a CD state space model (SSM) that enables data-dependent, fine-grained interactions across variables while scaling linearly with the number of variables. By connecting selective SSMs with the Kalman filter, we adapt the latter's measurement-update formulation for cross-variable modeling while preserving the SSM backbone for robust temporal modeling. We further identify favorable inductive biases of GatedDeltaNet for time series, adapt it as our backbone, and improve generalization through additional techniques, including a previously unexplored stochastic perturbation of reversible instance normalization. On strongly dependent ODE and PEMS datasets, Chameleon achieves the best MSE and MAE across all settings, while its CI ablation and prior CD methods incur 61-178% higher MSE on average. Across 28 standard benchmark settings, Chameleon also achieves better MSE and MAE than each baseline in at least 27 and 22 cases, respectively. Training-time, FLOP, and peak-memory analyses on Traffic and ETT further demonstrate competitive efficiency and favorable scalability across different variable counts.

### Paper [479]
Title: DatalogBench: Evaluating Large Language Models on Text-to-Datalog Synthesis
URL: https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.37233
Authors: Yuan Li, Hanyun Jiang, Guowei Tian, Chengpeng Wang, Peisen Yao
Abstract: Datalog underpins reasoning tasks such as program analysis, but its programs are hard to write. Existing synthesizers automate this task but require users to state their intent as input-output examples. Large language models (LLMs) suggest a more natural route, text-to-Datalog synthesis from a natural-language question, yet how well they do so has not been systematically evaluated. We present DatalogBench, a benchmark of 136 text-to-Datalog synthesis tasks curated from existing Datalog-based artifacts. Synthesized programs are graded by execution on held-out inputs against an oracle validated by mutation analysis. Across six LLMs and four prompting configurations, exact match peaks at 75.7%; an input-output example raises it by about six points, while relation descriptions add little. Under direct prompting, most failures occur at compile time, typically because a model invents auxiliary predicates that it never declares or types consistently. Two coding agents reach up to 86.8% and eliminate nearly all such failures, leaving mostly semantic errors concentrated in recursive tasks. DatalogBench thus identifies recursive reasoning and decomposition as open challenges for current LLMs and agents, and offers a reliable, execution-grounded measure of both.

### Paper [480]
Title: Clause-Factored Bilateral State Grounding and Scoped Passivity for Counterfactual Open-World Deductive Reasoning
URL: https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.37351
Authors: Zeyu Jia, Bo Yao, Zihao Yang
Abstract: Open-World Assumption (OWA) deduction requires distinguishing provable facts (True), refutations (False), and unprovable statements (Unknown) across counterfactual edits and unseen domains. Sequence and latent-recurrent transformers rely on distributional shortcuts, whereas chain-of-thought language models incur high autoregressive latency. We decompose OWA deduction into a clause-factored bilateral grounder ($F$) and a deductive deliberation operator ($B$), forming $B \circ F$ in Typed Edge-Factorized Bilateral Port-Hamiltonian Deliberation (TE-BPHD). Trained solely on three-way query labels without parsers or proof-depth annotations, TE-BPHD (37.85M parameters) maps clauses into a 1,440-dimensional Belnap space and propagates support through passivity-balanced implicit Port-Hamiltonian rule diodes. Across 17,036 locked ProofWriter test queries, TE-BPHD reaches 79.17 +/- 5.48% depth-5 counterfactual consistency (+23.83 to +38.67 percentage points over Seq2Seq T5, Flan-T5, and Recurrent-Depth T5) and 94.95 +/- 1.23% out-of-distribution (OOD) accuracy across 5,270 six-domain queries (92.54 +/- 3.38% macro-mean). On a paired 120-query OOD slice, TE-BPHD attains 93.61 +/- 4.59% versus 79.17% for 3-shot Qwen3-1.7B under a 2,048-token reasoning cap (+14.44 percentage points) at 9.44 versus 14,878.4 ms/query batch-16 NVIDIA L4 throughput. Operator interventions show that symmetric single-rule passivity prevents positive two-cycle modes seen in Gain-Matched Unilateral Horn (+50.00 percentage points counterfactual consistency), whereas unit-interval Grounded Product-T-Norm Logic Tensor Networks (LTN) achieve cycle-safe OOD transfer (94.50 +/- 0.87%) without linear solves, and multi-fact ParaRules prose exposes an upstream grounding trade-off (1.33 +/- 1.76% zero-shot vs. 34.11 +/- 5.01% with five-slot optimal-transport joint grounding).

### Paper [481]
Title: Predictive Self-Supervised Learning Provably Identifies Stochastic Signals under Nuisance
URL: https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.37789
Authors: Fabian A. Mikulasch, Friedemann Zenke
Abstract: Self-supervised learning (SSL) by predicting in latent space, without generating the input data itself, learns highly abstract, useful representations. Intuitively, this success is often attributed to its ability to discard nuisance information that is irrelevant to prediction. However, this poses a conundrum: both stochastic variation in a prediction-relevant latent signal and true nuisance make observations partly unpredictable; how could they be distinguished? Surprisingly, we prove that common SSL methods can achieve exactly this, by implicitly instantiating a latent-variable model with stochastic dynamics and observation-private nuisance. We trace their ability to recover the stochastic signal to two complementary principles: Predictive mutual information maximization ensures that representations retain the information needed for prediction, while latent distribution matching constrains how this information is encoded, thereby making the retained signal identifiable. We confirm this identifiability result in simulations for Gaussian predictors, which recover the true signal up to an affine transformation even in dynamic, nuisance-laden environments.

### Paper [482]
Title: STEPQuant: When and Where Errors Matter in Delta-Rule Recurrent State Quantization
URL: https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.38169
Authors: Bingchen Yao, Haobo Xu, Haokun Lin, Yichen Wu, Ziyu Guo, Renrui Zhang, Zhichao Lu, Zhenan Sun, Ying Wei
Abstract: Linear attention replaces growing KV caches with fixed-size recurrent states, yet these persistent states can become a substantial memory bottleneck under concurrent serving. Directly quantizing recurrent states to low precision often leads to severe accuracy degradation, as quantization errors propagate through successive state updates. We discover that the impact of these errors depends on two complementary dimensions: temporally, errors in long-lived memory can persist across many decoding steps; spatially, errors in different key rows affect model outputs differently, while state magnitudes vary substantially along both rows and columns. Motivated by these observations, we propose STEPQuant, a spatial-temporal post-training quantization framework for Delta-rule recurrent states. STEPQuant allocates precision according to error magnitude and memory lifetime, and jointly fits key-row and value-column scales based on state distributions and key-row impact on output error. Experiments on Qwen3.8-27B and Kimi-Linear-48B-A3B-Instruct across both long- and short-generation benchmarks show that STEPQuant closely matches FP32-state accuracy under a nominal 6-bit budget and outperforms uniform INT8 in its 4-bit configuration. Integrated into SGLang with optimized GPU kernels, 6-bit STEPQuant achieves over 5x recurrent-state compression and reduces total serving memory by up to 68.7%. Our code is available at https://github.com/Dreamer-Toby/STEPQuant.

### Paper [483]
Title: Capturing In-Context Learning Dynamics with Task Operators
URL: https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.01054
Authors: Guangzhi Xiong, Zhenghao He, Bohan Liu, Sanchit Sinha, Wenqian Ye, Aidong Zhang
Abstract: In-context learning (ICL) enables language models to perform new tasks from demonstrations without weight updates. However, every ICL inference requires processing the full set of examples, resulting in inefficient deployments, and how ICL works mechanistically is not fully understood. Prior work compresses ICL into fixed activation vectors extracted from specific layers or positions, but these input-independent interventions fail on complex tasks where the output depends on fine-grained interactions with the input. By analyzing the ICL forward pass, we show that each attention head's output is an affine transformation of its context-masked counterpart, and that the parameters of this transformation are empirically stable across samples for a given task. Building on this, we introduce Task Operator (TO), which replays this transformation as an analytically derived update to the attention output projection. Across lexical, algorithmic, and reasoning tasks, TO achieves the best overall performance among prior methods and substantially narrows the gap between zero-shot inference and ICL. We further show that the extracted knowledge concentrates in a task-specific sparse circuit across layers and positions, and that averaging operators from disjoint demonstration batches enables effective many-shot scaling without expanding the context window. Our code is available at https://github.com/gzxiong/task_operator.

### Paper [484]
Title: AgroGround: What Grounding Fine-Tuning Changes in Agricultural Vision-Language Models
URL: https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.04425
Authors: Abdulla Alshehhi, Zongyan Han, Rao Muhammad Anwer
Abstract: Large-scale agricultural grounding data can now be generated automatically, but how training on such data affects a vision-language model's localization, recognition, and refusal capabilities remains unclear. To study these effects, we introduce AgroGround, containing 794,850 automatically grounded agricultural instructions for training and a separate benchmark of 1,478 human-verified images for evaluation. Among these images, 500 are independently re-annotated, yielding an inter-annotator instance F1 of 0.789. We use controlled comparisons to isolate the effects of different training choices on localization, recognition, their joint correctness, and refusal. Grounding-only fine-tuning reduces four-way recognition from 51.7% to 29.2%, with predictions showing a strong candidate-position bias. However, changing only the instruction format raises recognition to 72.7% while maintaining similar grounding performance. Training with healthy-image negatives achieves 94.1% refusal on healthy images, but only 28.6% refusal of incorrect target names on positive images, showing that these two refusal behaviors do not necessarily improve together. Reinforcement learning reveals a further trade-off, improving localization by 0.014 F1 over a matched supervised control while reducing recognition and refusal. Across model families and held-out training sources, localization transfers more reliably than recognition and target-specific refusal. External benchmarks further show that the gains depend on the domain and task. Together, these findings show that grounding fine-tuning changes the balance between localization, recognition, and refusal rather than improving all three uniformly.

### Paper [485]
Title: Kandinsky 6.0 Video: Foundation Models for Synchronized Video and Audio Generation
URL: https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.05608
Authors: Team Kandinsky, Julia Agafonova, Bulat Akhmatov, Mikhail Aksyutin, Grigorii Alekseenko, Anastasia Aliaskina, Olga Androsova, Vladimir Arkhipkin, Anna Averchenkova, Alexander Belykh, Serafima Bocharova, Sofiya Bogakovskaya, Anton Bukashkin, Mark Bulygin, Kirill Buzygin, Irina Cheremnykh, Kirill Chernyshev, Mikhail Chernyshov, Vladimir Chernyy, David Chikovani, Georgy Daniltsev, Denis Dimitrov, Anna Dmitrienko, Vladimir Dokholyan, Sergey Emelyanov, Dmitry Ermilov, Georgii Fedorov, Polina Gavrilova, Nikolai Gerasimenko, Aleksandr Gordeev, Andrey Inozemtsev, Andrei Ivaniuta, Alexander Ivanov, Mikhail Karaev, Anastasiia Kargapoltseva, Ivan Kirillov, Nikita Kiselev, Valeria Kobenko, Yury Kolabushin, Denis Koposov, Anatoly Korobov, Vladimir Korviakov, Maria Kovaleva, Kirill Kozlov, Denis Krzhivokolskiy, Konstantin Kuklev, Alexander Kunitsyn, Sergey Kuzin, Vladislav Lakhtionov, Alexey Letunovskiy, Maxim Litvinov, Alexander Lyulkov, Georgy Makarov, Kirill Malakhov, Egor Malykh, Mikhail Mamaev, Dmitrii Mikhailov, Polina Mikhailova, Ivan Mikheev, Elizaveta Muromtseva, Nikolai Nazarkin, Tatiana Nikulina, Lev Novitskiy, Stanislav Onuchin, Nikita Osterov, Denis Parkhomenko, Anatoliy Parpara, Vladimir Polovnikov, Konstantin Reznikov, Azat Saginbaev, Nikita Samsonov, Alexander Sentsov, Nikita Shaimov, Artem Sherstyuk, Andrey Shutkin, Egor Silvestrov, Bulat Suleimanov, Matvey Suprunov, Sergey Taranov, Irina Tolstykh, Tatiana Trofimuk, Ilya Trushkin, Aleksandra Tsybina, Olga Varlashina, Viacheslav Vasilev, Ilya Vasiliev, Eugeny Vilisov, Sergey Yakubson, Konstantin Zakharov
Abstract: We present Kandinsky 6.0 Video, a family of foundation diffusion models for synchronized text-to-audio-video generation, comprising Kandinsky 6.0 Video Lite (3B parameters) and Kandinsky 6.0 Video Pro (29B parameters). Both models generate 5-second video clips with synchronized 44 kHz audio, including lip-sync, in text-to-audio-video (T2AV) and image-to-audio-video (I2AV) modes; a built-in super-resolution model raises the output resolution to Full-HD (1920x1080). Building on the video generation capabilities of Kandinsky 5.0, Kandinsky 6.0 Video employs a dual-stream CrossDiT architecture that connects a pretrained video stream and a newly trained audio stream through bidirectional cross-attention for temporal and semantic alignment. Our continuous pretraining strategy first trains the audio stream from scratch on large-scale audio corpora and then trains both streams jointly on paired audio-video data while preserving unimodal fidelity; pretraining is followed by supervised fine-tuning, reinforcement-learning-based post-training, and distillation. In side-by-side human evaluation, Kandinsky 6.0 Video Pro clearly outperforms its predecessor, Kandinsky 5.0 Video Pro, and remains competitive with leading audio-video generation models, outperforming LTX 2.5 and Seedance 2.0 in speech quality. To accelerate open research and deployment in multimedia generation, we release the code, model checkpoints, and diffusers integration under the MIT license.

### Paper [486]
Title: Safeguarding LLMs via Model-Agnostic Latent Safety Signals from Dark Knowledge
URL: https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.07532
Authors: Wonjun Lee, Kyungsik Yang, Gaeun Ji, Vaidehi Patil, Haon Park, Bumsub Ham, Mohit Bansal, Suhyun Kim
Abstract: LLMs have advanced rapidly, raising growing concerns about their safety. Recent work has proposed approaches to detect and defend against attacks including defenses at decoding stage that leverage models' hidden states. However, existing decoding-stage defenses suffer from two limitations. First, they introduce a trade-off between safety and over-refusal, where strengthening safety degrades the model's helpfulness on benign queries. Second, many of these methods rely on internal hidden states and are thus restricted to specific architectures, incurring substantial overhead and limited generalization across models. To address these limitations, we introduce LADE (Latent Safety Signals for Defense), which leverages latent safety signals extracted by contrasting harmful and benign queries from dark knowledge (i.e., information carried by the output probability distribution beyond its argmax) in the first-token output probability distribution. Our key insight is that, beyond surface-level refusal tokens, the dark knowledge in the first-token distribution contains latent safety signals, defined as tokens whose probabilities differ sharply between harmful and benign queries. We show that these signals consistently align across LLMs, forming a model-agnostic direction that emerges from safety alignment. LADE consists of three components: (1) Extracting Latent Safety Signals from Dark Knowledge, which selects top-k safety-discriminative tokens from the first-token probability distribution; (2) Tokenizer Mapping, which maps these tokens across different tokenizers to enable model-agnostic application; and (3) kNN-based Discrimination, which classifies queries via a k-Nearest Neighbors search over the mapped tokens. Across diverse LLMs and benchmarks, LADE is robust against a wide range of jailbreak attacks and lowers attack success rates while maintaining a competitive safety-utility trade-off.

### Paper [487]
Title: Mixing Regularization for Average-Reward $Q$-Learning and $Q$-Planning
URL: https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.14988
Authors: Julien Roger, Sam Bodmer, Jian Qian, Benjamin Riviere
Abstract: The average-reward setting for reinforcement learning is challenging because, without discounting, the span-contraction of the Bellman operator depends on the mixing properties of the underlying Markov dynamics. Whereas existing work typically assumes contraction, for instance through environment mixing, we study regularization toward a given reward-agnostic, $m$-step mixing policy to control the mixing properties of the learned policy. Our central mixing lemma quantifies how the reference policy's mixing properties transfer to the resulting learned policies. In particular, a single regularizer-dependent quantity simultaneously controls the contraction of the regularized Bellman operator and the exploration of the resulting policies. A second regularizer-dependent quantity controls the bias introduced by regularization, revealing a trade-off between convergence speed and approximation accuracy. Building on this result, we establish finite-time guarantees for regularized $Q$-iteration, synchronous and single-trajectory $Q$-learning, and $Q$-planning. Under one-step mixing, our bounds for approximating the regularized fixed point match the classical model-query bound for relative value iteration and, up to logarithmic factors, an existing sample-complexity bound for synchronous $Q$-learning. We also establish finite-time guarantees under multi-step mixing. For $Q$-planning, we characterize how the planning horizon trades off boundary bias against sample complexity. Finally, we present numerical studies validating the analysis: (i) knowledge of a strong mixing policy improves convergence and exploration, with larger benefits under more severe bottlenecks; and (ii) the observed rankings of $\alpha$-divergence regularizers by bias and convergence speed are consistent with our theoretical analysis.

### Paper [488]
Title: Non-Abelian quantum Hall states at strong Landau level mixing: finding anyons with first-principles AI
URL: https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.18967
Authors: Pierre-Antoine Graham, Ahmed Abouelkomsan, Liang Fu
Abstract: Non-Abelian anyons, strange particles forming the basis of fault-tolerant topological quantum computing, are predicted to emerge in fractional quantum Hall states at even-denominator filling factors. Identifying which topological order is realized has proven to be a challenging problem that requires an accurate, non-perturbative treatment of realistic Landau level mixing, which has so far been missing from theoretical studies. Here we present the first complete solution of non-Abelian quantum Hall problem that includes all electrons and all Landau levels, for spin-polarized electrons at filling factor $\nu = 3/2$. We develop a momentum-resolved neural-network variational wave function, optimized solely on the microscopic Hamiltonian, which gives direct access to the topologically degenerate ground states. Without any physics input about the expected outcome, it discovers a degenerate ground-state manifold whose entanglement reveals anyons with quantum dimension $\sqrt{2}$, the hallmark of Ising-type non-Abelian order. By directly computing the overlap with trial wave functions, we further show that Landau level mixing favors the anti-Pfaffian over other candidate states. Our results demonstrate the power of neural-network wave functions in finding and characterizing non-Abelian anyons from first principles, in regimes of strong Landau level mixing, relevant to many recent experiments.

### Paper [489]
Title: Refined Topology-Pass Scheduling: Damped Trust-Region Compensation for Dynamic k-NN Graphs in SE(3)-Equivariant Structure Prediction
URL: https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.19246
Authors: Jiankai Huang, Hanwen Shen
Abstract: Equivariant structure-prediction pipelines alternate coordinate updates with the construction of a k-nearest-neighbour (kNN) graph whose membership is a discrete, piecewise-constant function of the coordinates. Rebuilding that graph at every block boundary (PB) is the expensive part of an epoch, whereas reusing one frozen graph (F) is fast but leaves a topology gap. We study a two-pass design in which the cheap pass records a small amount of state (margins, kNN radii, measured per-boundary displacements) and a second pass compensates conservatively. We formulate the choice of the graph-construction basis as a damped fixed-point iteration whose unique fixed point is exactly PB, and derive (i) exact non-flip certificates with the two-sided constant $m_i/4$ and one-sided constant $m_i/2$, (ii) the minimum-variance (BLUE) replay gain $\gamma^*$ and the resulting feasibility crossover, (iii) an $\ell_\infty$ trust-region projection that preserves convergence, and (iv) an analytic near-tie spacing law $E[m] \approx r_k/(3k)$ that matches measurements to 1\%. Three compensation levels are implemented in C++ (L1: frozen set with live geometry; L2: margin-gated local repair; L3: damped, projected basis) and validated under a matched-conditions protocol (identical seed, MSA depth and trajectory family, only the topology mode differs). L2 reproduces the PB edge set exactly ($D = 0.0000$ at every boundary), L1 stays within 0.6% and L3 interpolates monotonically: L2 reproduces the PB edge set exactly when the trust region is removed, and L3 interpolates between the frozen and the PB topology. The recorded trajectory itself yields a measured $1.5\times$ Pass-1 and $1.76\times$ epoch speed-up with no measurable loss gap. An open-source implementation of the runtime and of all the experiments reported here is available at https://github.com/AlbertHuang96/ProteinPredictMachineLearning.

### Paper [490]
Title: REACT: Representational Early Analysis and Control of Chain-of-Thought Trajectories
URL: https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.32113
Authors: Hanwen Zhang, Enyi Jiang, Ruike Zhu, Qinan Yu, Sanmi Koyejo
Abstract: Chain-of-thought (CoT) generation offers an opportunity to intervene before a reasoning model completes its answer. How does the safety effect of a single activation edit vary across early reasoning states, and what factors govern that variation? We study this question with REACT, a conditional activation intervention that learns a safety direction from paired safe and unsafe trajectories and applies it once to an early CoT hidden state. The model weights remain frozen, and all subsequent tokens are generated without further edits. To study timing, we train directions separately at each of the first five CoT positions and evaluate ungated edits with full post-edit regeneration. Across three DeepSeek-R1 distilled models, site-specific edits concentrate safety gains in the opening CoT tokens. Norm-matched controls on DeepSeek-R1-Distill-Llama-8B (8B) disentangle two factors behind this curve: holding an effective direction and edit budget fixed still yields much larger early ($t_1$-$t_5$) than later ($t_6$-$t_8$) gains, whereas the near-zero site-specific $t_3$ gain reflects higher optimisation variance despite rising risk-probe AUROC. Forced prefixes shift the high-gain region with the opening context, and a four-arm intervention shows that ~98% of the net gain at $t_2$ propagates through the changed next token. On the same 8B model, REACT raises the rate of fully safe CoT-answer outputs on StrongREJECT-273 from 29.06% to 82.05%, while keeping benign refusal and usefulness near baseline. An anonymized implementation is available at https://anonymous.4open.science/r/REACT_official2-333B.

### Paper [491]
Title: From Fragmentation to Fusion: A Survey and Roadmap for Omnimodal Embeddings
URL: https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.00172
Authors: Ziyin Zhang, Yuyu Guo, Ying Zhang, Siqiao Xue, Zihan Liao, Jin Qin, Wenjie Yang, Siyuan Yang, Shijie Li, Cheng Chen, Hao Wu, Zhaoyang Zhu, Ruipeng Li, Zhaokun Jiang, Lei Lei, Rui Wang, Hang Yu, Rui Wang
Abstract: Embedding models underpin virtually every modern AI system, from search engines to recommendation systems. Despite shared mathematical foundations, research on embedding models remains fragmented by modality: the communities develop largely in isolation, seldom exchanging insights. We argue that this fragmentation obscures a deeper convergence. Driven by contrastive learning, Transformer architectures, and a granularity shift, embedding research across modalities is converging toward a common set of principles, even as modality-specific challenges remain underexplored. To reason across this convergence without conflating it, we propose a framework of three progressively harder capabilities: coverage, comparability, and compositionality. Three paths toward unification satisfy these in turn: transformation broadens coverage, alignment enables comparability, and joint embedding, the terminus we advocate, aims at compositionality. This position paper traces the resulting evolution, providing the first unified, cross-cutting treatment of omnimodal embedding research.

### Paper [492]
Title: VideoEditor-MoE: Scaling Complex Video Editing with Mixture of Experts
URL: https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.03043
Authors: Xiao Cao, Fuchen Long, Yansong Qu, Wen Xiao, Xin Zhang, Xinlong Sun, Xi Chen, Zhiyong Huang
Abstract: Complex video editing requires understanding complex instructions while jointly fulfilling multiple editing requests. Meeting these demands through dense model scaling substantially increases computational cost. We introduce VideoEditor-MoE, the first open-source mixture-of-experts framework for video editing. Beyond sparse MLP-MoE layers, MoE-Attention expands attention capacity through routed key--value experts while preserving global interactions among video tokens. Initialized from a 5B video editing model, VideoEditor-MoE scales to 14B and 56B parameters, with approximately 8B parameters activated per token in both variants. Comparisons with existing methods and two complementary baselines, Explicit-MoE and MLP-MoE, assess its benefits over explicit expert guidance and MLP-only expert architectures. Further analyses demonstrate that all experts remain active and reveal that learned routing responds more strongly to sample content and diffusion noise levels than to human-defined editing categories. Spatial analysis further indicates sensitivity to object-level semantic features. We also identify decoupled weight decay as a strategy for stabilizing expert scaling.

### Paper [493]
Title: Best-of-Depth: Learning Exploratory Dynamics in Looped Transformers
URL: https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.04663
Authors: Shota Takashiro, Takeshi Kojima, Masanori Koyama, Kohei Hayashi, Yusuke Iwasawa
Abstract: Looped Transformers extend inference-time computation through repeated latent-state updates, but final-depth training does not directly encourage useful intermediate alternatives. We introduce Best-of-Depth (BoD), which trains a recurrent trajectory as a candidate set by minimizing prediction loss across depths. Different valid targets can be matched at different depths without architectural changes or depth-specific labels. An exact-match analysis connects this objective to solution coverage. On N-Queens completion and graph coloring, BoD improves coverage over final-depth and intermediate-supervision objectives. N-Queens coverage reaches 91.03%, versus 45.75% for final-depth training. Further experiments show discovery beyond the training depth and recovery of solutions withheld from teacher pools on familiar inputs. Fine-tuning a pretrained looped language model with BoD yields higher pooled code-generation success than final-depth training when programs are generated separately at evaluated fixed depths. These results support training recurrent computation for solution discovery along its trajectory.

### Paper [494]
Title: Context--Answer Metamodels: Forecasting LLM Activations Before the First Answer Token
URL: https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.06837
Authors: Tommy He, Thomas Jiralerspong
Abstract: Under stochastic decoding, a single context can elicit many different answers from a language model. Characterizing this variation typically requires many expensive autoregressive rollouts, each revealing only one realization. We ask whether the activations produced while reading the context can directly forecast the model's rollout activations. We train context--answer metamodels that map a context activation sequence to point or distribution forecasts of mean-pooled answer activations. We find that (i) the four metamodel families we study forecast mean-pooled answer activations with high fidelity, and the distribution metamodels also capture rollout variability; (ii) performance improves as the training set grows to 500,000 prompts, and the metamodels generalize zero-shot to out-of-domain datasets; (iii) their forecasts retain behavior-predictive signal; and (iv) context activations from Qwen2.5 predict mean-pooled answer activations from Qwen3.5 and Gemma 4, suggesting that activation forecasts can transfer across models. Together, these results suggest that context activation traces can support scalable forecasts of behaviors in LLM answers before the first answer token is generated.

### Paper [495]
Title: SPILLWAY: Separating Target Admission from Sanitization to Defend Tool-Using Agents against Prompt Injection
URL: https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.07222
Authors: Woosang Lim (SAINT Machines, KAIST), Yeseul Chang (Chung-Ang University), Jaewoo Lee (Chung-Ang University)
Abstract: Tool-using agents act on email, files, and payments, so an instruction hidden in a web page or a shared document can make an agent send data or money to an attacker. Many runtime defenses put a language model on the decision that lets an action through, so the decision depends on a model that may read the attacker's text and adds model calls to every step. Provenance defenses keep the model off the final check but need a model to plan or route each task first. Where an action goes can instead be read from the tools' typed returns: an email's sender or a payment's account arrives in a typed field, while injected instructions arrive as free text. SPILLWAY turns this observation into a deterministic floor with no per-task plan. The check admits a call when each target value that appears in tool output but not in the user's task was returned by a read-only tool in a typed field or is covered by a fixed delegation rule. A deterministic sanitizer removes instruction-like regions its fixed rules match before the model reads them and retains the removed spans as evidence, and a local judge may restore a refused call, never one whose target lies in the removed text. Across three backbones, and seven task suites on one of them, the standard injection template (AgentDojo's important_instructions) never redirects an action to an attacker's target under SPILLWAY. Across 12 AgentDojo cells, SPILLWAY leads tested defenses in mean under-attack utility and composite scores. A forged chat template also produces a raw zero, mean attack success under a released reinforcement-learning attacker stays below one percent, and SPILLWAY reaches this floor with no defense-triggered backbone re-run and almost no added model call. As personal AI agents act on users' email, files, and payments, SPILLWAY gives them an auditable refusal floor on where their actions go that keeps them useful and holds across backbones.

### Paper [496]
Title: How to "train" a surgeon? Benchmarking generalist agents in surgical scene understanding
URL: https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.11437
Authors: Gengyuan Zhang, Xiao Han, Shijie Zhou, Abdullah Koukash, Jingpei Wu, Jindong Gu, Yuqicheng Zhu, Nassir Navab, Volker Tresp
Abstract: Evaluating general-purpose multimodal large language models (MLLMs) for surgical scene understanding remains limited due to scarce surgical data and fragmented evaluation protocols. We address these challenges through a two-stage evaluation framework for surgical scene understanding. In Stage I, we introduce SurgKnowBench, a diagnostic benchmark that harmonizes public surgical datasets across procedures and tasks and evaluates proprietary and open-source MLLMs to map the boundary of pretrained surgical competence. SurgKnowBench reveals that current models retain partial visual priors but remain weak on relational and procedural understanding and workflow assessment. Building on this failure frontier and motivated by the scaffolded nature of surgical training, we introduce SurgAgentGym, an agentic environment where general-purpose MLLM agents can use bounded knowledge search over SurgWiki and general-purpose visual grounding for surgical tasks. SurgAgentGym evaluates whether generalist agents can improve surgical understanding at inference time through tool-mediated evidence use, without additional surgical training. Our results show that agentic inference narrows the gap mainly for perception-heavy tasks, while fine-grained localization and expert-style assessment remain persistent bottlenecks. This opens a path toward future studies of agentic surgical understanding.

### Paper [497]
Title: Attention at Absolute Zero
URL: https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.11937
Authors: Mostafa 'Neo' Mohsenvand
Abstract: Attention scores candidate supports of an answer, which we call witnesses, and reads the scores at a temperature. We build a higher-order attention layer whose state lives on pairs of items: for a pair (a, b), each third item k is a witness judged through its legs (a, k) and (k, b). It reads witnesses at zero temperature, keeping the best and the number tied with it, passes on only bits comparing such counts, rebuilds its pair state from the input and these bits at every loop, and is trained from answers alone. Each choice removes one way soft attention fails at size: dispersed weight, unresolved gaps between learned scores, and a drifting soft state. In maximum retrieval it is right on at least 99.95% of sets 1,000 times larger than in training, on all ten seeds; trained with a soft count, the same network falls to 42% at 4,096 items. On CLRS-30 without hints, a general form of the layer (half its channels read at zero temperature, numbers embedded, state carried) beats the best published model, which uses hints on some tasks; the full cold layer is exact on every test input of 9 of the 12 tasks whose answers depend on numbers only through comparisons. From yes-or-no answers it decides 2-SAT and two-colouring exactly at 500 variables and, as our theory predicts, not parity equations. Beside a frozen 7B language model it chains family relations exactly at 1,000 statements after training on 16, where the model alone falls to 44%; finite checks on the weights prove every block that learned correct at every size, on acyclic contexts and given what the model reads. On CLUTRR it is right on every story of five to ten relations given the graphs, learning the 62 rules exactly; from text, with the model frozen, no hand-written rule and only a small read-in trained, it lifts an open 7B model from 33% to 65%, above every system we found without hand-written rules except frontier reasoning models.

### Paper [498]
Title: Say Shibboleth: Fingerprinting the LLMs and Harnesses Behind Agentic Attacks
URL: https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.12386
Authors: Dario Pasquini,  Reworr, Giuseppe Ateniese, Manish Bhatt, Sergej Epp
Abstract: We study the fingerprinting of agentic cyberattacks. Our objective is to reliably infer information about the attacker's configuration, such as LLM version and harness, from telemetry. To achieve this, we introduce a technique we call shibboleth traps: modular honeypot services designed to elicit discriminative behaviors from agentic attackers. The traps make informative interactions a condition for obtaining resources the agent seeks, eliciting model declarations and characteristic writing without requiring a successful prompt injection.
  To achieve harness fingerprinting, we introduce general mechanisms to indirectly probe peculiar execution behaviors of harnesses. These probes reveal how a harness masks secrets in tool output, enforces command time limits, and schedules tool calls, using only the effects visible in subsequent requests.
  We show that combined declarative and behavioral inference distinguishes among 15 candidate models with an average 90% top-1 accuracy, with behavioral classifiers evaluated on held-out harnesses or task families. In a separate experiment across four harnesses, fixed probe rules identify the correct harness with 92% accuracy. When instructed to conceal their identity, agents still leave useful behavioral evidence even as their declarations become uninformative.

### Paper [499]
Title: 2D Gum-Net: Unsupervised Dense Elastic Image Registration via Symmetric Correlation and Spectral Pooling
URL: https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.12805
Authors: Aarya Bhave, Anushka Ajay Bhave, Akanksha Narula, Marius Sebastian Shokoui, Alina Hafner
Abstract: Dense non-rigid registration, the recovery of a pixel-wise deformation field that warps one image into geometric agreement with another, is fundamental across medical imaging, structural biology, and correspondence estimation, yet learning-based approaches face a severe bottleneck: ground-truth deformation fields are notoriously difficult to obtain for real data. We present 2D Gum-Net, an end-to-end architecture that predicts dense elastic deformation fields fully unsupervised. We transfer the Geometric Unsupervised Matching Network (Gum-Net), originally developed for 3D subtomogram alignment in cryo-electron tomography, to 2D elastic registration: all three modules are redesigned in 2D, and the rigid transformation head is replaced by a Dense Spatial Transformer that regresses a sparse control-point grid, lifted to a full-resolution field $\Phi$ by bicubic interpolation. Downsampling uses DCT spectral pooling, implemented as frozen-basis matrix multiplications ($\approx 50\times$ GPU speedup), preserving the fine, quasi-periodic structure that max and average pooling destroy. We evaluate on contact-based fingerprints, a challenging testbed for this task: locally self-similar ridge patterns make correspondence maximally ambiguous, skin pressed against a sensor generates authentic large-displacement elastic distortion, and minutiae act as precise landmarks through which an independent matcher externally verifies the topological correctness of predicted fields. Trained purely on synthetic impressions and evaluated zero-shot on real benchmarks, our method improves structural correlation by up to 15.7% on FVC2004 and raises genuine match scores under an independent matcher while leaving imposter scores essentially unchanged. This suggests that the predicted deformations restore true structure without fabricating correspondences.

### Paper [500]
Title: SynthDCB: World Distillation for Scalable Device Control Benchmarking
URL: https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.18639
Authors: Zichen Zhu, Dingye Liu, Nan Jiang, Danyang Zhang, Hang Zheng, Shijie Zheng, Situo Zhang, Kunyao Lan, Yingzi Ma, Sheng Jiang, Lu Chen, Kai Yu
Abstract: Scaling device-control evaluation requires scaling both interactive worlds and the tasks agents must solve. We introduce SynthDCB, a benchmark for environment awareness and device control built through world distillation (WD). World distillation transforms knowledge of device behaviors, constraints, and environmental effects into modular executable components. A unified capability representation connects tool discovery, world execution, and task synthesis. SynthDCB instantiates Home, Car, and Phone with 114 device classes, 1,166 functions, and 95 events, supporting five task families with 32 subtypes. Explicit states and tool-call traces support task validation and agent evaluation. We evaluate 22 models on 2,560 test tasks; the best achieves 88.8% mean Pass@1. Construction experiments demonstrate token efficiency and expansion of task coverage. At the richest knowledge level, WD fully covers 22/23 properties, 19/29 functionalities, and 30/39 event requirements specified in a real RobotVacuum protocol. Furthermore, the 1,000 sampled tasks semantically cover 81.6% of the 854 reference instructions in the real-world corpus. SynthDCB connects an expanding space of real-to-sim executable capabilities to verifiable agent evaluation.

### Paper [501]
Title: Perceptual Weight Space Programming for Diffusion Super-Resolution
URL: https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.18690
Authors: Chenzhi Zheng, Zhuoran Zheng
Abstract: Diffusion models for image super-resolution can recover scene content while producing distracting artifacts or unbalanced colors. We study whether a large language model (LLM) can improve perceptual quality by editing a small subset of existing U-Net weights without a clean reference image. The proposed framework provides the editor with architecture code, selected weights, activation summaries, and sampling previews. It permits up to 1,020 candidate trials while limiting accepted changes to three layers and fewer than 0.1\% of model parameters. The evaluation protocol uses ResShift, SinSR, and SUPIR and compares GPT-6 Astra with a specified Qwen3-VL model under matched access. Feedback metrics guide editing, while separate quality metrics and blinded human comparisons evaluate the final weights. We also report an executed color calibration experiment using real photographs and two small residual networks. This experiment demonstrates correction of a fixed color shift and reveals that an improving feedback score can accompany worse performance on unseen scenes. A separate lightweight super-resolution experiment isolates modest changes in luminance detail while preserving color. The findings motivate restricted updates and independent evaluation of perceptual weight editing.

### Paper [502]
Title: Synthetic Neurosymbolic Task Generation with Controlled Constraint Complexity
URL: https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.19597
Authors: Mykhailo Buleshnyi, Zsolt Zombori, Michael Benedikt
Abstract: We introduce NeSySy, a synthetic generator for neurosymbolic learning tasks that jointly produces logical constraints and consistent datasets. Unlike existing benchmarks, NeSySy provides fine-grained control over key properties such as constraint structure, tightness, input dependence, and data generation and alignment strategies. In particular, it allows the use of rich constraints over infinite domains, and uses solvers to ensure that the constraints are meaningful and that generated samples satisfy the constraints.
  We design NeSySy to support both learning with declarative constraints and learning from distant supervision. Across a range of constraint families, we provide evidence that its parameters induce systematic and largely independent effects on task difficulty. Using NeSySy, we study constraint-aware learning methods, identifying regimes where explicit constraint enforcement is beneficial and where expressive unconstrained models can instead learn constraint structure directly from data. Our results establish NeSySy as a controlled testbed for analysing the interaction between learning and logical structure.
  NeSySy is built around a pipeline that incorporates solvers. Satisfiability checkers (CNFgen and Z3) generate and certify constraints. Constrained optimizers (RC2 for MaxSAT, Z3 for OMT) compute optimal repairs that guarantee every generated sample is consistent.

### Paper [503]
Title: What Matters for Cross-Modal Alignment
URL: https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.20000
Authors: Yipeng Sun, Jiajun Wang, Siyuan Mei, Yan Xia, Siming Bayer, Vincent Christlein, Andreas Maier
Abstract: The Platonic Representation Hypothesis posits that models trained on different modalities converge toward a shared representation. Related work aligns frozen image and text encoders on natural images without paired data. We test zero-pair alignment on five datasets across four modalities: natural images, environmental audio, chest radiographs, and 3D computed tomography (CT). It succeeds only on COCO and fails on the other four. We analyze the failures and find that alignment must first locate the subspace where the modalities co-vary and then orient the mapping within it. Consistent with previous studies, medical imaging is the hardest case: its shared subspace carries the smallest fraction of the variance. When this fraction is small, alignment needs image-text pairs, and random-matrix theory predicts how many pairs are needed. These findings motivate LightAlign: a few pairs let a ridge regression between two frozen encoders locate and orient the mapping simultaneously, and a classifier fitted only on report text then predicts findings from the mapped images. On 3D CT, LightAlign surpasses models trained end to end on image-report pairs without finding-level labels: with only 200 pairs, LightAlign outperforms CT-CLIP on CT-RATE (0.754 vs. 0.733 AUROC), and with only 100 CT-RATE pairs, it does so on RAD-ChestCT, an out-of-distribution dataset from a different hospital. On CheXpert, it reaches 0.862 mean AUROC with only about 5% as many pairs as models trained on MIMIC-CXR.

### Paper [504]
Title: EDITORS Know Your Style! Editing LoRA Subspaces for Stylistic Attribution and Imitation
URL: https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.20846
Authors: Zixuan Wang, Gregory Kang Ruey Lau, Xiao Tian, Jue Fan, Caroline Chaux, Bryan Kian Hsiang Low
Abstract: Disentangling writing style from semantic content is a fundamental challenge in literary text modeling. Style-content entanglement causes models to rely on semantic content for authorship attribution (AA) tasks and memorize author-specific content for style imitation (SI) tasks. We propose EDITORS (EditingLoRA Subspaces), a diagnose-then-deflate framework that adapts pretrained large language models and prioritizes stylistic features, making it effective for both AA and SI tasks. EDITORS first trains a diagnostic LoRA adapter using style-neutral statements derived from the training corpus to identify a content-oriented subspace. It then trains the final style adapter under activation-space deflation that projects input activations away from the identified content-oriented directions. We empirically demonstrate that EDITORS is effective. On three AA benchmarks spanning literature, social media, and news, EDITORS achieves state-of-the-art accuracy, outperforming the strongest existing baseline by up to 29%. On SI, EDITORS generates stylistically faithful and diverse passages, outperforming baselines in achieving style similarity while adhering to content specifications.

### Paper [505]
Title: Scientific Coding with Large Language Models: Evidence from Finite Element Analysis
URL: https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.21176
Authors: Erfan Hamdi, Joel Shor, Emma Lejeune
Abstract: Large language models (LLMs) offer a potential route to automating scientific programming, but their usefulness depends on consistently producing numerically correct code. We extend FEM-Bench to 17 finite element tasks involving nonlinear and history-dependent mechanics and structural dynamics, and evaluate 15 models on implementation and test generation without execution feedback. The best model succeeds in 87.1% of single-pass implementation trials, but rankings differ between tracks. The leading implementer achieves 49.9% joint test success, while the leading tester reaches 82.6%. For six models, we compare single-pass generation with two agentic harnesses that allow code execution and revision. Agentic execution solves some tasks that never succeeded in single-pass trials and improves consistency on others, but gains vary across models and harnesses, and regressions also occur. Claude Opus 5 with SimpleAgent reaches 96.5% Pass@3, the probability of at least one success in three attempts, but 77.1% Pass$^3$, the probability that all three succeed. No model-harness combination exceeds 78.8% Pass$^3$. To understand why agents submit incorrect code after passing their own checks, we analyze 249 failed agentic trajectories and identify six categories of self-verification failure. The most common are inadequate test coverage (73.1%) and reliance on generic consistency checks (70.3%), and in 28.5% of cases the tests share an error with the implementation. Motivated by these findings, an exploratory configuration with separate coder and tester agents achieves the highest observed scores, with 97.6% Pass@3 and 88.8% Pass$^3$. These results identify consistency across attempts and independent verification as the main obstacles to trusting LLM-generated scientific code.

### Paper [506]
Title: ScenarioForge: Resource-Grounded Generation and Execution Validation of Cyber Exercises
URL: https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.21579
Authors: Jaime C. Acosta, Anantaa Kotal, Aritran Piplai
Abstract: Cyber-range and capture-the-flag scenario construction remains labor-intensive because authors must coordinate infrastructure, vulnerable services, attack paths, artifacts, and success conditions. LLMs can propose detailed exercises, but plausible descriptions do not guarantee deployable software or verifiable objectives. We present ScenarioForge, which turns high-level requirements into resource-grounded topologies, services, traffic, segmentation, and challenge chains, then instantiates and validates them in the Common Open Research Emulator (CORE). The ScenarioForge Execution Dataset contains 226 materialized scenarios covering 294 Vulhub images and all 85 custom-built challenge-resource generators developed with our AI-assisted scaffolding. All 226 passed construction, preview, sequencing, deployment, strict runtime validation, and live artifact checks; their 867 steps, each an individually verifiable challenge objective, comprise 457 vulnerability-backed and 410 generated-resource-backed instances. In a separate 50-prompt matched benchmark, ScenarioForge+Qwen3.8 and ScenarioForge+GPT5.6 each achieved 50/50 (100%) topology/build success and 160/160 intent fields; direct frontier generation reached 41/50 builds and 147/160 fields only with the full cumulative scaffold: structure, vocabulary, policy, and container-image guidance. The test still omitted ScenarioForge's downstream challenge-resource generation, dependency/flag sequencing, deployment, and live validation. The artifacts support operationally rich, reproducible exercises for training and evaluation.

### Paper [507]
Title: Revisiting the Survival of Agent-Attributed Code: How Measurement Choices Decide the Verdict
URL: https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.21784
Authors: Musfiqur Rahman, Mahi Begoug, Emad Shihab
Abstract: Studies of how long code from AI coding agents survives after it is merged reach conflicting verdicts: agent code is reported to be retained longer than human code, about as long, or modified sooner. Each study makes defensible measurement choices, but how much these choices decide the verdict is unknown. We revisit the survival of agent-attributed code in our earlier study: 854,139 lines that 2,802 merged pull requests (PRs) added to 215 repositories of the AIDev dataset. We vary nine measurement choices in five research questions and build a specification curve of 88 survival analyses, tested under three variance estimators, several observation windows, and permuted labels. For code from human-supervised agents, mostly GitHub Copilot, the choices change the size of the effect but not its direction. For code from the fully-autonomous agent Devin, they decide the verdict. Across repositories, 15 of 22 specifications report it modified sooner when lines are treated as independent, but only 3 when standard errors are clustered by repository, no more than permuted labels produce. Within repositories, no specification detects a difference. LLM intent classifiers agree with human labels about as well as two humans agree with each other, whereas keyword rules do not (kappa = 0.69 to 0.72 against 0.38), and the two reach different verdicts on why agent code is modified. Humans make most modifications to agent code under every unit and window. Re-run on our data, five published designs each change their result with a single choice. Whether agent code survives longer than human code is therefore decided as much by the measurement as by the code. We derive an operationalization checklist for post-merge studies.

### Paper [508]
Title: Hierarchical Control of Antiphase Synchronization in Biomorphic Spiking Neural Architectures for Locomotor Control
URL: https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.22639
Authors: Artem Guba, Nikolai Kovalev, Alexey Zharinov, Vyacheslav Demin, Viktor B. Kazantsev
Abstract: Central pattern generators (CPGs) provide a biologically inspired foundation for adaptive locomotor control, yet systematically characterizing their dynamic regimes in spiking neural networks remains challenging. This study introduces a framework for identifying and stabilizing antiphase synchronization in a biomorphic spiking CPG model embedded in a closed neuromuscular loop. A composite synchronization metric is developed, integrating phase coherence, amplitude balance, and temporal stability. Its performance is evaluated across systematically varied stimulation waveforms and synaptic configurations. Statistical analysis reveals that stimulation type dominates regime determination, with tonic drive yielding the most robust antiphase synchronization. Periodic inputs induce nonlinear saturation and context-dependent network routing. Although synaptic weights alone show limited discriminative power, they significantly modulate robustness within a given stimulation context, with reciprocal Ia interneuron connections emerging as key stabilizers. The proposed composite metric achieves robust antiphase detection, outperforming traditional phase-only measures while preserving biophysical interpretability. These findings establish a statistically validated, hierarchical modeling approach in which external drive defines the operating regime and targeted synaptic configurations fine-tune dynamic stability, providing actionable guidelines for closed-loop neuromechanical controllers and adaptive neuroprosthetic interfaces.

### Paper [509]
Title: Where Global Context and Weight Sharing Work in Compact Vision Backbones
URL: https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.23446
Authors: Michael Schroeder
Abstract: Compact convolutional backbones such as EfficientNet achieve strong parameter efficiency through local depthwise separable convolutions, yet two structural questions remain central to hybrid design: where does global self-attention improve recognition over pure convolution, and can repeated blocks share weights without losing representational capacity? We investigate both questions on ImageNet-1k (50,000-image validation) using controlled EfficientNet-B0 experiments. First, we show that wrapping a classification backbone in multi-scale feature pyramids (BiFPN) fails to outperform a plain MBConv trunk (69.99% - 71.81% vs. 72.86% plain B0) until paired with stage attention (73.15%). In contrast, keeping the MBConv trunk intact and inserting lightweight zero-gated residual self-attention adapters at stages P3 (28x28 windowed), P4 (14x14 global), and P5 (7x7 global) reaches 74.02% standalone and outperforms a bit-for-bit matched plain-B0 control by +0.954 percentage points (73.88% vs. 72.92%) across an 88-epoch stateless replay schedule, with learned attention amplitude rising monotonically with semantic depth (27.2% at P3 -> 45.4% at P4 -> 66.3% at P5). Second, we evaluate recurrent weight sharing across repeated MBConv blocks with pass-specific BatchNorm statistics (+1,536 parameters per pass). While post-hoc kernel averaging degrades accuracy to 17.03% and single-template stage tying (-27.6% parameters) plateaus at 66.10%, alternating two shared block templates ([B1, A, B, A]) in Stage 6 cuts total model parameters by 10.9% (582K weights) and recovers by +10.82 points in a single epoch to 70.92% (-1.84 points vs. unshared control).

### Paper [510]
Title: Weight Space Programming for Vision Language Model Repair
URL: https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.23615
Authors: Avinash Goutham Aluguvelly
Abstract: In weight-space programming, a language model edits a network's weights directly, without gradients. We ask what a successful repair of this kind relies on. We plant faults in the weights of two vision-language models, SmolVLM-256M and LFM2-VL-450M, and check each edit made by a coding agent against the original weights. Without being told where to look, the agent finds rescaled projector rows and noise in the vision encoder, infers the unknown scale factors, and returns most or all of the rescaled weights to their exact original values and accuracy to near its original level. These repairs depend on a trace of how the faults were computed. The original weights have 16-bit precision, and faults computed in 32-bit arithmetic leave low bits that only the damaged weights carry, a cue that a real failure need not leave. When we round those bits away, each model answers our test questions as before, but the agent, unaided, no longer repairs either model: on LFM2-VL accuracy recovers by about 4 points, against 17 from the unrounded weights. Marking the damaged weights again in their low bits brings the edits back to them. Rounded weights still hold statistical evidence of the rescaling. On SmolVLM the agent uses it once a sentence in the prompt, which we wrote knowing the fault, names such a statistic; on LFM2-VL, whose scale factors are larger, most sessions find the rescaled rows unaided, but none finds the noise. Swapped rows or columns, which keep the set of stored values, are not found unaided on either model within our budgets. We recommend that studies of weight repair report the number format of the weights and include a rounded control.

### Paper [511]
Title: ReflectScene:MLLM-Guided Closed-Loop Diffusion for 3D Indoor Scene Generation
URL: https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.23845
Authors: Jiawei Shen, Jie Feng, Junpeng Zhang, Guanbin Li, Wanyi Chen, Weisheng Dong, Guangming Shi
Abstract: Text-conditioned 3D indoor scene generation requires layouts satisfying geometric, functional, and semantic constraints. However, existing diffusion methods rely primarily on static textual conditions, limiting their ability to assess evolving scenes and translate semantic discrepancies into executable object-level corrections. MLLM-based post-hoc refinement provides semantic feedback but remains decoupled from denoising, potentially disrupting scene coherence. We propose ReflectScene, an MLLM-guided closed-loop diffusion framework. First, we introduce Hierarchical Prior-Grounded Scene Understanding that aligns structured layouts with multi-view visual evidence, to interpret object-specific context and scene global semantics via a frozen multi-modal large language model (MLLM). Second, we propose MLLM-Guided Semantic Reward Evaluation to distill MLLM's diagnoses and assessments on possible candidate corrections into a lightweight Reward Predictor, enabling correction decision making with reduced computation burden. Finally, Decision-Conditioned Trajectory-Consistent Diffusion is proposed to integrate selected corrections through transient conditioning and persistent clean-estimate projection, correcting object layout in the generated scene while preserving non-target arrangements throughout denoising. Experiments on 3D-FRONT demonstrate improved functional consistency and semantic alignment while maintaining competitive generation quality and diversity.
